MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1827
During a dissection of the upper limb, the brachial fascia is incised and the muscles lying posterior to the humerus are reflected as a single group. The muscles in this space share one functional role at the elbow. Which of the following best describes the muscle group found in this compartment?
A. Rotator muscles
B. Flexor muscles
C. Abductor muscles
D. Extensor muscles
E. Adductor muscles
Answer: D. Extensor muscles
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Regions and fascial compartments of the upper limb
Why this is correct:
The lecture divides both the arm and the forearm into an anterior compartment holding flexor muscles and a posterior compartment holding extensor muscles, so muscles posterior to the humerus are extensors.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture doesn't describe a rotator compartment in the arm.
B. Flexors occupy the anterior compartment of the arm, not the posterior one.
C. The lecture doesn't describe an abductor compartment in the arm.
E. The lecture doesn't describe an adductor compartment in the arm.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1828
A surgeon planning an incision over the anterior superior thoracic wall notes that the layer she will open first contains skin, superficial fascia, and glandular tissue, and that the muscles she needs lie beneath it. Which of the following structures is most likely contained in the layer she opens first?
A. Pectoralis minor
B. The breast
C. Pectoralis major
D. Subclavius
E. Serratus anterior
Answer: B. The breast
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Regions and fascial compartments of the upper limb; Breast: composition, location, and the axillary tail of Spence
Why this is correct:
The lecture splits the pectoral region into a superficial compartment containing skin, superficial fascia, and the breasts, and a deep compartment containing the muscles and their associated structures.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pectoralis minor is a muscle of the deep compartment, lying deep to pectoralis major.
C. Pectoralis major is a muscle and belongs to the deep compartment.
D. Subclavius is a muscle and belongs to the deep compartment.
E. Serratus anterior is a muscle and belongs to the deep compartment.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1829
An anatomy instructor describes a loosely defined region as the external aspect of the anterior superior thoracic wall, and explains that it helps hold the upper limb onto the trunk. Which of the following best describes the role this region plays?
A. It transmits the weight of the trunk to the pelvis
B. It forms the floor of the thoracic cavity
C. It encloses the flexor muscles of the forearm
D. It anchors the upper limb to the trunk
E. It separates the thoracic and abdominal cavities
Answer: D. It anchors the upper limb to the trunk
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Regions and fascial compartments of the upper limb
Why this is correct:
The lecture defines the pectoral region as the external aspect of the anterior superior thoracic wall and states that it helps anchor the upper limb to the trunk.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture doesn't give the pectoral region any weight-bearing role toward the pelvis.
B. The lecture doesn't describe the pectoral region as a floor of the thoracic cavity.
C. The forearm flexors sit in the anterior compartment of the forearm, well distal to this region.
E. The lecture doesn't assign the pectoral region a role separating body cavities.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1830
During a dissection of the anterior thoracic wall, the most superior part of the sternum is cleaned and found to articulate with both clavicles and with the costal cartilages of the first pair of ribs. Which of the following parts of the sternum is most likely being described?
A. Body
B. Xiphoid process
C. Sternal angle
D. Manubrium
E. Jugular notch
Answer: D. Manubrium
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Sternum and the sternal angle of Louis
Why this is correct:
The manubrium is the most superior of the three parts of the sternum, and the lecture states that it articulates with the clavicle and with the costal cartilages of the first pair of ribs.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The body is the middle portion and articulates with the costal cartilages of ribs 2 through 7.
B. The xiphoid process is the smaller inferior portion of the sternum.
C. The sternal angle is the projection formed where the manubrium and body fuse, not a part that articulates with the clavicle.
E. The jugular notch is a palpable indentation on the superior border of the manubrium, not a separate part.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1831
A 46-year-old man comes to the clinic for evaluation of chest pain. The physician palpates a transverse ridge on the anterior chest wall and uses it to begin counting ribs, because the second pair of ribs articulates at that ridge. Which of the following vertebral levels does this landmark most likely correspond to?
A. The T8/T9 intervertebral disc
B. The T10/T11 intervertebral disc
C. The T2/T3 intervertebral disc
D. The T4/T5 intervertebral disc
E. The T6/T7 intervertebral disc
Answer: D. The T4/T5 intervertebral disc
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternum and the sternal angle of Louis
Why this is correct:
The ridge described is the sternal angle of Louis, formed by the fusion of the manubrium and body. The lecture places it at the level of the T4/T5 intervertebral disc and notes that the second pair of ribs articulates there, which is what makes it useful for counting ribs.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. T8/T9 is well below the sternal angle.
B. T10/T11 is near the inferior thorax, far below this landmark.
C. The lecture doesn't place the sternal angle at T2/T3.
E. T6/T7 sits below the level given in the lecture.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1832
A radiologist reviewing a chest CT uses the junction between the manubrium and the body of the sternum as a reference plane, because several internal structures are found at that same level. Which of the following structures is most likely found at this level?
A. The origin of the brachiocephalic trunk
B. The opening of the thoracic duct into the venous system
C. The bifurcation of the trachea
D. The apex of the left ventricle
E. The termination of the azygos vein
Answer: C. The bifurcation of the trachea
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternum and the sternal angle of Louis
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists the sternal angle as an important landmark because several internal landmarks sit at that level, and it names the arch of the aorta and the bifurcation of the trachea as examples.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture doesn't place the origin of the brachiocephalic trunk at the sternal angle.
B. The lecture doesn't place the thoracic duct's venous opening at this level.
D. The lecture doesn't place the apex of the left ventricle at this level.
E. The lecture doesn't place the termination of the azygos vein at this level.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1833
During a dissection of the thoracic wall, a student traces the upper seven pairs of ribs and finds that each one reaches the sternum through its own costal cartilage without joining the cartilage above it. Which of the following terms is most likely used for these ribs?
A. Atypical ribs
B. Floating ribs
C. False ribs
D. Cervical ribs
E. True ribs
Answer: E. True ribs
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Ribs of the pectoral region
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that ribs 1 through 7 attach directly to the sternum and calls them true ribs.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture doesn't use the term atypical for the first seven ribs.
B. The lecture doesn't describe the first seven ribs as floating.
C. The lecture reserves the direct attachment described here for the first seven ribs only.
D. A cervical rib isn't part of the 12 pairs the lecture describes.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1834
A 61-year-old woman is evaluated after blunt chest trauma. The physician notes that the ribs connect to the sternum through a flexible material rather than directly by bone, which allows the chest wall to deform during the impact. Which of the following best describes this connecting material?
A. Synovial membrane
B. Dense intermuscular septum
C. Fibrous periosteum
D. Elastic ligament
E. Costal cartilage
Answer: E. Costal cartilage
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Ribs of the pectoral region; Sternum and the sternal angle of Louis
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the ribs are connected to the sternum by flexible costal cartilages, and that the body of the sternum articulates with the costal cartilages of ribs 2 through 7.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture doesn't describe a synovial membrane connecting ribs to the sternum.
B. Intermuscular septa divide limb compartments and aren't part of the rib to sternum connection.
C. Periosteum covers bone surfaces and isn't the structure the lecture names here.
D. The lecture names cartilage rather than an elastic ligament.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1835
A surgeon is counseling a patient before a chest wall procedure and explains that the bones she will work around serve the pectoral muscles as well as protecting the thorax. Which of the following roles do the ribs most directly serve in the pectoral region?
A. They transmit force from the upper limb to the axial skeleton
B. They form the apex of the axilla together with the scapula
C. They suspend the mammary glands from the overlying dermis
D. They provide attachment sites for muscles of the pectoral region
E. They articulate with the acromion of the scapula
Answer: D. They provide attachment sites for muscles of the pectoral region
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Ribs of the pectoral region; Pectoralis minor
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the ribs provide an attachment site for the muscles of the pectoral region, which is consistent with pectoralis minor arising from ribs 3 through 5 and serratus anterior from ribs 1 through 8.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Transmitting force from the limb to the axial skeleton is a function the lecture gives to the clavicle.
B. The apex of the axilla is formed by the 1st rib with the clavicle and the superior border of the scapula, which is narrower than the role described here.
C. The suspensory ligaments of Cooper attach the mammary glands to the dermis.
E. The clavicle, not a rib, articulates with the acromion at the acromioclavicular joint.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1836
During a dissection of the shoulder girdle, a bone is traced from the manubrium of the sternum laterally to the acromion of the scapula, and it can be followed by palpation along its whole length. Which of the following joints does this bone form with the axial skeleton?
A. Acromioclavicular joint
B. Glenohumeral joint
C. Costochondral joint
D. Scapulothoracic joint
E. Sternoclavicular joint
Answer: E. Sternoclavicular joint
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Clavicle: articulations and functions
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the clavicle attaches the upper limb to the axial skeleton at the sternoclavicular joint, where its medial end meets the manubrium.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The acromioclavicular joint is where the clavicle meets the scapula, which is part of the limb rather than the axial skeleton.
B. The glenohumeral joint is between the scapula and the head of the humerus.
C. A costochondral joint lies between a rib and its costal cartilage and doesn't involve the clavicle.
D. The lecture doesn't describe a scapulothoracic joint as an articulation of the clavicle.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1837
A 22-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after falling onto his shoulder. Radiographs show a displaced fracture of the collarbone, and the surgical team is concerned about the structures lying immediately deep to the fractured bone. Which of the following functions of this bone most directly explains their concern?
A. It serves as the origin of the sternocostal head of pectoralis major
B. It provides the attachment for the suspensory ligaments of the breast
C. It stabilizes the scapula against the thoracic wall during protraction
D. It protects the neurovascular structures supplying the upper limb
E. It forms the lateral wall of the axilla at the intertubercular groove
Answer: D. It protects the neurovascular structures supplying the upper limb
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Clavicle: articulations and functions; Subclavius
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists protection of the underlying neurovascular structures supplying the upper limb as one of the clavicle's functions, which is why a displaced clavicular fracture raises concern for the vessels and nerves beneath it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The sternocostal head of pectoralis major arises from the sternum and the upper six costal cartilages.
B. The suspensory ligaments of Cooper attach the mammary glands to the dermis of the skin.
C. Holding the scapula against the thoracic wall is an action of serratus anterior, not a function of the clavicle.
E. The lateral wall of the axilla is the narrow wall at the intertubercular groove of the humerus.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1838
A biomechanics lecture describes how a load applied to the hand is carried through the upper limb and is finally delivered to the trunk through a single strut of bone. Which of the following bones most directly transmits that force to the axial skeleton?
A. Manubrium
B. First rib
C. Humerus
D. Scapula
E. Clavicle
Answer: E. Clavicle
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Clavicle: articulations and functions
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists transmitting force from the upper limb to the axial skeleton as one of the three functions of the clavicle, along with attaching the limb to the trunk and protecting neurovascular structures.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The manubrium is part of the axial skeleton receiving the force, not the bone transmitting it.
B. The lecture doesn't give the first rib a force transmission role from the limb.
C. The humerus carries load within the limb but reaches the axial skeleton only through the scapula and clavicle.
D. The scapula is described as a stable platform for muscles rather than the strut carrying force to the trunk.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1839
During a dissection of the shoulder, a hook-like bony process is cleaned on the anterior superolateral edge of a flat triangular bone, and several muscles and ligaments are found attaching to it. Which of the following structures is most likely being described?
A. Lesser tubercle
B. Spine of the scapula
C. Acromion
D. Coracoid process
E. Glenoid fossa
Answer: D. Coracoid process
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Scapula: coracoid process, acromion, and glenoid fossa
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the coracoid process (Latin for crow's beak) as a hook-like process on the anterior superolateral edge of the scapula that serves as a site of muscle and ligament attachment.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lesser tubercle is a prominence on the humerus, not on the scapula.
B. The spine is a ridge on the posterior scapula from which the acromion extends.
C. The acromion is the most lateral portion of the scapula extending anteriorly from the scapular spine.
E. The glenoid fossa is a shallow cavity on the lateral border, not a hook-like process.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1840
A radiologist examining a shoulder film notes that the cavity receiving the head of the humerus is strikingly shallow compared with the depth of the hip socket. Which of the following features of the scapula is most likely being described?
A. Subscapular fossa
B. Glenoid fossa
C. Coracoid process
D. Acromion
E. Suprascapular notch
Answer: B. Glenoid fossa
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Scapula: coracoid process, acromion, and glenoid fossa
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the glenoid fossa as a shallow cavity on the lateral border of the scapula that articulates with the head of the humerus to form the glenohumeral joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The subscapular fossa is on the anterior surface of the scapula and gives origin to subscapularis.
C. The coracoid process is a hook-like projection for muscle and ligament attachment.
D. The acromion is the lateral extension of the scapular spine and articulates with the clavicle.
E. The suprascapular notch is an indentation on the superior border and doesn't receive the humeral head.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

B. Acromioclavicular joint

E. Scapulocostal joint
Answer: B. Acromioclavicular joint

The structure followed from the scapular spine is the acromion, which the lecture describes as the most lateral part of the scapula and as the site of the acromioclavicular joint with the clavicle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The glenohumeral joint is between the glenoid fossa and the head of the humerus.
C. The lecture doesn't describe a costoclavicular joint in this region.
D. The sternoclavicular joint is at the medial end of the clavicle with the manubrium.

The lecture describes the greater tubercle as a prominence on the superior posterior portion of the humerus.

A. The intertubercular groove is the groove between the two tubercles, not a prominence.
B. The lesser tubercle is the prominence on the superior anterior portion of the humerus.

E. The coracoid process is a feature of the scapula.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1844
During a dissection of the shoulder, a tendon is followed from the pectoral region and found to insert into a groove on the proximal humerus that lies between two bony prominences. Which of the following structures does this groove lie between?
A. The greater and lesser tubercles
B. The acromion and the coracoid process
C. The deltoid tuberosity and the surgical neck
D. The glenoid fossa and the head
E. The head and the anatomical neck
Answer: A. The greater and lesser tubercles
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Proximal humerus: tubercles and the intertubercular groove; Pectoralis major

The lecture defines the intertubercular groove of the humerus as the groove between the greater and lesser tubercles, and pectoralis major inserts on its lateral lip.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The acromion and coracoid process are both scapular features.
C. The deltoid tuberosity and surgical neck are on the humerus but don't form this groove.
D. The glenoid fossa is scapular and articulates with the humeral head.
E. The head and anatomical neck are proximal to the tubercles and don't bound this groove.
Objective: Identify the bones and their major features associated with the pectoral region, including the ribs, sternum, clavicle, scapula, and proximal humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1845
A histology specimen taken from the breast of a lactating woman shows glands that the pathologist describes as modified versions of another skin gland type. Which of the following best describes the mammary glands?
A. Modified sebaceous glands
B. Modified eccrine sweat glands
C. Modified apocrine sweat glands
D. Modified ceruminous glands
E. Modified mucous glands
Answer: C. Modified apocrine sweat glands
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Breast: composition, location, and the axillary tail of Spence
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes mammary glands as modified apocrine sweat glands consisting of a series of ducts and secretory lobules that produce and secrete milk immediately after pregnancy.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture places sebaceous glands in the areola, where they lubricate the nipple, rather than calling the mammary gland a modified sebaceous gland.
B. The lecture specifies apocrine rather than eccrine sweat glands.
D. The lecture doesn't describe mammary glands as modified ceruminous glands.
E. The lecture doesn't describe mammary glands as modified mucous glands.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1846
A 53-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a lump near her armpit. Examination shows the mass lies in a tongue of glandular tissue that extends from the breast along the inferior border of pectoralis major and into the axilla. Which of the following best identifies the tissue containing the mass?
A. Suspensory ligaments of Cooper
B. Axillary tail of Spence
C. Lactiferous sinus
D. Subareolar lymphatic plexus
E. Retromammary space
Answer: B. Axillary tail of Spence
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Breast: composition, location, and the axillary tail of Spence
Why this is correct:
The lecture defines the axillary tail of Spence as mammary gland tissue that extends along the inferior border of pectoralis major into the axilla, which matches the location of this mass.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The suspensory ligaments of Cooper are dense connective tissue bands attaching the glands to the dermis.
C. A lactiferous sinus is a dilation of a duct just proximal to the nipple.
D. The subareolar lymphatic plexus sits beneath the areola and carries lymph rather than forming a glandular extension.
E. The retromammary space is a layer of loose connective tissue behind the breast, not glandular tissue running into the axilla.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1847

B. From the 2nd to the 6th rib, and from the lateral edge of the sternum to the midaxillary line

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Breast: composition, location, and the axillary tail of Spence

The lecture states that female breasts vary a great deal in size but normally extend from the 2nd to the 6th ribs vertically and from the lateral edge of the sternum to the midaxillary line transversely.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture gives rib levels and a transverse range rather than clavicle to costal margin limits.
C. The lecture doesn't extend the breast to the 10th rib or the scapular line.
D. The lecture doesn't place the upper limit at the 1st rib or the medial limit at the midline.
E. The lecture doesn't extend the breast to the 8th rib or the posterior axillary line.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1848
A plastic surgeon placing a breast implant develops a plane of loose connective tissue that lies behind the breast and in front of the deep fascia covering pectoralis major and serratus anterior. Which of the following best identifies this plane?
A. Deltopectoral groove
B. Subareolar plexus
C. Retromammary space
D. Axillary tail of Spence
E. Clavipectoral interval
Answer: C. Retromammary space
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Retromammary space
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the retromammary space as a layer of loose connective tissue separating the breast from the deep fascia overlying the pectoralis and serratus anterior muscles, and names it as a potential site for placing breast implants.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The deltopectoral groove is the furrow between deltoid and pectoralis major that carries the cephalic vein.
B. The subareolar plexus is a lymphatic network under the areola.
D. The axillary tail of Spence is glandular tissue extending into the axilla, not a plane behind the breast.
E. The lecture doesn't describe a clavipectoral interval as the implant plane.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1849
During a dissection of the anterior chest wall, the breast is lifted away from the chest and separates easily from the underlying muscle fascia along a layer of loose connective tissue. Which of the following structures lies immediately deep to that layer?
A. The axillary lymph nodes
B. The suspensory ligaments of Cooper
C. Deep fascia overlying pectoralis major
D. The lactiferous sinuses
E. The subareolar lymphatic plexus
Answer: C. Deep fascia overlying pectoralis major
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Retromammary space; Breast: composition, location, and the axillary tail of Spence
Why this is correct:
The lecture places the retromammary space between the breast and the deep fascia overlying the pectoralis and serratus anterior muscles, so the fascia over pectoralis major is what lies deep to that loose layer.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The axillary lymph nodes sit in the axilla rather than directly behind the breast.
B. The suspensory ligaments run from the glands to the dermis, superficial to the retromammary space.
D. The lactiferous sinuses are dilations of the ducts just proximal to the nipple, well superficial to this plane.
E. The subareolar lymphatic plexus lies beneath the areola, far superficial to this plane.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1850
On physical examination, a clinician finds that a patient's breast glides freely over the chest wall when it is moved from side to side. Which of the following best explains this mobility?
A. A layer of loose connective tissue separates the breast from the deep fascia
B. The axillary tail extends past the border of pectoralis major
C. The lactiferous ducts converge loosely beneath the nipple
D. The suspensory ligaments are attached only to the dermis of the skin
E. The mammary glands lie deep to the pectoral fascia
Answer: A. A layer of loose connective tissue separates the breast from the deep fascia
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Retromammary space
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the retromammary space, a layer of loose connective tissue between the breast and the deep fascia over the muscles, allows the breast some degree of movement.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The axillary tail describes the extent of glandular tissue, not the mobility of the breast.
C. The arrangement of the lactiferous ducts doesn't determine how the breast moves on the chest wall.
D. The suspensory ligaments anchor the glands to the dermis and tether the breast rather than freeing it.
E. The lecture places the breast within the skin and subcutaneous tissue, superficial to the pectoral fascia.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1851
A histology section through a lactating breast shows roughly 15 to 20 radially arranged glandular units, each made up of small sacs arranged in clusters that resemble bunches of grapes. Which of the following terms applies to these radially arranged units?
A. Lactiferous sinuses
B. Areolar glands
C. Suspensory ligaments
D. Alveoli
E. Lobules
Answer: E. Lobules
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Internal structure of the breast: lobules, ducts, and suspensory ligaments
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that each breast consists of 15 to 20 radially aligned glands called lobules, each of which contains many smaller sacs called alveoli arranged in clusters like bunches of grapes.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lactiferous sinuses are duct dilations just proximal to the nipple.
B. Areolar glands are sebaceous glands of the areola that lubricate the nipple.
C. The suspensory ligaments are connective tissue bands rather than glandular units.
D. The alveoli are the small milk-producing sacs inside each lobule, not the radially arranged units themselves.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1852
During a dissection of the breast, a duct is traced from a glandular lobule toward the nipple and is found to widen noticeably just before it reaches its opening. Which of the following best identifies this widened segment?

B. Subareolar lymphatic plexus
C. Lactiferous sinus
D. Areolar gland
E. Retromammary space
Answer: C. Lactiferous sinus
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Internal structure of the breast: lobules, ducts, and suspensory ligaments
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that secretions from each glandular lobule drain into lactiferous ducts, which dilate into lactiferous sinuses just proximal to their opening in the nipple.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An alveolus is a small milk-producing sac within a lobule, at the opposite end of the duct system.
B. The subareolar lymphatic plexus carries lymph rather than milk.
D. Areolar glands are sebaceous glands in the areola.
E. The retromammary space is a connective tissue plane behind the breast.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1853
A surgeon dissecting the superior aspect of the breast encounters dense connective tissue bands that run from the glandular tissue to the dermis of the overlying skin and separate the glandular lobes from one another. Which of the following structures is most likely being described?
A. Lactiferous ducts
B. Retromammary space
C. Axillary tail of Spence
D. Suspensory ligaments of Cooper
E. Pectoral fascia
Answer: D. Suspensory ligaments of Cooper
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Internal structure of the breast: lobules, ducts, and suspensory ligaments
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the suspensory ligaments of Cooper as dense connective tissue that firmly attaches the mammary glands to the dermis of the overlying skin, separates and supports the glandular lobes and lobules, and is most clearly defined in the superior aspect of the breast.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lactiferous ducts carry secretions toward the nipple rather than anchoring tissue to skin.
B. The retromammary space is loose rather than dense connective tissue and lies behind the breast.
C. The axillary tail is glandular tissue extending into the axilla.
E. The pectoral fascia covers the muscle deep to the breast rather than running to the dermis.
Objective: Identify and discuss the location and structure of the female breast, including its basic blood supply, innervation, and lymphatic drainage.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1854
A woman who is breastfeeding has intact skin over the pigmented circular area of her breast, which remains lubricated during nursing. Which of the following best explains why that area resists chafing?

B. The lactiferous sinuses release milk continuously onto the skin
C. The subareolar lymphatic plexus drains excess fluid from the skin

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Nipple and areola
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the areola as a circular pigmented area containing areolar sebaceous glands that secrete an oily substance to aid in nipple lubrication and prevent nipple chafing during nursing.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture assigns the lubricating secretion to sebaceous glands of the areola, not to apocrine glands of the nipple.
B. The lactiferous sinuses store milk proximal to the nipple openings rather than coating the skin.
C. The subareolar lymphatic plexus carries lymph toward the axillary nodes.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1857
During a mastectomy, bleeding is encountered from perforating vessels entering the medial aspect of the breast near the sternal edge. Which of the following vessels most likely gives rise to these perforating branches?
A. Circumflex scapular artery

C. Internal thoracic artery
D. Thoracodorsal artery
E. Superior thoracic artery
Answer: C. Internal thoracic artery

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Arterial supply and venous drainage of the breast

The lecture states that the arterial supply to the medial aspect of the breast comes mainly from perforating branches of the internal thoracic artery, also called the internal mammary artery, which is a branch of the subclavian artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1863
A 57-year-old woman has a carcinoma in the upper outer quadrant of the left breast. The surgical team plans to sample the nodal group that receives the great majority of lymph from the breast. Which of the following groups of nodes are they most likely to sample?
A. Nodes of the opposite breast
B. Axillary lymph nodes
C. Parasternal lymph nodes
D. Supraclavicular lymph nodes
E. Abdominal lymph nodes
Answer: B. Axillary lymph nodes
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Lymphatic drainage of the breast
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that more than 75 percent of the lymph from the breast, especially from the lateral quadrants, drains to the axillary lymph nodes located near the lateral border of pectoralis major.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The opposite breast is an alternative route for medial quadrant lymph rather than the main one.
C. The parasternal nodes receive lymph from the medial quadrants.
D. The lecture doesn't name supraclavicular nodes as the main drainage of the breast.
E. Abdominal nodes receive lymph that passes deeply from the inferior quadrants.
Objective: Describe the lymphatic drainage of the upper limb to axillary lymph nodes.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1864
A tumor arises in the medial quadrant of the right breast, close to the sternal border. The oncology team is concerned about nodal spread along vessels that run beside the internal thoracic artery. Which of the following nodal groups is most likely involved first?

B. Abdominal lymph nodes
C. Cervical lymph nodes
D. Parasternal lymph nodes
E. Subareolar lymphatic plexus

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Lymphatic drainage of the breast

The lecture states that lymph from the medial quadrants of the breast drains to the parasternal lymph nodes, which sit near the internal thoracic artery, or crosses to the opposite breast.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The axillary nodes take lymph mainly from the lateral quadrants.
B. Abdominal nodes receive lymph passing deeply from the inferior quadrants.
C. The lecture doesn't name cervical nodes as a drainage route for the medial breast.
E. The subareolar plexus is the collecting network beneath the nipple and areola rather than a nodal group.
Objective: Describe the lymphatic drainage of the upper limb to axillary lymph nodes.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1865
After lymph leaves the axillary nodes of the left upper limb, it is carried through a lymphatic trunk and then returns to the venous system. Which of the following routes does that lymph most likely follow on the left side?
A. The parasternal nodes and then the right lymphatic duct
B. The subclavian lymphatic trunk and then the right lymphatic duct
C. The subareolar plexus and then the internal thoracic vein

E. The subclavian lymphatic trunk and then the thoracic duct

Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Lymphatic drainage of the breast

The lecture states that lymph from the axillary lymph nodes is drained by the subclavian lymphatic trunk, and that on the left lymph returns to the venous system by way of the thoracic duct.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The parasternal nodes take lymph from the medial breast and aren't the route from the axillary nodes.
B. The right lymphatic duct returns lymph on the right side, not the left.
C. The subareolar plexus collects lymph before it reaches the nodes rather than after.
D. The lecture doesn't route axillary lymph through abdominal nodes and the cisterna chyli.
Objective: Describe the lymphatic drainage of the upper limb to axillary lymph nodes.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1866
A 49-year-old woman with a newly diagnosed breast carcinoma is scheduled for a procedure that samples the node most likely to contain cancer cells first, so the surgeon can decide whether the disease has spread beyond the primary tumor. Which of the following best describes the node being sampled?
A. The first node or nodes to which cancer cells from the tumor are likely to spread
B. The node closest to the lateral border of pectoralis minor in the axilla
C. The largest palpable node found in the axillary fat at surgery
D. The node that drains lymph from the opposite breast
E. The node lying nearest the internal thoracic artery on the affected side
Answer: A. The first node or nodes to which cancer cells from the tumor are likely to spread
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Sentinel lymph node biopsy and axillary lymph node dissection
Why this is correct:
The lecture defines the sentinel node as the first lymph node or nodes to which cancer cells are likely to spread from a primary tumor, and describes sentinel node biopsy as a way to determine whether cancer has spread into the lymphatic system.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The sentinel node is defined by drainage pattern rather than by a fixed position relative to pectoralis minor.
C. Size at surgery doesn't define the sentinel node.
D. The sentinel node drains the affected breast rather than the opposite one.
E. A node near the internal thoracic artery is a parasternal node, which isn't how the sentinel node is defined.
Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1867
During a breast cancer operation, the surgeon injects material near the tumor before making the axillary incision, then uses a handheld probe and inspects the tissue for staining in order to locate a specific node. Which of the following materials is most likely injected?
A. Local anesthetic combined with epinephrine
B. A sclerosing agent to occlude lymphatics

Why this is correct:
The lecture states that to identify the sentinel lymph node the surgeon injects a radioactive substance, blue dye, or both near the tumor, then uses a probe to find the node containing the radioactive substance or looks for the node stained with dye.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Local anesthetic with epinephrine doesn't identify the sentinel node.

Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1868
A 62-year-old woman develops persistent swelling of her right arm several months after undergoing removal of axillary nodes and adjuvant radiation to the axilla for breast cancer. Which of the following best explains the swelling?
A. Damage to lymphatic channels in the axilla interferes with drainage from the arm
B. Compression of the axillary vein by scar tissue raises venous pressure in the arm
C. Loss of the axillary tail of Spence reduces glandular drainage from the chest wall
D. Division of the long thoracic nerve weakens the muscular pump of the limb
E. Resection of pectoralis minor removes support from the axillary vessels
Answer: A. Damage to lymphatic channels in the axilla interferes with drainage from the arm
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sentinel lymph node biopsy and axillary lymph node dissection
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that surgical or radiation damage to the lymphatic channels in the axilla can interfere with lymphatic drainage from the arm on that side, producing lymphedema, which is swelling due to accumulation of lymph fluid.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The lecture attributes post-treatment arm swelling to lymphatic damage rather than to venous compression.
C. The axillary tail is breast tissue and isn't described as a drainage pathway for the limb.
D. Long thoracic nerve injury produces a winged scapula rather than limb swelling.
E. The lecture doesn't link removal of pectoralis minor to swelling of the arm.
Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1869
A 58-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of a change in the appearance of her left breast. Examination shows a firm mass and an area of skin over it that is pulled inward, producing a visible dimple when she raises her arms. Which of the following most directly produces the dimpling?
A. Fibrosis places traction on the suspensory ligaments
B. Sebaceous glands of the areola become distended and fibrotic
C. Tumor obstructs the lactiferous ducts beneath the nipple
D. The retromammary space is obliterated by tumor invasion

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Signs of breast cancer and their anatomical basis; Internal structure of the breast: lobules, ducts, and suspensory ligaments

The lecture explains that abnormal contours or dimpling result from cancerous invasion of the glandular tissue and from fibrosis that shortens or places traction on the suspensory ligaments, which are anchored to the dermis of the skin.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The lecture doesn't link the areolar sebaceous glands to dimpling.
C. Pressure on the lactiferous ducts produces nipple retraction rather than skin dimpling.
D. The lecture doesn't attribute dimpling to loss of the retromammary space.
E. Blocked lymphatics produce the pitted peau d'orange appearance rather than a focal dimple.
Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

Question 1870
A 63-year-old woman is found on examination to have a breast mass with a nipple that has become drawn inward compared with photographs taken a year earlier. Which of the following best explains this finding?

Question 1871
A 55-year-old woman comes to the clinic because of changes in the skin of her right breast. Examination shows thickened skin with a pitted appearance resembling the peel of an orange. Which of the following most directly explains this appearance?

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1872
A 47-year-old woman undergoes an operation in which all of the breast tissue, including the nipple, the areola, and most of the overlying skin, is removed, while the muscles beneath the breast are left in place and no nodes are taken. Which of the following procedures was most likely performed?
A. Total (simple) mastectomy
B. Radical mastectomy
C. Sentinel lymph node biopsy
D. Axillary lymph node dissection
E. Modified radical mastectomy
Answer: A. Total (simple) mastectomy
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Mastectomy and breast reconstruction
Why this is correct:
The lecture defines a total or simple mastectomy as removal of all breast tissue including the nipple, the areola, and most of the overlying skin, while leaving the pectoral muscles beneath.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. A radical mastectomy removes the axillary nodes and the pectoral muscles as well.
C. Sentinel node biopsy samples a node rather than removing the breast.
D. Axillary dissection removes nodes from the axilla rather than the breast itself.
E. A modified radical mastectomy also removes some axillary lymph nodes, which weren't taken here.
Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1873
During an operation for breast carcinoma, the surgeon removes all breast tissue, the axillary lymph nodes, and the pectoral muscles lying under the breast. Which of the following procedures was most likely performed?
A. Total (simple) mastectomy
B. Lumpectomy with sentinel node sampling
C. Modified radical mastectomy
D. Radical mastectomy
E. Flap reconstruction using latissimus dorsi
Answer: D. Radical mastectomy
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Mastectomy and breast reconstruction; Boundaries of the axilla
Why this is correct:
The lecture defines a radical mastectomy as removal of all breast tissue, the axillary lymph nodes, and the pectoral muscles under the breast, which is the most extensive of the three operations it describes.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A total or simple mastectomy spares the pectoral muscles and takes no nodes.
B. The lecture doesn't describe a lumpectomy as removing the pectoral muscles.
C. A modified radical mastectomy takes some axillary nodes but leaves the pectoral muscles.
E. Flap reconstruction rebuilds the breast rather than removing tissue.
Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1874
A 51-year-old woman who has had a mastectomy elects to have the breast rebuilt using her own tissue rather than a prosthesis. Which of the following muscles is most likely used for this reconstruction?
A. Pectoralis minor
B. Subclavius
C. Subscapularis
D. Latissimus dorsi
E. Serratus anterior
Answer: D. Latissimus dorsi
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Mastectomy and breast reconstruction
Why this is correct:
The lecture names implant reconstruction and tissue (flap) reconstruction as the two main types of breast reconstruction, and gives latissimus dorsi as its example of a flap.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pectoralis minor is a small triangular muscle deep to pectoralis major and isn't the flap the lecture names.
B. Subclavius is a small muscle under the clavicle and isn't named as a flap.
C. Subscapularis forms part of the posterior axillary wall and isn't named as a flap.
E. Serratus anterior forms the medial wall of the axilla and isn't named as a reconstructive flap.
Objective: Discuss the anatomical basis for the following conditions associated with breast cancer: dimpling of skin, nipple retraction, peau d'orange sign, lymphogenous metastasis (pathways for spread), sentinel lymph node biopsy, lymphedema in the upper extremity, and reconstructive options following mastectomy.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1875
During a dissection of the anterior chest wall, a broad fan-shaped muscle is reflected and traced from the medial half of the clavicle, the sternum, and the upper six costal cartilages to a single insertion on the humerus. Which of the following is the most likely distal attachment?
A. Lesser tubercle of the humerus
B. Lateral lip of the intertubercular groove
C. Coracoid process of the scapula
D. Medial lip of the intertubercular groove
E. Deltoid tuberosity of the humerus
Answer: B. Lateral lip of the intertubercular groove
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pectoralis major; Proximal humerus: tubercles and the intertubercular groove
Why this is correct:
The muscle described is pectoralis major, and the lecture gives its distal attachment as the lateral lip of the intertubercular groove of the humerus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lesser tubercle is the attachment the lecture gives for subscapularis.
C. The coracoid process is where pectoralis minor attaches.
D. The lecture doesn't assign pectoralis major to the medial lip of the groove.
E. The lecture doesn't give pectoralis major an attachment to the deltoid tuberosity.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1876
A 35-year-old man injures the anterior chest wall and afterward has marked weakness of adduction and medial rotation of the arm, with reduced flexion when he tries to bring the limb forward. Which of the following muscles is most likely affected?
A. Subclavius
B. Pectoralis minor
C. Subscapularis
D. Serratus anterior
E. Pectoralis major
Answer: E. Pectoralis major
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pectoralis major
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists the actions of pectoralis major as adduction and medial rotation of the humerus plus flexion of the arm by the clavicular head, which together match this pattern of weakness.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Subclavius anchors and depresses the clavicle.
B. Pectoralis minor stabilizes the scapula by drawing it anteroinferiorly rather than moving the humerus.
C. Subscapularis medially rotates the arm but doesn't adduct or flex it in the lecture's account.
D. Serratus anterior protracts and upwardly rotates the scapula.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1877
During a dissection of the axilla, a student is asked to identify the muscle that forms most of the fold of tissue bounding the armpit in front. Which of the following muscles forms most of that fold?

D. Serratus anterior
E. Subscapularis
Answer: A. Pectoralis major

Difficulty: Easy

Why this is correct:
The lecture describes pectoralis major as a superficial, fan-shaped muscle that forms most of the anterior axillary fold, and it is one of the two muscles forming the anterior wall of the axilla.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Teres major contributes to the posterior wall of the axilla.
C. Latissimus dorsi contributes to the posterior wall of the axilla.
D. Serratus anterior forms the medial wall of the axilla.
E. Subscapularis forms part of the posterior wall of the axilla.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1878
During a dissection of the pectoral region, a triangular muscle lying deep to pectoralis major is traced from the 3rd through 5th ribs near their costal cartilages to a process on the scapula. Which of the following is the most likely attachment on the scapula?
A. Subscapular fossa
B. Acromion
C. Coracoid process
D. Glenoid fossa
E. Spine of the scapula
Answer: C. Coracoid process
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Pectoralis minor; Scapula: coracoid process, acromion, and glenoid fossa
Why this is correct:
The muscle described is pectoralis minor, and the lecture gives its superior attachment as the coracoid process of the scapula and its inferior attachment as ribs 3 through 5 close to the costal cartilage.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The subscapular fossa gives origin to subscapularis.
B. The acromion articulates with the clavicle and isn't where pectoralis minor attaches.
D. The glenoid fossa receives the head of the humerus.

Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1879
During an axillary dissection, the surgeon uses a triangular muscle crossing the axilla as the landmark that divides the artery behind it into named segments. Which of the following muscles serves as that landmark?
A. Pectoralis major
B. Subclavius
C. Teres major
D. Serratus anterior
E. Pectoralis minor
Answer: E. Pectoralis minor
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Pectoralis minor; Axillary artery: course and three parts
Why this is correct:
The lecture calls pectoralis minor an important clinical landmark used to locate and subdivide the axillary vessels deep to the muscle, and the axillary artery is divided into three parts by its relation to that muscle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pectoralis major is superficial to pectoralis minor and isn't the landmark used to subdivide the artery.
B. Subclavius sits under the clavicle, above the course of the axillary artery.
C. Teres major marks where the axillary artery becomes the brachial artery rather than dividing it into parts.
D. Serratus anterior forms the medial wall of the axilla rather than crossing the artery.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1880
During a dissection of the pectoral region, a nerve is followed as it passes through the substance of pectoralis minor and continues on to reach pectoralis major. Which of the following nerves is most likely being followed?
A. Long thoracic nerve
B. Lateral pectoral nerve
C. Medial pectoral nerve
D. Nerve to subclavius
E. Thoracodorsal nerve
Answer: C. Medial pectoral nerve
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Pectoralis minor; Branches of the lateral and medial cords
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the medial pectoral nerve supplies pectoralis major and minor and pierces pectoralis minor before traveling on to pectoralis major.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The long thoracic nerve runs on the superficial surface of serratus anterior.
B. The lateral pectoral nerve supplies pectoralis major but isn't described as piercing pectoralis minor.
D. The nerve to subclavius supplies the subclavius muscle.
E. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies latissimus dorsi.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1881
During a dissection of the pectoral region, a small muscle is found running from the 1st rib to the inferior surface of the middle third of the clavicle. Which of the following best describes the action of this muscle?
A. It flexes the arm at the shoulder
B. It anchors and depresses the clavicle
C. It protracts the scapula against the thoracic wall
D. It draws the scapula anteroinferiorly
E. It medially rotates the humerus
Answer: B. It anchors and depresses the clavicle
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Subclavius
Why this is correct:
The lecture gives the subclavius proximal attachment as the 1st rib, its distal attachment as the inferior side of the middle third of the clavicle, and its action as anchoring and depressing the clavicle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Flexion of the arm is performed by the clavicular head of pectoralis major.
C. Protracting the scapula against the thoracic wall is an action of serratus anterior.
D. Drawing the scapula anteroinferiorly is the action of pectoralis minor.
E. Medial rotation of the humerus is performed by subscapularis and pectoralis major.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1882
A 29-year-old man sustains a fracture of the middle third of the clavicle. During exploration, the surgeon notes a small muscle lying immediately beneath the bone that has limited the displacement of the fragments toward the underlying vessels. Which of the following roles does this muscle most likely serve?
A. It forms the apex of the axilla with the 1st rib
B. It provides minor protection to the underlying neurovascular structures
C. It holds the medial border of the scapula against the thoracic wall
D. It suspends the axillary sheath from the clavicle
E. It transmits force from the upper limb to the sternum
Answer: B. It provides minor protection to the underlying neurovascular structures
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Subclavius; Clavicle: articulations and functions
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that subclavius provides minor protection to the underlying neurovascular structures, for example in cases of clavicular fracture or other trauma.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The apex of the axilla is formed by the clavicle, 1st rib, and superior border of the scapula.
C. Holding the scapula against the thoracic wall is the role of serratus anterior.
D. The lecture doesn't describe subclavius as suspending the axillary sheath.
E. Transmitting force to the axial skeleton is a function of the clavicle itself.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1883
During a dissection of the root of the neck, a small nerve arising from the superior trunk of the brachial plexus is traced to a muscle lying beneath the clavicle. Which of the following muscles does this nerve most likely supply?
A. Serratus anterior
B. Subscapularis
C. Subclavius
D. Supraspinatus
E. Pectoralis minor
Answer: C. Subclavius
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Subclavius; Trunks of the brachial plexus: suprascapular nerve and nerve to subclavius
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the nerve to subclavius, also called the subclavian nerve, comes off the superior trunk and provides motor innervation to the subclavius muscle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Serratus anterior is supplied by the long thoracic nerve from the roots.
B. Subscapularis is supplied by the upper and lower subscapular nerves from the posterior cord.
D. Supraspinatus is supplied by the suprascapular nerve, which also arises from the superior trunk but doesn't reach beneath the clavicle.
E. Pectoralis minor is supplied by the medial pectoral nerve from the medial cord.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1885
A 26-year-old woman is unable to raise her right arm above shoulder height after an injury to the lateral chest wall. Testing shows that her scapula fails to rotate upward during attempted elevation. Which of the following muscles is most likely weakened?
A. Subclavius
B. Pectoralis major
C. Serratus anterior
D. Subscapularis
E. Pectoralis minor
Answer: C. Serratus anterior
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Serratus anterior; Long thoracic nerve injury and winged scapula

The lecture states that serratus anterior rotates the scapula upward, which allows the arm to be raised over 90 degrees, so weakness of this muscle limits elevation above shoulder height.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Subclavius anchors and depresses the clavicle.
B. Pectoralis major adducts, medially rotates, and flexes the arm rather than rotating the scapula upward.
D. Subscapularis medially rotates the arm at the glenohumeral joint.
E. Pectoralis minor draws the scapula anteroinferiorly rather than rotating it upward.
Objective: Identify the muscles of the pectoral region. Discuss their attachments, innervation, and actions. Describe the clinical implications of nerve damage and their loss of function.

Question 1886
During a dissection of the axilla, a long thin nerve is found running on the outer surface of the muscle it supplies rather than on its deep surface. Which of the following muscles is this nerve most likely supplying?

E. Latissimus dorsi

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Serratus anterior; Long thoracic nerve injury and winged scapula

The lecture states that the long thoracic nerve is unique because it innervates serratus anterior on the muscle's superficial side, which is what makes it vulnerable to injury.
Why the other answers are wrong:

A 48-year-old woman notices that the inner edge of her right shoulder blade sticks out from her back after a mastectomy with axillary dissection. The protrusion is more obvious when she pushes against a wall. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?

Question 1888
A physician evaluating a patient for suspected nerve injury asks him to place both palms flat against a wall and push forward. Which of the following findings would most likely indicate that serratus anterior is paralyzed?
A. Sensation is lost over the skin covering the deltoid muscle
B. The arm falls into medial rotation with the forearm pronated
C. The medial border of the scapula becomes abnormally prominent

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Long thoracic nerve injury and winged scapula; Serratus anterior

Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Loss of sensation over the deltoid follows axillary nerve injury.
B. A medially rotated arm with a pronated forearm is the waiter's tip position of Erb palsy.

B. It winds around the surgical neck of the humerus

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1890
During dissection of the axilla, a muscle is traced from the large fossa on the costal surface of the scapula to a prominence on the superior anterior aspect of the humerus. The muscle fills the space between the scapula and the thoracic wall. Which of the following is most likely the action of this muscle?
A. Medial rotation of the arm

The muscle described arises from the subscapular fossa and inserts on the lesser tubercle of the humerus, which identifies it as subscapularis. The lecture lists its action as medial rotation of the arm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Extension of the arm is not an action the lecture assigns to any pectoral region muscle.
C. Protraction of the scapula is the action of serratus anterior.
D. Upward rotation of the scapula is produced by serratus anterior, not by a muscle inserting on the humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1893
A penetrating wound enters the armpit from the medial side and damages the wall of the axilla that lies directly against the ribs. The patient later has difficulty raising the arm above the horizontal plane. Which of the following most likely forms the wall that was injured?
A. Teres major
B. Serratus anterior

D. Pectoralis minor
E. Latissimus dorsi

Difficulty: Medium

The medial wall of the axilla is formed by serratus anterior together with the thoracic wall. Serratus anterior rotates the scapula upward, which is what allows the arm to be raised past 90 degrees, so losing it explains the deficit.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Teres major contributes to the posterior wall of the axilla.
C. Subscapularis contributes to the posterior wall of the axilla.
D. Pectoralis minor contributes to the anterior wall of the axilla.
E. Latissimus dorsi contributes to the posterior wall of the axilla.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1894
A tumor spreads from the root of the neck into the axilla by passing through the narrow triangular gap at the top of the axillary pyramid. Which of the following structures most likely bound this passageway?
A. Clavicle, first rib, and superior border of the scapula
B. Manubrium, first rib, and acromion
C. First rib, coracoid process, and humeral head
D. Scapular spine, clavicle, and second rib
E. Clavicle, second rib, and coracoid process
Answer: A. Clavicle, first rib, and superior border of the scapula
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Boundaries of the axilla
Why this is correct:
The apex of the axilla is the small triangular gap between the clavicle, the first rib, and the superior border of the scapula, and it forms the cervico-axillary canal between the neck and the axilla.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The manubrium and acromion are not listed as boundaries of the apex.
C. The humeral head lies at the lateral wall, not at the apex.
D. The scapular spine and second rib are not listed as boundaries of the apex.
E. The second rib and coracoid process are not listed as boundaries of the apex.
Objective: Discuss the contents and boundaries of the axilla.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1895
During a cadaver dissection the narrow lateral wall of the axilla is identified where the anterior and posterior walls converge on the humerus. Which of the following most likely forms this wall?
A. Coracoid process of the scapula
B. Surgical neck of the humerus
C. Lesser tubercle of the humerus
D. Intertubercular groove of the humerus
E. Lateral border of the scapula
Answer: D. Intertubercular groove of the humerus
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Boundaries of the axilla
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the lateral wall of the axilla as a narrow wall at the intertubercular groove of the humerus, where the anterior and posterior walls meet.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The coracoid process is an attachment site for pectoralis minor on the anterior wall.
B. The surgical neck is a fracture site distal to the tubercles, not the named lateral wall.
C. The lesser tubercle is the attachment for subscapularis rather than the lateral wall itself.
E. The lateral border of the scapula contributes to the posterior wall region, not the lateral wall.
Objective: Discuss the contents and boundaries of the axilla.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1896
A 29-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a stab wound to the armpit. He has a cool, pulseless upper limb, a rapidly expanding hematoma, and weakness in several muscle groups of the arm and forearm. Which of the following groups of structures is most likely injured together?
A. Axillary artery, axillary vein, and cords of the brachial plexus
B. Subclavian artery, internal jugular vein, and cervical plexus
C. Internal thoracic artery, azygos vein, and intercostal nerves
D. Lateral thoracic artery, thoracic duct, and phrenic nerve
E. Brachial artery, cephalic vein, and roots of the brachial plexus
Answer: A. Axillary artery, axillary vein, and cords of the brachial plexus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Contents of the axilla
Why this is correct:
The contents of the axilla are the axillary artery and its branches, the axillary vein and its tributaries, the cords and branches of the brachial plexus, the axillary lymph nodes, and axillary fat, so a single penetrating wound can injure all three together.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. These structures lie in the neck rather than in the axilla.
C. These structures lie within the thorax.
D. The thoracic duct and phrenic nerve are not axillary contents.
E. The brachial artery and the plexus roots lie distal and proximal to the axilla respectively.
Objective: Discuss the contents and boundaries of the axilla.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1897
A pathologist receives tissue removed from the axilla during a staging procedure and is asked to confirm that the specimen came from a normal content of that space. Which of the following structures is most likely to be present in the specimen?
A. Subclavian vein
B. Axillary lymph nodes
C. Parasternal lymph nodes
D. Thoracic duct
E. Internal thoracic artery
Answer: B. Axillary lymph nodes
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Contents of the axilla; Lymphatic drainage of the breast
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists the axillary lymph nodes among the contents of the axilla, along with the axillary artery, axillary vein, brachial plexus, and fat.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The axillary vein becomes the subclavian vein only after it passes the first rib, so the subclavian vein lies beyond the axilla.
C. Parasternal nodes lie near the internal thoracic artery alongside the sternum.
D. The thoracic duct returns lymph to the venous system at the root of the neck.
E. The internal thoracic artery runs inside the thoracic wall, not in the axilla.
Objective: Discuss the contents and boundaries of the axilla.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1898
An instructor points out that although the axilla carries the entire neurovascular supply of the upper limb in a space covered only by skin and fascia at its base, injuries to its contents are relatively uncommon in everyday life. Which of the following best explains this observation?
A. The apex of the axilla is closed by the superior border of the scapula
B. The pectoralis minor tendon holds the contents against the thoracic wall
C. The adducted upper limb covers and shields the base of the axilla
D. The axillary fascia is reinforced by the costal cartilages of ribs 1 to 4
E. The axillary fat pad absorbs most of the force applied to the region
Answer: C. The adducted upper limb covers and shields the base of the axilla
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Contents of the axilla; Boundaries of the axilla
Why this is correct:
The lecture notes that the axilla provides a passageway for neurovascular structures to and from the upper limb and that it is protected by the adducted upper limb.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The apex is an open passageway, the cervico-axillary canal, rather than a closed protective lid.
B. Pectoralis minor is a landmark on the anterior wall and a scapular stabilizer, not a restraint on the axillary contents.
D. The costal cartilages lie in the medial wall and do not reinforce the base.
E. Axillary fat is listed as a content of the space, not as a protective mechanism.
Objective: Discuss the contents and boundaries of the axilla.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1899
During an operation the surgeon follows the main artery of the upper limb proximally and notes the point at which its name changes from subclavian to axillary. Which of the following most likely marks this transition?
A. Medial border of pectoralis major
B. Inferior border of teres major
C. Lateral third of the clavicle
D. Lateral border of the first rib
E. Lateral border of pectoralis minor
Answer: D. Lateral border of the first rib
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Axillary artery: course and three parts
Why this is correct:
The axillary artery begins at the lateral border of the first rib as the continuation of the subclavian artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pectoralis major forms the anterior wall of the axilla but does not define the artery's name change.
B. The inferior border of teres major is where the axillary artery becomes the brachial artery.
C. The clavicle lies superficial to the vessel and is not the landmark used.
E. The borders of pectoralis minor divide the axillary artery into its three parts rather than marking its origin.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1900
A 61-year-old man undergoes angiography of the left upper limb. The catheter is advanced from the aorta directly into the vessel that becomes the axillary artery, without passing through any intervening trunk. Which of the following best explains this finding?
A. On the left the subclavian artery arises from the brachiocephalic trunk
B. On the left the thoracoacromial trunk connects the aorta to the axillary artery
C. On the left the superior thoracic artery replaces the proximal subclavian artery
D. On the left the axillary artery arises directly from the descending aorta
E. On the left the subclavian artery branches directly from the arch of the aorta
Answer: E. On the left the subclavian artery branches directly from the arch of the aorta
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Axillary artery: course and three parts
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that on the right the subclavian artery arises from the brachiocephalic trunk, while on the left it branches directly from the arch of the aorta, so no intervening trunk is crossed on the left.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. That pattern describes the right side.
B. The thoracoacromial trunk is a branch of the second part of the axillary artery.
C. The superior thoracic artery is a small branch of the first part of the axillary artery.
D. The axillary artery is always the continuation of the subclavian artery at the first rib.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1901
A radiologist describing an axillary arteriogram divides the vessel into three parts and explains that the subdivision depends on one overlying muscle. Which of the following muscles is most likely used as this landmark?
A. Serratus anterior
B. Teres major
C. Subclavius
D. Pectoralis major
E. Pectoralis minor
Answer: E. Pectoralis minor
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Axillary artery: course and three parts; Pectoralis minor
Why this is correct:
Pectoralis minor crosses the axillary artery and divides it into a first part proximal to the muscle, a second part deep to it, and a third part distal to it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Serratus anterior forms the medial wall of the axilla rather than crossing the artery.
B. Teres major marks the end of the axillary artery, not its subdivisions.
C. Subclavius lies under the clavicle above the level of the artery's parts.
D. Pectoralis major is superficial to pectoralis minor and is not the landmark used.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1902
During dissection the segment of the axillary artery lying proximal to the superior border of pectoralis minor is cleaned, and a single small branch is found leaving it. Which of the following is most likely that branch?
A. Thoracoacromial trunk
B. Lateral thoracic artery
C. Superior thoracic artery
D. Subscapular artery
E. Anterior humeral circumflex artery
Answer: C. Superior thoracic artery
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: First part of the axillary artery: superior thoracic artery; Axillary artery: course and three parts
Why this is correct:
The first part of the axillary artery lies proximal to pectoralis minor and gives off one branch, the superior thoracic artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The thoracoacromial trunk arises from the second part.
B. The lateral thoracic artery arises from the second part.
D. The subscapular artery arises from the third part.
E. The anterior humeral circumflex artery arises from the third part.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1903
A small artery arising from the axillary artery close to the first rib is followed medially into the upper thoracic wall. Which of the following territories does this vessel most likely supply?

C. The acromion, clavicle, deltoid, and pectoral muscles
D. Latissimus dorsi and the posterior scapular muscles

Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: First part of the axillary artery: superior thoracic artery
Why this is correct:
The superior thoracic artery is the single branch of the first part of the axillary artery, and the lecture states that it supplies the first and second intercostal spaces.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Serratus anterior and the lateral breast are supplied by the lateral thoracic artery.
C. These territories are supplied by the four branches of the thoracoacromial trunk.
