MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1904
An artery supplying the first two intercostal spaces is traced back toward its origin on the axillary artery. The point of origin lies above the superior border of pectoralis minor. Which of the following is most likely the part of the axillary artery that gave rise to it?

C. Third part
D. The brachial artery distal to teres major
E. Second part
Answer: B. First part
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: First part of the axillary artery: superior thoracic artery; Axillary artery: course and three parts
Why this is correct:
The part of the axillary artery proximal to pectoralis minor is the first part, and its only branch is the superior thoracic artery to the first and second intercostal spaces.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The vessel proximal to the first rib is the subclavian artery, which is not divided into these parts.
C. The third part lies distal to pectoralis minor and gives off three branches.
D. The brachial artery begins only after the inferior border of teres major.
E. The second part lies deep to pectoralis minor and gives off the thoracoacromial trunk and lateral thoracic artery.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1905
During a mastectomy the surgeon identifies an artery descending along the lateral margin of pectoralis minor and running with a nerve on the superficial surface of the muscle that forms the medial wall of the axilla. Which of the following is most likely this artery?
A. Circumflex scapular artery
B. Thoracodorsal artery
C. Superior thoracic artery
D. Thoracoacromial trunk
E. Lateral thoracic artery
Answer: E. Lateral thoracic artery
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Second part of the axillary artery: thoracoacromial trunk and lateral thoracic artery; Long thoracic nerve injury and winged scapula
Why this is correct:
The lateral thoracic artery courses inferiorly along the lateral margin of pectoralis minor and usually runs with the long thoracic nerve on the superficial surface of serratus anterior, the muscle of the medial wall.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The circumflex scapular artery curves around the lateral border of the scapula.
B. The thoracodorsal artery runs with the thoracodorsal nerve to latissimus dorsi.
C. The superior thoracic artery is a small vessel of the first part supplying the upper two intercostal spaces.
D. The thoracoacromial trunk runs superiorly and divides into four branches near the clavicle and acromion.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1906
A short arterial trunk arising deep to pectoralis minor divides almost immediately into four branches that supply the acromion, the clavicle, the pectoral muscles, and a muscle attaching to the deltoid tuberosity. Which of the following branches most likely supplies the last of these?

D. Acromial branch
E. Circumflex scapular branch

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Second part of the axillary artery: thoracoacromial trunk and lateral thoracic artery
Why this is correct:
The thoracoacromial trunk divides into clavicular, acromial, deltoid, and pectoral branches, and the deltoid branch supplies the deltoid muscle, which attaches to the deltoid tuberosity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The clavicular branch supplies the clavicle.
C. The pectoral branch supplies pectoralis major and pectoralis minor.
D. The acromial branch supplies the acromion.
E. The circumflex scapular artery is a branch of the subscapular artery, not of the thoracoacromial trunk.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1907

The second part of the axillary artery lies deep to pectoralis minor and gives off two branches, the thoracoacromial trunk and the lateral thoracic artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1908
A 67-year-old woman sustains a fracture of the surgical neck of the humerus. Imaging shows injury to a vessel that had passed medially through the posterior wall of the axilla in company with a nerve before reaching the humerus. Which of the following vessels was most likely injured?
A. Anterior humeral circumflex artery
B. Thoracodorsal artery
C. Posterior humeral circumflex artery
D. Subscapular artery
E. Circumflex scapular artery
Answer: C. Posterior humeral circumflex artery
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Third part of the axillary artery: subscapular and humeral circumflex arteries
Why this is correct:
The posterior humeral circumflex artery passes medially through the posterior wall of the axilla by way of the quadrangular space together with the axillary nerve, which places it at the level of the surgical neck.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The anterior humeral circumflex artery passes anteriorly around the surgical neck and does not travel through the posterior wall.
B. The thoracodorsal artery descends with the thoracodorsal nerve to latissimus dorsi.
D. The subscapular artery is the short parent trunk and does not itself reach the humerus.
E. The circumflex scapular artery curves around the lateral border of the scapula to the posterior scapular muscles.
Objective: Identify the axillary artery and vein. Trace the course of the major branches arising from the axillary artery and describe their course/distribution.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla

The subscapular artery divides into the circumflex scapular artery and the thoracodorsal artery, and the thoracodorsal artery continues inferiorly with the thoracodorsal nerve to supply latissimus dorsi.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1910
In a dissection of the third part of the axillary artery, a branch is followed as it curves around the lateral border of the scapula to reach muscles on the posterior aspect of that bone, where it takes part in an arterial network. Which of the following is most likely this branch?
A. Circumflex scapular artery
B. Lateral thoracic artery

E. Posterior humeral circumflex artery
Answer: A. Circumflex scapular artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Third part of the axillary artery: subscapular and humeral circumflex arteries
Why this is correct:
The circumflex scapular artery is one of the two divisions of the subscapular artery, and it courses around the lateral border of the scapula to supply the posterior scapular muscles and join the arterial anastomoses around the scapula.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The lateral thoracic artery arises from the second part and runs on the thoracic wall.
C. The anterior humeral circumflex artery encircles the surgical neck of the humerus anteriorly.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1911
A resident placing a needle into the axilla is warned that one large vessel will be encountered before the main artery is reached, because the two lie together within a common fibrous covering. Which of the following best describes the position of the vein relative to the artery in the axilla?
A. The vein lies anterior to the artery
B. The vein lies posterior to the artery
C. The vein lies outside the fibrous covering of the artery
D. The vein lies lateral to the artery
E. The vein lies deep to the posterior cord
Answer: A. The vein lies anterior to the artery
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Axillary vein and the axillary sheath
Why this is correct:
The axillary vein lies anterior to the axillary artery within the axillary sheath, so an anterior approach reaches the vein first.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The artery lies posterior to the vein rather than the reverse.
C. The axillary sheath covers the artery and the three cords, and the vein lies with them in the axilla.
D. The vein lies anterior rather than lateral.
E. The cords of the plexus surround the artery, and the vein is not deep to the posterior cord.
Objective: Identify and discuss the venous drainage patterns of the axillary and cephalic veins.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1912
During dissection of the arm two veins are followed distally from the point where the axillary vein begins. One is a deep vein accompanying an artery and the other is a superficial vein on the medial side of the limb. Which of the following best describes the formation of the axillary vein?
A. Union of the subclavian and cephalic veins
B. Union of the brachial and basilic veins
C. Union of the cephalic and basilic veins
D. Union of the brachial and cephalic veins
E. Union of the radial and ulnar veins
Answer: B. Union of the brachial and basilic veins
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Axillary vein and the axillary sheath; Basilic vein
Why this is correct:
The axillary vein is formed by the union of the brachial and basilic veins in the arm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The axillary vein becomes the subclavian vein, so the subclavian vein cannot help form it.
C. The cephalic vein joins the axillary vein near the clavicle rather than helping to form it.
D. The cephalic vein is not one of the two veins that unite to form the axillary vein.
E. The radial and ulnar veins unite to form the brachial veins in the forearm.
Objective: Identify and discuss the venous drainage patterns of the axillary and cephalic veins.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1916
Two superficial veins are identified in a dissection of the upper limb. One of them is found lying in the groove between the deltoid and pectoralis major muscles. Which of the following best describes the other superficial vein of the limb?

B. It accompanies the brachial artery through the arm
C. It drains the lateral breast into the internal thoracic vein
D. It passes through the quadrangular space with the axillary nerve
E. It runs laterally and joins the subclavian vein

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Basilic vein; Cephalic vein and the deltopectoral groove
Why this is correct:
The vein in the deltopectoral groove is the cephalic vein, so the other named superficial vein of the arm and forearm is the basilic vein, which runs medially and joins the brachial vein to form the axillary vein.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The deep brachial veins accompany the brachial artery, and they are not superficial veins.
C. The lateral breast drains toward the axillary nodes and vessels rather than by a superficial limb vein.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla

Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1918
A catheter is threaded up the superficial vein of the deltopectoral groove and is advanced until it enters the next named vessel just below the clavicle. Which of the following vessels does the catheter most likely enter at that point?
A. Axillary vein
B. Internal thoracic vein
C. Basilic vein
D. Brachial vein
E. Subclavian vein
Answer: A. Axillary vein
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Cephalic vein and the deltopectoral groove; Axillary vein and the axillary sheath
Why this is correct:
The cephalic vein runs in the deltopectoral groove and empties directly into the axillary vein near or just below the clavicle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The internal thoracic vein drains the anterior thoracic wall and medial breast.
C. The basilic vein is the separate superficial vein of the medial arm.
D. The brachial vein lies deep in the arm and is not entered from the deltopectoral groove.
E. The subclavian vein begins only after the axillary vein crosses the lateral border of the first rib.
Objective: Identify and discuss the venous drainage patterns of the axillary and cephalic veins.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1919
A surgeon plans a cut-down to expose the superficial vein that lies in the groove on the anterior aspect of the shoulder. Which of the following pairs of muscles must be separated to reach this vessel?
A. Subclavius and pectoralis major
B. Deltoid and subscapularis
C. Deltoid and pectoralis major
D. Pectoralis major and pectoralis minor
E. Pectoralis minor and serratus anterior
Answer: C. Deltoid and pectoralis major
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Cephalic vein and the deltopectoral groove; Pectoralis major
Why this is correct:
The deltopectoral groove separates the deltoid from pectoralis major, and the cephalic vein runs within that groove.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Subclavius lies beneath the clavicle and does not border the groove.
B. Subscapularis lies on the anterior surface of the scapula in the posterior wall of the axilla.
D. Pectoralis minor lies deep to pectoralis major, and the interval between them does not contain the cephalic vein.
E. Serratus anterior forms the medial wall of the axilla.
Objective: Identify and discuss the venous drainage patterns of the axillary and cephalic veins.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1920
An anatomist describes the nerve network of the upper limb as arising from a specific set of spinal nerve components rather than from the whole spinal nerves. Which of the following most likely gives rise to this network?
A. Posterior rami of C5 to T1
B. Anterior rami of C6 to T2
C. Anterior rami of C4 to C8
D. Posterior rami of C4 to T1
E. Anterior rami of C5 to T1
Answer: E. Anterior rami of C5 to T1
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Brachial plexus: overall organization
Why this is correct:
The brachial plexus is a network of nerves derived from the anterior rami of the C5 to T1 spinal nerves.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Posterior rami supply the deep back muscles and the overlying skin.
B. The plexus does not extend to T2 and begins at C5.
C. The plexus begins at C5 and includes T1.
D. The plexus is formed from anterior rami, not posterior rami.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

Question 1921
A student is asked to trace the brachial plexus in sequence from the spinal nerves to the named peripheral nerves of the upper limb. Which of the following best describes the correct order of its components?
A. Roots, divisions, trunks, cords, branches
B. Roots, trunks, divisions, cords, branches
C. Roots, trunks, cords, divisions, branches
D. Roots, cords, trunks, divisions, branches
E. Trunks, roots, cords, divisions, branches
Answer: B. Roots, trunks, divisions, cords, branches
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Brachial plexus: overall organization
Why this is correct:
The plexus is organized as roots, trunks, divisions, cords, and branches, which the lecture gives with the mnemonic Real Truckers Drink Cold Beer.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The trunks form before the divisions appear.
C. The divisions lie between the trunks and the cords.
D. The cords form from the divisions, well after the trunks.

Why the other answers are wrong:

B. The axillary lymph nodes lie in the fat of the axilla and are not encircled by the cords.

Question 1923
A 24-year-old man has weakness retracting the scapula and elevating its medial border after a deep laceration in the posterior triangle of the neck. The injured nerve was found arising directly from a single root of the brachial plexus. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?
A. Dorsal scapular nerve
B. Suprascapular nerve
C. Long thoracic nerve
D. Thoracodorsal nerve
E. Nerve to subclavius
Answer: A. Dorsal scapular nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Roots of the brachial plexus: dorsal scapular and long thoracic nerves
Why this is correct:
The dorsal scapular nerve arises directly from the C5 root and supplies levator scapulae, rhomboid major, and rhomboid minor, the muscles that retract the scapula and elevate its medial border.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The suprascapular nerve comes off the superior trunk and supplies supraspinatus and infraspinatus.
C. The long thoracic nerve also comes off the roots but supplies serratus anterior, which protracts the scapula.
D. The thoracodorsal nerve comes off the posterior cord and supplies latissimus dorsi.
E. The nerve to subclavius comes off the superior trunk and supplies subclavius.

Question 1926
A dissection of the brachial plexus in the neck shows that one of the three trunks is not formed by the union of two roots but instead continues from a single root. Which of the following roots most likely continues as that trunk?
A. C5
B. C6
C. T1
D. C8
E. C7
Answer: E. C7
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Trunks of the brachial plexus: suprascapular nerve and nerve to subclavius
Why this is correct:
The middle trunk is the continuation of the C7 root, while the superior trunk is formed by C5 and C6 and the inferior trunk by C8 and T1.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. C5 unites with C6 to form the superior trunk.
B. C6 unites with C5 to form the superior trunk.
C. T1 unites with C8 to form the inferior trunk.

Question 1929
A peripheral nerve supplying extensor muscles of the upper limb is traced proximally through the brachial plexus to the point where the trunks split. Which of the following components of the plexus most likely gave rise to this nerve?
A. The anterior divisions
B. The medial cord
C. The posterior divisions
D. The C8 and T1 roots
E. The superior trunk
Answer: C. The posterior divisions
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Divisions of the brachial plexus
Why this is correct:
Each trunk splits into an anterior and a posterior division, and nerves arising from the posterior divisions generally supply the extensor compartments of the upper extremity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Nerves from the anterior divisions generally supply the flexor compartments.
B. The medial cord is formed from an anterior division and supplies flexor compartment nerves.
D. Root level does not determine whether a nerve supplies flexors or extensors.
E. The superior trunk gives rise to both an anterior and a posterior division, so it does not by itself determine the compartment.

Question 1932
During dissection of the axilla a large cord of the brachial plexus is found to receive contributions from the posterior part of every trunk. Which of the following cords is most likely being described?
A. Inferior trunk
B. Medial cord
C. Posterior cord
D. Lateral cord
E. Superior trunk
Answer: C. Posterior cord

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Cords of the brachial plexus and their relation to the axillary artery

D. The lateral cord is formed from the anterior divisions of the superior and middle trunks only.
E. The superior trunk is formed proximally by C5 and C6 and is not a cord.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1938
A 45-year-old man has weakness of extension, adduction, and medial rotation of the arm after a penetrating injury to the posterior wall of the axilla. The nerve involved is also known as the middle subscapular nerve. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?
A. Upper subscapular nerve
B. Thoracodorsal nerve
C. Dorsal scapular nerve
D. Lower subscapular nerve
E. Axillary nerve
Answer: B. Thoracodorsal nerve
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Branches of the posterior cord
Why this is correct:
The thoracodorsal nerve (C6, 7, 8), also called the middle subscapular nerve, arises from the posterior cord and supplies latissimus dorsi.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The upper subscapular nerve supplies subscapularis.
C. The dorsal scapular nerve arises from the C5 root and supplies the rhomboids and levator scapulae.
D. The lower subscapular nerve supplies subscapularis and teres major.
E. The axillary nerve supplies deltoid and teres minor.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

Question 1939
A branch of the posterior cord is traced to two muscles, one on the anterior surface of the scapula and one arising from the inferior angle of the scapula. Which of the following nerves most likely supplies both of these muscles?
A. Axillary nerve
B. Suprascapular nerve
C. Upper subscapular nerve
D. Lower subscapular nerve
E. Thoracodorsal nerve
Answer: D. Lower subscapular nerve

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Branches of the posterior cord
Why this is correct:
The lower subscapular nerve (C5, 6) supplies both subscapularis, on the anterior surface of the scapula, and teres major.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The axillary nerve supplies deltoid and teres minor.
B. The suprascapular nerve arises from the superior trunk and supplies supraspinatus and infraspinatus.
C. The upper subscapular nerve supplies subscapularis only.
E. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies latissimus dorsi only.

Question 1940
Three nerves supplying subscapularis, teres major, and latissimus dorsi are traced proximally in the axilla and are all found to arise from the same component of the brachial plexus. Which of the following is most likely their common origin?
A. Lateral cord
B. C5 root
C. Medial cord
D. Superior trunk
E. Posterior cord
Answer: E. Posterior cord
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Branches of the posterior cord; Cords of the brachial plexus and their relation to the axillary artery
Why this is correct:
The upper subscapular, thoracodorsal, and lower subscapular nerves all come directly off the posterior cord.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lateral cord gives off the lateral pectoral nerve, the lateral root of the median nerve, and the musculocutaneous nerve.
B. The C5 root gives off the dorsal scapular nerve and contributes to the long thoracic nerve.
C. The medial cord gives off the medial pectoral nerve, the two medial cutaneous nerves, and the medial root of the median nerve.
D. The superior trunk gives off the suprascapular nerve and the nerve to subclavius.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1941
A 22-year-old man cannot flex his forearm at the elbow with the forearm supinated and has lost sensation over the lateral aspect of the forearm. During exploration a nerve is found entering the substance of coracobrachialis. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?
A. Median nerve
B. Radial nerve
C. Ulnar nerve
D. Musculocutaneous nerve
E. Axillary nerve
Answer: D. Musculocutaneous nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Terminal branches: musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves
Why this is correct:
The musculocutaneous nerve (C5 to C7) supplies the anterior compartment muscles of the arm and pierces coracobrachialis, then continues as the lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm to supply skin on the lateral forearm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The median nerve supplies most forearm flexors and the thenar muscles, with sensation on the lateral palm and lateral three and a half digits.
B. The radial nerve supplies the posterior compartments of the arm and forearm.
C. The ulnar nerve supplies flexor carpi ulnaris, flexor digitorum profundus, and most intrinsic hand muscles.
E. The axillary nerve supplies deltoid and teres minor with sensation over the superior lateral shoulder.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

Question 1942
A 54-year-old woman has weakness of most of the small muscles of the hand together with loss of sensation over the medial one and a half digits. Forearm flexion and supination are preserved. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?
A. Radial nerve
B. Musculocutaneous nerve
C. Medial cutaneous nerve of the forearm

Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Terminal branches: musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves

The ulnar nerve (C8, T1) is motor to flexor carpi ulnaris, flexor digitorum profundus, and most intrinsic hand muscles, and sensory from the medial hand and the medial one and a half digits.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve supplies the extensors and the skin of the posterior limb.
B. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior arm muscles and skin of the lateral forearm.
C. The medial cutaneous nerve of the forearm is purely sensory to the medial forearm.
E. The median nerve supplies the thenar muscles and two lateral lumbricals with sensation over the lateral three and a half digits.

Question 1943
A terminal branch of the brachial plexus receives fibers from all five roots and supplies the thenar muscles and the two lateral lumbricals, along with skin over the lateral part of the palm. Which of the following is most likely this nerve?

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Terminal branches: musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves

The median nerve carries C5 to T1 fibers and supplies most forearm flexors, the thenar muscles, and the two lateral lumbricals, with sensation from the lateral palm and the lateral three and a half digits.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve carries C5 to T1 fibers but supplies the extensor compartments.
B. The musculocutaneous nerve carries C5 to C7 fibers and supplies the anterior arm.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1944
A 71-year-old woman cannot abduct her arm and has lost sensation over the skin of the superior lateral aspect of the shoulder after a fall. Laterally rotating the arm is also weak. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?
A. Radial nerve
B. Axillary nerve

Answer: B. Axillary nerve

The axillary nerve (C5, 6) is motor to deltoid and teres minor and sensory from the skin over the superior lateral shoulder, which matches both the weak abduction and lateral rotation and the sensory loss.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve supplies triceps brachii and the posterior forearm extensors.
C. The lateral pectoral nerve is motor to pectoralis major.
D. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior arm muscles and skin of the lateral forearm.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1947
A newborn delivered after a difficult vaginal delivery holds the right upper limb limply at the side. The limb is medially rotated, the forearm is pronated, and the wrist is weakly flexed. Sensation is reduced over the lateral aspect of the limb. Which of the following best describes the injury?
A. Injury to the medial cord of the brachial plexus
B. Injury to the C8 and T1 roots of the brachial plexus
C. Injury to the C5 and C6 roots of the brachial plexus
D. Injury to the C7 root of the brachial plexus
E. Injury to the posterior cord of the brachial plexus
Answer: C. Injury to the C5 and C6 roots of the brachial plexus
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Erb palsy
Why this is correct:
The waiter's tip posture with sensory loss over the lateral limb is Erb palsy, an upper brachial plexus injury at the C5 and C6 roots, which can follow a difficult vaginal delivery.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The medial cord carries C8 and T1 fibers, which would not give this posture.
B. C8 and T1 injury produces Klumpke palsy with a clawed hand and medial limb sensory loss.
D. The lecture does not describe an isolated C7 root injury syndrome.
E. Posterior cord injury would primarily affect the axillary and radial nerves without this characteristic posture.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1948
A 19-year-old man thrown from a motorcycle sustains an upper brachial plexus injury. On examination the affected forearm rests in pronation. Which of the following most directly explains this position of the forearm?
A. Loss of the intrinsic muscles of the hand
B. Loss of biceps brachii
C. Loss of supraspinatus and infraspinatus
D. Unopposed action of pectoralis major
E. Normal tone of the wrist flexors
Answer: B. Loss of biceps brachii
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Erb palsy
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that in Erb palsy the forearm is pronated due to the loss of biceps brachii, which is a supinator of the forearm and is supplied by the musculocutaneous nerve from C5 and C6.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The intrinsic hand muscles are affected in Klumpke palsy and produce a clawed hand.
C. Loss of supraspinatus and infraspinatus accounts for weak abduction and lateral rotation at the shoulder.
D. The unopposed action of pectoralis major accounts for medial rotation of the limb, not forearm pronation.
E. Normal tone of the wrist flexors relative to weakened extensors accounts for the weakly flexed wrist.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1949
A 27-year-old woman is thrown from a horse and lands so that the angle between her neck and shoulder is forced open. Imaging shows stretching of the superior part of the brachial plexus. Which of the following combinations of motor losses is most likely?
A. Adduction of the arm, medial rotation of the arm, and extension of the arm
B. Extension of the elbow, extension of the wrist, and extension of the fingers
C. Protraction of the scapula, retraction of the scapula, and elevation of the scapula
D. Abduction at the shoulder, lateral rotation of the arm, and supination of the forearm
E. Flexion of the fingers, opposition of the thumb, and adduction of the fingers
Answer: D. Abduction at the shoulder, lateral rotation of the arm, and supination of the forearm
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Erb palsy; Trunks of the brachial plexus: suprascapular nerve and nerve to subclavius
Why this is correct:
Erb palsy affects nerves from C5 and C6, including the suprascapular, axillary, and musculocutaneous nerves, so the motor functions lost are abduction at the shoulder, lateral rotation of the arm, supination of the forearm, and flexion at the shoulder.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Adduction and medial rotation are preserved, and the limb is held medially rotated because pectoralis major is unopposed.
B. These are radial nerve functions, which the lecture does not list among the losses in Erb palsy.
C. Scapular movements depend on the dorsal scapular and long thoracic nerves rather than the C5 and C6 peripheral nerves listed.
E. These are functions of the intrinsic hand muscles lost in Klumpke palsy.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1950
A 33-year-old man slips from a ladder and catches a rung with one hand, so that the upper limb is suddenly pulled superiorly. He later has a clawed hand and numbness along the medial side of the limb. Which of the following best describes the level of injury?
A. The lateral cord and its branches
B. The C8 and T1 roots and the inferior trunk
C. The C7 root and the middle trunk
D. The C5 and C6 roots and the superior trunk
E. The posterior cord and its branches
Answer: B. The C8 and T1 roots and the inferior trunk
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Klumpke palsy
Why this is correct:
Klumpke palsy is a lower brachial plexus injury of the C8 and T1 roots that occurs when the upper limb is suddenly pulled superiorly, injuring the inferior trunk and sometimes avulsing the roots.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lateral cord carries C5 to C7 fibers and does not produce a clawed hand.
C. The lecture does not describe an isolated middle trunk syndrome.
D. C5 and C6 injury produces Erb palsy with the waiter's tip posture.
E. The posterior cord supplies the axillary and radial nerves and does not produce a clawed hand.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1951
A newborn whose upper limb was pulled excessively during delivery is noted to hold the fingers extended at the metacarpophalangeal joints and flexed at the interphalangeal joints. Which of the following most directly explains this posture?
A. Paralysis of the long finger extensors
B. Unopposed action of pectoralis major
C. Paralysis of biceps brachii
D. Paralysis of the lumbrical muscles
E. Weakness of flexor carpi ulnaris alone
Answer: D. Paralysis of the lumbrical muscles
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Klumpke palsy
Why this is correct:
The lumbricals normally flex the metacarpophalangeal joints and extend the interphalangeal joints, so when they are paralyzed the fingers take the opposite position and the hand looks clawed.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The long finger extensors are supplied by the radial nerve, which is not the nerve affected here.
B. An unopposed pectoralis major holds the limb medially rotated in Erb palsy.
C. Loss of biceps brachii produces a pronated forearm in Erb palsy.
E. Flexor carpi ulnaris acts at the wrist and does not by itself produce the clawed position.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Pectoral Region and Axilla
Question 1952
A patient with a lower brachial plexus injury is examined for sensory loss. Motor testing shows weakness confined to the small muscles of the hand. Which of the following distributions of sensory loss is most likely?
A. Along the lateral side of the upper limb
B. Along the medial side of the upper limb
C. Over the lateral aspect of the forearm only
D. Over the posterior aspect of the arm and forearm
E. Over the superior lateral aspect of the shoulder
Answer: B. Along the medial side of the upper limb
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Klumpke palsy
Why this is correct:
Klumpke palsy involves the C8 and T1 roots, and the lecture states that sensation is affected along the medial side of the upper limb in the C8 and T1 dermatomes.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Sensory loss over the lateral limb in the C5 and C6 dermatomes occurs in Erb palsy.
C. Loss limited to the lateral forearm follows musculocutaneous nerve injury.
D. Loss over the posterior arm and forearm follows radial nerve injury.
E. Loss over the superior lateral shoulder follows axillary nerve injury.
Objective: Examine the organizational structure of the brachial plexus including the spinal cord levels from which the brachial plexus arises. Identify the components of the brachial plexus. Demonstrate the areas of distribution of these motor and sensory nerves. Begin to predict neuromuscular deficits that would result from brachial plexus injuries (such as Erb's palsy and Klumpke's palsy) or peripheral nerves that arise from the brachial plexus.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1953
During a cadaver dissection of the upper limb, the tendon of the long head of the biceps brachii is traced proximally out of the arm and is found lying within a bony groove on the anterior aspect of the proximal humerus. Which of the following bony features is most likely holding the tendon at this point?
A. Anatomical neck
B. Radial groove
C. Intertubercular sulcus
D. Coronoid fossa
E. Surgical neck
Answer: C. Intertubercular sulcus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Proximal humerus landmarks of the arm; Biceps brachii
Why this is correct:
The intertubercular sulcus, also called the bicipital groove, is the groove on the anterior aspect of the proximal humerus, and the lecture identifies it as the groove occupied by the tendon of the long head of the biceps brachii.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The anatomical neck is the constriction next to the head and isn't a groove for a tendon.
B. The radial groove lies on the posterior aspect of the humeral shaft, not on the anterior proximal humerus.
D. The coronoid fossa is a depression at the distal end of the humerus.
E. The surgical neck is the narrowed region distal to the tubercles, not a tendon groove.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1954
A 68-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after falling onto his shoulder. A radiograph shows a fracture line that separates the head of the humerus from the greater and lesser tubercles, leaving the shaft intact. Which of the following bony features has most likely been fractured?
A. Radial groove
B. Deltoid tuberosity
C. Anatomical neck
D. Surgical neck
E. Intertubercular sulcus
Answer: C. Anatomical neck
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Proximal humerus landmarks of the arm
Why this is correct:
The anatomical neck is the line that marks off the head of the humerus from the tubercles, so a fracture that separates the head from the greater and lesser tubercles passes through it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial groove is on the posterior shaft and is nowhere near the head.
B. The deltoid tuberosity is on the lateral aspect of the shaft, well distal to the head.
D. The surgical neck lies distal to both tubercles, so a fracture there would carry the tubercles with the head.
E. The intertubercular sulcus is the groove running between the tubercles, not a plane separating the head from them.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1955
In the anatomy lab a probe is laid along the bicipital groove of an isolated humerus so that students can appreciate its walls. Which of the following pairs of bony features most directly borders this groove?
A. Capitulum and trochlea
B. Medial epicondyle and lateral epicondyle
C. Anatomical neck and surgical neck
D. Greater tubercle and lesser tubercle
E. Head of the humerus and anatomical neck
Answer: D. Greater tubercle and lesser tubercle
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Proximal humerus landmarks of the arm
Why this is correct:
The bicipital groove, also called the intertubercular sulcus, lies on the anterior proximal humerus with the greater tubercle on one side and the lesser tubercle on the other.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The capitulum and trochlea are parts of the distal humeral condyle.
B. The epicondyles are at the distal end of the humerus.
C. The anatomical and surgical necks are both transverse constrictions, not longitudinal walls.
E. The head and anatomical neck sit proximal to the groove and don't form its walls.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1956
During a dissection of the arm, a fascial sheet is traced from the lateral epicondyle of the humerus proximally along the lateral side of the shaft. The sheet ends proximally at a roughened prominence on the lateral surface of the shaft. Which of the following bony features marks the proximal end of this sheet?
A. Olecranon fossa
B. Deltoid tuberosity
C. Surgical neck
D. Medial epicondyle
E. Radial groove
Answer: B. Deltoid tuberosity
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Humeral shaft: deltoid tuberosity and radial groove; Fascial compartments of the arm: brachial fascia and intermuscular septa
Why this is correct:
The lateral intermuscular septum runs between the insertion of the deltoid muscle and the lateral epicondyle of the humerus, and the deltoid inserts on the deltoid tuberosity of the shaft.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The olecranon fossa is a posterior depression at the distal humerus.
C. The surgical neck is proximal to the shaft and lies above the septum's attachment.
D. The medial epicondyle is the distal attachment of the medial intermuscular septum, on the opposite side.
E. The radial groove is a shallow channel on the posterior shaft, not an attachment prominence for the septum.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1957
A student is given a dried humerus at a lab practical station and is told to place a pin in the radial groove, which the lecture also calls the spiral groove. The student turns the bone over several times looking for a shallow channel running obliquely across it, and has to decide which surface to search. Which of the following best describes where this feature should be sought?
A. On the lateral surface just distal to the greater tubercle
B. On the posterior surface of the shaft
C. On the medial border just proximal to the medial epicondyle
D. On the anterior distal surface just proximal to the trochlea
E. On the anterior surface of the proximal shaft
Answer: B. On the posterior surface of the shaft
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Humeral shaft: deltoid tuberosity and radial groove
Why this is correct:
The lecture places the radial (spiral) groove on the shaft of the humerus and labels it on the posterior view.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lateral surface just below the greater tubercle has no labeled groove.
C. The medial border above the medial epicondyle isn't where this groove is labeled.
D. The anterior distal surface just above the trochlea holds the coronoid fossa.
E. The anterior proximal shaft carries the intertubercular sulcus rather than the radial groove.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1958
For a lab practical, a humerus is separated conceptually into a proximal end, a shaft, and a distal end, and students are asked to assign each labeled feature to the correct part. Which of the following features belongs to the shaft?
A. Coronoid fossa
B. Olecranon fossa
C. Capitulum
D. Deltoid tuberosity
E. Greater tubercle
Answer: D. Deltoid tuberosity
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Humeral shaft: deltoid tuberosity and radial groove
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists the deltoid tuberosity and the radial groove as features of the shaft, or diaphysis, of the humerus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The coronoid fossa is a feature of the distal end of the humerus.
B. The olecranon fossa is on the distal end, seen from behind.
C. The capitulum is part of the condyle at the distal end.
E. The greater tubercle is a feature of the proximal end.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

Question 1959
A student working through a lab practical is asked to place a probe into one of the depressions on the anterior surface of the distal end of a dried humerus. A. Trochlear notch
B. Radial fossa
C. Ulnar groove

E. Coronoid fossa
Answer: E. Coronoid fossa
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Distal humerus: condyle, epicondyles, and fossae
Why this is correct:
On the anterior view of the distal humerus the coronoid fossa lies above the trochlea, while the radial fossa lies above the capitulum.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The trochlear notch is a feature of the ulna, not of the humerus.
B. The radial fossa is also anterior but sits above the capitulum, lateral to the coronoid fossa.
C. The ulnar groove is labeled on the posterior view near the medial epicondyle.
D. The olecranon fossa is on the posterior surface of the distal humerus.
Objective: Identify the bony features, articulations, and associated ligaments of the humerus, proximal radius, and ulna. Discuss the anatomical and clinical considerations of fractures of these bones and radial head subluxation.

Question 1963
A student runs a finger distally along the proximal radius starting at its articular end and is asked to name the three labeled features encountered along the way, in order. Which of the following sequences is most likely correct?
A. Radial tuberosity, head of the radius, neck of the radius
B. Radial tuberosity, neck of the radius, head of the radius
C. Head of the radius, neck of the radius, radial tuberosity
D. Head of the radius, radial tuberosity, neck of the radius

Answer: C. Head of the radius, neck of the radius, radial tuberosity
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Proximal radius: head, neck, and radial tuberosity

The lecture labels the proximal radius from its articular end downward as the head, then the neck, then the radial tuberosity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The tuberosity isn't the articular end of the bone.
B. This reverses the sequence and starts at the most distal of the three features.
D. The tuberosity is distal to the neck, so it can't come second.
E. The neck lies below the head, not above it.

Question 1967
During dissection of the elbow region, the ulnar nerve is traced from the arm into the forearm. Behind the elbow it passes posterior to the medial epicondyle of the humerus and then runs immediately medial to a projection of the ulna. Which of the following features does the nerve pass medial to?

Objective: Discuss the organization of the fascial compartments of the arm in terms of functional muscle groups and neurovasculature supplying each compartment.

Question 1969
A first-year student is asked to explain how the arm comes to have an anterior and a posterior compartment, each with its own muscle group and its own nerve and artery. Which of the following best accounts for this division?

Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Fascial compartments of the arm: brachial fascia and intermuscular septa
Why this is correct:
The lecture pairs the brachial fascia with a medial and a lateral intermuscular septum, and it's those septa that separate the anterior compartment from the posterior compartment.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The brachial fascia by itself only surrounds the arm and can't divide it.
B. The fascia over the deltoid is outside the arm's compartments.
D. The bicipital aponeurosis lies at the elbow and roofs the cubital fossa.
E. The antebrachial fascia belongs to the forearm.
Objective: Discuss the organization of the fascial compartments of the arm in terms of functional muscle groups and neurovasculature supplying each compartment.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1977
A 44-year-old man sustains an injury that denervates the entire anterior compartment of the arm while the posterior compartment is spared. Which of the following combinations of actions is most likely to be lost or severely weakened?
A. Extension of the arm at the shoulder, extension of the forearm at the elbow, and pronation
B. Abduction of the arm at the shoulder, flexion of the forearm at the elbow, and pronation
C. Adduction of the arm at the shoulder, extension of the forearm at the elbow, and supination
D. Flexion of the arm at the shoulder, extension of the forearm at the elbow, and pronation
E. Flexion of the arm at the shoulder, flexion of the forearm at the elbow, and supination

Difficulty: Medium

Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Extension of the forearm belongs to the posterior compartment, and pronation isn't an anterior arm action in this lecture.
B. Abduction of the arm isn't listed among the anterior compartment actions.
C. Adduction of the arm at the shoulder is an action of coracobrachialis, but extension of the forearm is posterior.
D. Extension of the forearm and pronation are both outside the anterior compartment group actions.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, functions, and testing procedures of muscles found in the anterior arm. Predict functional consequences of loss of action of these muscles.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1980
During a dissection of the shoulder and arm, the two heads of the biceps brachii are separated from one another and each is traced proximally out of the arm to its own attachment on the scapula. The student is asked to record which head goes to which bony site before the specimen is reassembled. Which of the following best matches the heads to their proximal attachments?
A. Short head from the coracoid process, long head from the middle third of the humerus
B. Short head from the supraglenoid tubercle, long head from the radial tuberosity
C. Short head from the radial tuberosity, long head from the coracoid process
D. Short head from the supraglenoid tubercle, long head from the coracoid process
E. Short head from the coracoid process, long head from the supraglenoid tubercle
Answer: E. Short head from the coracoid process, long head from the supraglenoid tubercle
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Biceps brachii
Why this is correct:
The lecture gives the short head of the biceps brachii the coracoid process of the scapula and the long head the supraglenoid tubercle of the scapula.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The middle third of the humeral diaphysis is the distal attachment of coracobrachialis.

C. The radial tuberosity is the distal attachment of the muscle, not a head of origin.
D. This reverses the two heads.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, functions, and testing procedures of muscles found in the anterior arm. Predict functional consequences of loss of action of these muscles.

Question 1981
A 35-year-old man is taken to the operating room for repair of an avulsed distal biceps brachii tendon after he felt a tearing sensation while lifting a heavy crate. The surgeon exposes the proximal forearm and prepares to reattach the retracted tendon to the bony site from which it was torn. Which of the following sites is most likely used?
A. Ulnar tuberosity
B. Olecranon process
C. Head of the radius
D. Radial tuberosity
E. Coronoid process
Answer: D. Radial tuberosity
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Biceps brachii; Proximal radius: head, neck, and radial tuberosity
Why this is correct:
The distal attachment of the biceps brachii is the radial tuberosity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ulnar tuberosity, with the coronoid process, receives the brachialis.
B. The olecranon process is the posterior projection of the proximal ulna.
C. The head of the radius is the articular proximal end of that bone.
E. The coronoid process is the other brachialis attachment.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, functions, and testing procedures of muscles found in the anterior arm. Predict functional consequences of loss of action of these muscles.

Question 1982
In the dissection lab, a fibrous expansion is seen leaving the distal biceps brachii tendon and passing medially across the front of the elbow, where it blends with the fascia of the forearm. Which of the following structures is this expansion?

The lecture labels the bicipital aponeurosis alongside the biceps brachii tendon and lists it as part of the superficial roof of the cubital fossa with the brachial and antebrachial fascia.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The medial intermuscular septum passes inward to the medial side of the humerus.
C. The antebrachial fascia is the fascia of the forearm that the aponeurosis blends with.

Question 1983
A physician prepares to elicit the bicipital myotatic reflex in a 42-year-old woman being evaluated for arm weakness. Which of the following describes the limb position that should be used before the tendon is struck?
A. Relaxed, passively supinated, and fully flexed at the elbow
B. Relaxed, in the anatomical position, and abducted at the shoulder
C. Actively contracted, supinated, and partially flexed at the elbow
D. Relaxed, passively pronated, and partially extended at the elbow
E. Actively contracted, pronated, and fully extended at the elbow
Answer: D. Relaxed, passively pronated, and partially extended at the elbow
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Bicipital myotatic reflex
Why this is correct:
The lecture specifies that the upper limb is relaxed, passively pronated, and partially extended at the elbow when the bicipital myotatic reflex is tested.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The forearm is pronated rather than supinated, and the elbow is partially extended rather than fully flexed.
B. Shoulder abduction has no part in the described technique.
C. Active contraction again defeats the test, and the forearm should be pronated.

Question 1984

B. The radial nerve and the C7 and C8 cord levels

Why the other answers are wrong:

C. The ulnar nerve also gives no branches in the arm.

Question 1985
A clinician positions a patient's relaxed, pronated, partially extended limb and then places a thumb firmly over a tendon before striking it with a reflex hammer. Which of the following is the correct site for the thumb?
A. Over the olecranon process at the back of the elbow
B. Over the medial epicondyle of the humerus
C. Over the bicipital groove at the front of the shoulder
D. Over the tendon of the biceps brachii in the cubital fossa

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Bicipital myotatic reflex; Contents of the cubital fossa
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the thumb is placed firmly over the biceps brachii tendon at the cubital fossa, which is where that tendon lies as the most lateral of the TAN structures.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The olecranon is the attachment of the triceps brachii, which isn't being tested.
B. The medial epicondyle is a bony landmark related to the ulnar nerve.
C. The bicipital groove at the shoulder holds the long head tendon but isn't the site used for this reflex.

Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1986
A 46-year-old man comes to the clinic because of several months of anterior shoulder pain that worsens with overhead work. On examination there's point tenderness over a groove on the anterior aspect of the proximal humerus, and shoulder motion is otherwise preserved. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?
A. Strain of the coracobrachialis near its origin
B. Rupture of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon
C. Strain of the brachialis near its distal attachment
D. Rupture of the distal biceps brachii tendon
E. Tendinopathy of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon
Answer: E. Tendinopathy of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Biceps tendinopathy
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes biceps tendinopathy as pain and tenderness in the tendon of the long head of the biceps brachii, presenting as anterior shoulder pain with point tenderness of the bicipital groove.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Coracobrachialis attaches to the coracoid process, not within the bicipital groove.
B. A rupture of the long head produces a visible deformity of the arm rather than isolated point tenderness.
C. The brachialis attaches to the ulna at the elbow and wouldn't cause anterior shoulder tenderness.
D. A distal tendon problem would localize to the elbow, not to the anterior shoulder.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: biceps tendinopathy and rupture of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1987
Ultrasound of the anterior shoulder in a 52-year-old woman with activity-related pain shows an inhomogeneous tendon with a thickened surrounding sheath, and Doppler shows increased vascularity in the same area. Which of the following best explains these findings?
A. Inflammation of the tendon and its surrounding sheath
B. Displacement of the tendon out of its bony groove
C. Complete discontinuity of the tendon with retraction of the muscle
D. Calcification of the tendon with loss of blood flow
E. Replacement of the tendon by a fluid-filled bursa
Answer: A. Inflammation of the tendon and its surrounding sheath
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Biceps tendinopathy
Why this is correct:
The lecture presents this picture as a case of tenosynovitis of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon, with an inhomogeneous tendon, a thickened sheath, and increased vascularity on Doppler due to inflammation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Displacement out of the groove isn't what the lecture attributes these findings to.
C. A complete tear would show a discontinuous tendon rather than a thickened, inflamed one.
D. Increased Doppler signal indicates more blood flow, not loss of it.
E. The lecture doesn't describe bursal replacement of the tendon in this case.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: biceps tendinopathy and rupture of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon.

Question 1989
A 58-year-old man with a long history of anterior shoulder pain feels a sudden pop while lifting a heavy box. Over the next day a soft bulge appears in the lower part of the front of his arm, and the contour of the upper arm looks flattened. Which of the following is the most likely diagnosis?

B. Rupture of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon

Question 1991
A 61-year-old man sustains a rupture of the long head of the biceps brachii tendon after years of shoulder symptoms, with no history of a single major injury. Which of the following best explains why the tendon failed?
A. Repeated traction on the tendon by the bicipital aponeurosis at the elbow
B. Progressive compression of the tendon by the medial intermuscular septum
C. Chronic wear of the tendon within the bicipital groove after prolonged tendinitis

Difficulty: Medium

The lecture states that rupture of the long head results from wear and tear of the tendon in the bicipital groove following prolonged tendinitis.
Why the other answers are wrong:

B. The medial intermuscular septum is related to the ulnar nerve, not to this tendon.

E. The radial tuberosity is the distal attachment and isn't the site the lecture gives for this rupture.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 1998
A 30-year-old man is evaluated after a penetrating wound high in the front of the arm. He has weakness of elbow flexion and of supination, together with reduced sensation over the lateral aspect of the forearm. Which of the following nerves is most likely injured?
A. Radial nerve
B. Musculocutaneous nerve
C. Lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm
D. Ulnar nerve
E. Median nerve
Answer: B. Musculocutaneous nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Musculocutaneous nerve in the arm; Anterior compartment muscle group: actions and innervation
Why this is correct:
The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior compartment muscles that flex the forearm and supinate, and it continues as the lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm to the skin of the lateral forearm, so a single lesion explains both the motor and the sensory loss.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve supplies the posterior compartment, so extension rather than flexion would be lost.
C. That nerve is the sensory continuation of the musculocutaneous nerve, so a lesion of it alone wouldn't cause weakness.
D. The ulnar nerve also gives no branches in the arm.
E. The median nerve gives no branches in the arm.
Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

A 26-year-old man sustains a deep laceration to the back of the arm. He subsequently has weakness of extension of the forearm and of the wrist and fingers, while elbow flexion and supination are intact. Which of the following nerves is most likely injured?
A. Ulnar nerve
B. Radial nerve
C. Musculocutaneous nerve
D. Lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm
E. Median nerve
Answer: B. Radial nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Radial nerve in the arm; Neurovasculature of the posterior compartment of the arm
Why this is correct:
The radial nerve enters the posterior compartment of the arm and supplies all of the muscles of the posterior compartment of both the arm and the forearm, so a posterior arm injury produces weak extension while the anterior compartment functions are spared.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ulnar nerve passes through the anterior compartment and gives no branches in the arm.
C. A musculocutaneous injury would weaken elbow flexion and supination instead.
D. That nerve is sensory and wouldn't produce weakness.
E. The median nerve travels with the brachial artery in the anterior compartment and gives no branches in the arm.
Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2003
The radial nerve is followed distally through the posterior compartment of the arm during a dissection, and the student notes that it ends up on the front of the lateral side of the elbow, where it divides. To reach that position the nerve has to pass through a structure attached along the lateral side of the humerus. Which of the following structures must it pass through to reach that position?
A. Lateral intermuscular septum
B. Bicipital aponeurosis
C. Coracobrachialis muscle
D. Medial intermuscular septum
E. Antebrachial fascia
Answer: A. Lateral intermuscular septum
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Radial nerve in the arm; Fascial compartments of the arm: brachial fascia and intermuscular septa
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the radial nerve pierces the lateral intermuscular septum as it travels distally.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The bicipital aponeurosis roofs the cubital fossa and isn't pierced by this nerve.
C. Coracobrachialis is pierced by the musculocutaneous nerve.
D. The medial intermuscular septum is pierced by the ulnar nerve.
E. The antebrachial fascia belongs to the forearm.
Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2004
During dissection of the front of the arm, a nerve is found lying alongside the brachial artery through the whole length of the arm, giving off no branches along the way, and is followed with the artery toward the cubital fossa. Which of the following nerves is being followed?
A. Radial nerve
B. Musculocutaneous nerve
C. Ulnar nerve
D. Median nerve
E. Lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm
Answer: D. Median nerve
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Median and ulnar nerves in the arm; Contents of the cubital fossa
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the median nerve has no branches in the axilla or arm and travels with the brachial artery to the cubital fossa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve runs in the posterior compartment.
B. The musculocutaneous nerve pierces coracobrachialis and supplies the anterior compartment muscles.
C. The ulnar nerve also gives no branches in the arm but leaves the anterior compartment through the medial intermuscular septum rather than staying with the artery.
E. That nerve is the sensory continuation of the musculocutaneous nerve in the forearm.
Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2005
A nerve that arises in the anterior compartment of the arm and supplies no muscle there is traced distally and is seen to leave that compartment by passing through the medial intermuscular septum. Which of the following nerves is most likely being traced?
A. Musculocutaneous nerve
B. Lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm
C. Radial nerve
D. Ulnar nerve
E. Median nerve
Answer: D. Ulnar nerve
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Median and ulnar nerves in the arm; Fascial compartments of the arm: brachial fascia and intermuscular septa
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the ulnar nerve arises in the anterior compartment of the arm, gives no branches in the axilla or arm, and pierces the medial intermuscular septum.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior compartment muscles.
B. That nerve is a sensory branch in the forearm.
C. The radial nerve pierces the lateral intermuscular septum instead.
E. The median nerve also gives no branches in the arm but stays with the brachial artery into the cubital fossa.
Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2006
A student preparing for a practical examination is asked what the median and ulnar nerves have in common as they pass through the arm, given that both are branches of the brachial plexus that enter the anterior compartment of the arm and then continue on into the forearm. Which of the following statements best characterizes both nerves in this region?
A. Neither gives any branches in the axilla or the arm
B. Both pierce the medial intermuscular septum in the arm
C. Both end by dividing into superficial and deep branches at the elbow
D. Both run with the profunda brachii artery in the posterior compartment
E. Both supply muscles of the anterior compartment of the arm
Answer: A. Neither gives any branches in the axilla or the arm
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Median and ulnar nerves in the arm
Why this is correct:
The lecture states for each of these nerves that it has no branches in the axilla or arm, which is what sets them apart from the musculocutaneous and radial nerves in this region.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Only the ulnar nerve pierces the medial intermuscular septum.
C. The radial nerve is the one that divides into superficial and deep branches.
D. The profunda brachii artery runs with the radial nerve in the posterior compartment.
E. Neither nerve supplies muscles in the arm, which is the point of the comparison.
Objective: Describe the course and targets (i.e., muscular and cutaneous innervation) of the musculocutaneous, median, and ulnar nerves within the arm. Describe common sites of injury to neural structures and subsequent clinical manifestations. Describe the bicipital myotatic reflex.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2007
During dissection the axillary artery is traced distally out of the axilla, and at a muscular landmark the vessel takes a new name as it enters the arm. Which of the following marks this transition?
A. Medial epicondyle of the humerus
B. Inferior border of the teres major muscle
C. Proximal edge of the cubital fossa
D. Lateral border of the first rib
E. Inferior border of the pectoralis minor muscle
Answer: B. Inferior border of the teres major muscle
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Brachial artery
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the brachial artery is the continuation of the axillary artery at the inferior border of the teres major muscle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The medial epicondyle is a landmark for the ulnar collateral arteries and the ulnar nerve.
C. The brachial artery ends in the cubital fossa by dividing rather than by changing name there.
D. The lateral border of the first rib is where the subclavian artery becomes the axillary artery.
E. Pectoralis minor is the landmark used to divide the axillary artery into parts, not to rename it.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2008
A vascular surgeon exposes the brachial artery at the front of the elbow during repair of a laceration and follows the vessel distally to the point where it ends by dividing into the two arteries that continue into the forearm. The assistant is asked to name that pair before any clamps are applied. Which of the following pairs is most likely found?
A. Radial artery and ulnar artery
B. Axillary artery and profunda brachii artery
C. Radial collateral artery and middle collateral artery
D. Profunda brachii artery and radial collateral artery
E. Superior ulnar collateral artery and inferior ulnar collateral artery
Answer: A. Radial artery and ulnar artery
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Brachial artery
Why this is correct:
The lecture marks the radial and ulnar arteries as the terminal branches of the brachial artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The axillary artery is proximal to the brachial artery rather than a terminal branch of it.
C. These two are branches of the profunda brachii artery.
D. The profunda brachii is a branch along the arm, and the radial collateral comes off it.
E. These two are collateral branches given off by the brachial artery along the arm.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2009

A. Ulnar artery
B. Superior ulnar collateral artery
C. Radial collateral artery
D. Inferior ulnar collateral artery
E. Radial artery
Answer: C. Radial collateral artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Brachial artery; Neurovasculature of the posterior compartment of the arm
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists the radial collateral artery and the middle collateral artery as the branches of the profunda brachii artery, which is also called the deep artery of the arm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ulnar artery is a terminal branch of the brachial artery.
B. The superior ulnar collateral artery comes directly off the brachial artery.
D. The inferior ulnar collateral artery also comes directly off the brachial artery.
E. The radial artery is the other terminal branch of the brachial artery.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

Question 2010
During dissection of the medial side of the arm, an artery is found accompanying the ulnar nerve, and the two structures pass together behind the medial epicondyle of the humerus. Which of the following arteries is most likely being followed?
A. Radial collateral artery
