B. The inferior ulnar collateral artery passes anterior to the medial epicondyle.
C. The profunda brachii artery runs with the radial nerve, not the ulnar nerve.
D. The middle collateral artery is the other profunda brachii branch, in the posterior compartment.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2011
Two collateral branches of the brachial artery are traced down the medial side of the arm in a dissection and are seen to approach the medial epicondyle of the humerus from opposite sides, one passing in front of it and the other passing behind it alongside a nerve. The student must label each vessel correctly. Which of the following vessels passes anterior to that epicondyle?
A. Radial collateral artery
B. Inferior ulnar collateral artery
C. Middle collateral artery
D. Superior ulnar collateral artery
E. Profunda brachii artery
Answer: B. Inferior ulnar collateral artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Ulnar collateral arteries and their relation to the medial epicondyle
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the inferior ulnar collateral artery passes anterior to the medial epicondyle of the humerus, while the superior ulnar collateral artery passes posterior to it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial collateral artery is the other profunda brachii branch and descends laterally.
C. The middle collateral artery is a profunda brachii branch on the posterior aspect of the arm.
D. The superior ulnar collateral artery passes behind the epicondyle with the ulnar nerve.
E. The profunda brachii artery is the parent of the two collateral arteries on the lateral and posterior sides.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2012
A 17-year-old boy falls on an outstretched hand, and a radiograph shows a displaced fracture of the medial epicondyle of the humerus. Which of the following pairs of structures passing behind that epicondyle is most at risk?
A. Median nerve and inferior ulnar collateral artery
B. Ulnar nerve and superior ulnar collateral artery
C. Radial nerve and middle collateral artery
D. Musculocutaneous nerve and brachial artery
E. Radial nerve and radial collateral artery
Answer: B. Ulnar nerve and superior ulnar collateral artery
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Ulnar collateral arteries and their relation to the medial epicondyle; Median and ulnar nerves in the arm
Why this is correct:
The lecture places both the ulnar nerve and the superior ulnar collateral artery posterior to the medial epicondyle of the humerus, since the artery travels with that nerve.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The median nerve runs with the brachial artery anteriorly, and the inferior ulnar collateral artery passes in front of the epicondyle.
C. The middle collateral artery runs with the radial nerve in the posterior compartment.
D. The musculocutaneous nerve and brachial artery lie in the anterior compartment.
E. The radial nerve and radial collateral artery are on the lateral side of the arm.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2013
A patient holds the elbow in full flexion for a prolonged period, which narrows the brachial artery where it crosses the front of the joint, yet the forearm stays well perfused throughout. Which of the following best explains this finding?
A. The profunda brachii artery enlarges to replace the radial and ulnar arteries
B. The superficial veins of the arm take over arterial supply to the forearm
C. The brachial veins reverse their flow to supply the forearm tissues
D. A network of arteries around the elbow carries blood past the narrowed segment
E. The bicipital aponeurosis protects the artery from any narrowing in flexion
Answer: D. A network of arteries around the elbow carries blood past the narrowed segment
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Elbow anastomosis and collateral circulation
Why this is correct:
The lecture describes the elbow anastomosis as a network of arteries surrounding the elbow joint that ensures continuous perfusion regardless of the position of the joint or if a main artery is compressed or blocked.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lecture doesn't describe the profunda brachii replacing the terminal branches.
B. Superficial veins drain the limb rather than perfuse it.
C. Veins return blood toward the heart and don't supply tissue with arterial blood.
E. The bicipital aponeurosis forms part of the roof of the cubital fossa and isn't described as protecting against narrowing.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2014
A student preparing a summary of the blood supply of the arm is asked which vessels contribute to the arterial network surrounding the elbow joint, the network that keeps the forearm perfused when the main vessel is compressed. The answer should include only the branches that descend toward the joint rather than the main trunks themselves. Which of the following sets is most likely correct?
A. Cephalic, basilic, median cubital, and brachial vessels
B. Profunda brachii, axillary, subclavian, and brachial arteries
C. Superior ulnar collateral, inferior ulnar collateral, radial, and ulnar arteries
D. Radial collateral, middle collateral, superior ulnar collateral, and inferior ulnar collateral arteries
E. Radial, ulnar, axillary, and brachial arteries
Answer: D. Radial collateral, middle collateral, superior ulnar collateral, and inferior ulnar collateral arteries
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Elbow anastomosis and collateral circulation; Brachial artery
Why this is correct:
The lecture's collateral vessels of the arm are the radial and middle collateral branches of the profunda brachii plus the superior and inferior ulnar collateral branches of the brachial artery, and these form the network around the elbow joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. These are veins, which don't form an arterial anastomosis.
B. The axillary and subclavian arteries lie far proximal to the elbow.
C. The radial and ulnar arteries are the terminal branches the collaterals rejoin rather than collaterals themselves.
E. These are the main trunks of the limb rather than the collateral vessels that bypass the joint.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2015
A 38-year-old woman sustains a penetrating injury to the distal arm, and the brachial artery is ligated just above the elbow. The hand remains warm with a palpable pulse at the wrist. Which of the following best explains the preserved flow?
A. Collateral vessels around the elbow rejoin the arteries of the forearm beyond the ligation
B. The median cubital vein becomes an arterial channel after the ligation
C. The ulnar nerve carries vasodilator fibers that open new channels in the forearm
D. The brachial veins deliver oxygenated blood to the forearm after the ligation
E. The bicipital aponeurosis diverts flow from the cubital fossa into the forearm
Answer: A. Collateral vessels around the elbow rejoin the arteries of the forearm beyond the ligation
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Elbow anastomosis and collateral circulation; Ulnar collateral arteries and their relation to the medial epicondyle
Why this is correct:
The lecture explains that the elbow anastomosis keeps the limb perfused when a main artery is compressed or blocked, because the collateral arteries bypass the obstructed segment and rejoin the circulation distal to it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The median cubital vein is a superficial vein and doesn't carry arterial blood.
C. The lecture doesn't attribute collateral flow to vasodilator fibers in the ulnar nerve.
D. Veins can't take over arterial supply.
E. The bicipital aponeurosis is a fascial structure, not a vascular channel.
Objective: Trace the courses of the major arteries, superficial and deep veins, and lymphatics within the arm. Define collateral circulation and describe why it is important at joints in general and specifically at the elbow. Identify sites where a pulse rate can be measured.

The lecture lists the cephalic, basilic, and median cubital veins as the superficial veins of the arm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ulnar veins are the other deep forearm veins that join to form the brachial veins.
C. The radial veins are deep veins of the forearm that help form the brachial veins.
D. The axillary vein is a deep vein formed where the brachial and basilic veins merge.
E. The brachial veins are deep veins that accompany the brachial artery.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2022
A surgeon exploring the cubital fossa of a 48-year-old woman after a penetrating injury to the front of the elbow needs to widen the exposure. The forearm muscle forming the medial boundary of the fossa is retracted so that the structures lying inside the space can be inspected directly. Which of the following muscles is being retracted?
A. Pronator teres
B. Brachialis
C. Biceps brachii
D. Brachioradialis
E. Supinator
Answer: A. Pronator teres
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Boundaries of the cubital fossa
Why this is correct:
The lecture gives the pronator teres muscle as the medial boundary of the cubital fossa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Brachialis contributes to the floor of the fossa.
C. The biceps brachii contributes its tendon as a content of the fossa rather than a boundary.
D. Brachioradialis forms the lateral boundary.
E. Supinator makes up the rest of the floor.
Objective: Describe the boundaries, contents, and relationships of structures found in the cubital fossa.

Question 2023
After the contents of the cubital fossa have been removed in a dissection, a student is asked to clean and then name the two muscles that make up the floor of the space, noting that one of them descends from the arm and the other belongs to the forearm. A. Brachioradialis and supinator
B. Pronator teres and brachioradialis

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Boundaries of the cubital fossa

The lecture gives the brachialis and supinator muscles as the deep boundary, or floor, of the cubital fossa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Brachioradialis is the lateral boundary, so only the supinator here belongs to the floor.
B. These two muscles form the medial and lateral boundaries rather than the floor.
C. The biceps brachii contributes a tendon as a content of the fossa.
D. Pronator teres is the medial boundary, so only the brachialis here belongs to the floor.
Objective: Describe the boundaries, contents, and relationships of structures found in the cubital fossa.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2024
A student begins a dissection of the front of the elbow by incising the layer that covers the cubital fossa from the surface, and notes while cutting that this layer is continuous above with the deep fascia of the arm and below with the deep fascia of the forearm. Which of the following best describes the layer being divided?
A. The tendon of the biceps brachii and the brachialis muscle
B. The lateral intermuscular septum together with the brachial fascia
C. Brachial fascia alone, without any tendinous reinforcement
D. The medial intermuscular septum together with the antebrachial fascia
E. Brachial and antebrachial fascia reinforced by the bicipital aponeurosis
Answer: E. Brachial and antebrachial fascia reinforced by the bicipital aponeurosis
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Boundaries of the cubital fossa; Biceps brachii
Why this is correct:
The lecture gives the superficial boundary, or roof, of the cubital fossa as the brachial and antebrachial fascia together with the bicipital aponeurosis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The biceps tendon is a content of the fossa and the brachialis is part of its floor.
B. The lateral intermuscular septum lies in the arm and doesn't roof the fossa.
C. The lecture includes the bicipital aponeurosis in the roof, so the fascia isn't acting alone.
D. The medial intermuscular septum is also a structure of the arm.
Objective: Describe the boundaries, contents, and relationships of structures found in the cubital fossa.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2025
A student is asked to list the three main contents of the cubital fossa in the order in which a probe would meet them if it were moved across the fossa from the lateral side toward the medial side, using the three letter mnemonic given in the lecture. Which of the following sequences is most likely correct?
A. Median nerve, tendon of the biceps brachii, brachial artery
B. Tendon of the biceps brachii, median nerve, brachial artery
C. Median nerve, brachial artery, tendon of the biceps brachii
D. Tendon of the biceps brachii, brachial artery, median nerve
E. Brachial artery, median nerve, tendon of the biceps brachii
Answer: D. Tendon of the biceps brachii, brachial artery, median nerve
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Contents of the cubital fossa
Why this is correct:
The lecture uses the mnemonic TAN for the contents of the cubital fossa from lateral to medial, standing for tendon of the biceps brachii muscle, brachial artery, and median nerve.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The tendon is the most lateral of the three.
B. The median nerve is the most medial of the three, not the middle one.
C. This is the medial to lateral order, which reverses the mnemonic.
E. The brachial artery sits between the tendon and the nerve rather than most laterally.
Objective: Describe the boundaries, contents, and relationships of structures found in the cubital fossa.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2026
During a procedure in the cubital fossa of a 52-year-old man, the brachial artery is identified and controlled with a vessel loop. The operator is warned to avoid the structure lying immediately medial to the artery before any deeper dissection is carried out toward the floor of the fossa. Which of the following structures is this?
A. Median nerve
B. Tendon of the biceps brachii
C. Radial nerve
D. Ulnar nerve
E. Bicipital aponeurosis
Answer: A. Median nerve
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Contents of the cubital fossa; Median and ulnar nerves in the arm
Why this is correct:
In the TAN arrangement from lateral to medial, the median nerve lies medial to the brachial artery in the cubital fossa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The tendon of the biceps brachii lies lateral to the artery.
C. The radial nerve lies at the lateral margin of the fossa.
D. The ulnar nerve leaves the anterior compartment higher up and passes behind the medial epicondyle.
E. The bicipital aponeurosis is part of the roof rather than a structure beside the artery.
Objective: Describe the boundaries, contents, and relationships of structures found in the cubital fossa.

MSK / Week 14 / Anterior Arm and Cubital Fossa
Question 2027
At the lateral margin of the cubital fossa, beyond the three structures named by the TAN mnemonic, a dissector identifies a further nerve. As it passes the elbow this nerve promptly separates into a superficial and a deep branch, each continuing into the forearm. Which of the following nerves has been found?
A. Ulnar nerve
B. Musculocutaneous nerve
C. Lateral cutaneous nerve of the forearm
D. Radial nerve
E. Median nerve
Answer: D. Radial nerve
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Contents of the cubital fossa; Radial nerve in the arm
Why this is correct:
The lecture places the radial nerve with its superficial and deep branches at the cubital fossa, alongside the TAN structures.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The ulnar nerve passes behind the medial epicondyle and doesn't enter the fossa.
B. The musculocutaneous nerve runs between the biceps brachii and brachialis before becoming cutaneous.
C. That nerve is the sensory continuation of the musculocutaneous nerve into the forearm.
E. The median nerve is the most medial of the TAN structures and doesn't divide this way here.
Objective: Describe the boundaries, contents, and relationships of structures found in the cubital fossa.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2028
The shoulder complex positions the hand for both gross and skilled tasks and at the same time carries load from the upper extremity into the trunk. Which of the following pairs of properties is most critical for the complex to achieve both of those roles?
A. Stability and ligamentous laxity
B. Stability and congruency
C. Mobility and stability
D. Mobility and ligamentous laxity
E. Mobility and congruency
Answer: C. Mobility and stability
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Purpose of the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
The lecture frames the shoulder complex around two requirements. It has to be mobile enough to place the hand almost anywhere in space, and stable enough to serve as a strong base while loads pass through it.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Ligamentous laxity isn't listed as a requirement, and stability alone doesn't supply mobility.
B. Stability alone with congruency would give a strong base but not the range the hand needs.
D. Ligamentous laxity isn't listed as a requirement of the complex.
E. Congruency isn't one of the two stated requirements, and the glenohumeral joint is deliberately incongruent.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2029
A 24-year-old man is lifting a loaded barbell overhead when his shoulder gives way. In working through the injury, the examiner considers how force generated in the upper extremity normally reaches the trunk. Which of the following best describes this function of the shoulder complex?
A. It absorbs loads within the upper extremity so the trunk stays unloaded
B. It distributes loads from the upper extremity into the cervical spine alone
C. It redirects loads from the upper extremity into the thoracic cage muscles
D. It transmits loads from the upper extremity to the axial skeleton
E. It transmits loads from the axial skeleton to the upper extremity
Answer: D. It transmits loads from the upper extremity to the axial skeleton
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Purpose of the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
One of the two stated purposes of the shoulder complex is to transmit loads from the upper extremity to the axial skeleton, which is why the complex has to be strong as well as mobile.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The complex transfers load rather than absorbing it so the trunk is spared.
B. The load reaches the axial skeleton as a whole, not the cervical spine alone.
C. The thoracic cage muscles aren't the destination named for this load transfer.
E. The direction is reversed, since the load being carried is generated in the limb.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2030
A 58-year-old woman has a stiff, painful shoulder after several weeks in a sling. Her grip strength and finger movements are normal, but she can't bring her hand to her mouth or reach behind her back. Which of the following functions of the shoulder complex has been most directly lost?
A. Providing the intrinsic force needed for the hand's pinch and grip
B. Providing a mobile base that positions the hand for its gross and skilled tasks
C. Providing the vascular supply needed for sustained activity of the hand
D. Providing the sensory feedback needed for the hand's skilled movements
E. Providing a rigid base that holds the hand in a single working position
Answer: B. Providing a mobile base that positions the hand for its gross and skilled tasks
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Purpose of the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
The complex exists to give the hand a very mobile yet strong base so the hand can carry out its intricate gross and skilled functions. When the shoulder stiffens, the hand itself still works but can no longer be placed where it's needed.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pinch and grip force comes from the hand and forearm, not from the shoulder complex.
C. Vascular supply to the hand isn't the function the complex is described as serving.
D. The complex is described in terms of mobility and stability, not sensory feedback for the hand.
E. A rigid base is the opposite of what the complex provides.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2031
During a physical examination of the shoulder, the clinician follows a subcutaneous bone with a fingertip from the midline of the sternum laterally to the point of the shoulder, feeling it along its whole length without losing contact. Which of the following structures is most likely being palpated?
A. Spine of the scapula
B. Acromion
C. Shaft of the humerus
D. Clavicle
E. Coracoid process
Answer: D. Clavicle
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Clavicle in the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
The clavicle runs from the manubrium to the acromion and is visible and palpable along its entire length, which is unusual for a bone of the shoulder.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The spine of the scapula is palpable posteriorly and doesn't reach the sternum.
B. The acromion is a short lateral process and can't be followed to the midline.
C. The humeral shaft lies in the arm, distal to the point of the shoulder.
E. The coracoid process is a small hook on the anterior scapula, not a bone spanning to the sternum.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2032
A 19-year-old football player is examined after a blow to the shoulder, and the clinician palpates both ends of the clavicle in turn, checking each articulation for tenderness and step-off. Which of the following pairs of joints is being examined?
A. Sternoclavicular and glenohumeral
B. Sternoclavicular and acromioclavicular
C. Acromioclavicular and scapulothoracic
D. Acromioclavicular and glenohumeral
E. Sternoclavicular and scapulothoracic
Answer: B. Sternoclavicular and acromioclavicular
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Clavicle in the shoulder complex; Sternoclavicular joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The medial end of the clavicle articulates with the manubrium at the sternoclavicular joint and the lateral end articulates with the acromion at the acromioclavicular joint, so palpating both ends examines both joints.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The glenohumeral joint is between the humeral head and the glenoid fossa, not at an end of the clavicle.
C. Neither pairing is complete, since the scapulothoracic interface doesn't involve the clavicle directly.
D. Only one of these two involves the clavicle.
E. The scapulothoracic interface is the scapula moving on the thorax and has no clavicular end.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2033
A 7-year-old boy is brought to the emergency department after falling onto his shoulder. Before any imaging is obtained, the clinician detects a clear step-off deformity in the bone simply by running a finger along it. Which of the following properties of this bone most directly allows that?
A. It's enclosed within the fibers of the pectoralis major
B. It's surrounded by a thick layer of subcutaneous fat
C. It's visible and palpable along its entire length
D. It lies deep to the trapezius along its medial half
E. It's covered by the deltoid along its lateral half
Answer: C. It's visible and palpable along its entire length
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Clavicle in the shoulder complex; Clavicle fracture
Why this is correct:
The clavicle is described as visible and palpable along its entire length, so a displaced fracture anywhere along it can usually be felt directly through the skin.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The clavicle isn't enclosed within pectoralis major.
B. Overlying fat would blunt the contour rather than sharpen it.
D. Lying deep to a muscle would make palpation harder, not easier.
E. Muscle covering the bone would hide a step-off rather than reveal it.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2034
A 16-year-old boy is brought to the emergency department after falling onto an outstretched hand during a soccer match. A radiograph shows a fractured clavicle, broken through the weakest part of the bone. Which of the following sites does the fracture most likely involve?
A. The inferior surface at the subclavian groove
B. The sternal end at the sternoclavicular joint
C. Junction of the middle and lateral thirds
D. The acromial end at the acromioclavicular joint
E. Junction of the medial and middle thirds
Answer: C. Junction of the middle and lateral thirds
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Clavicle fracture
Why this is correct:
The clavicle is commonly fractured from an indirect force transmitted through a fall on an outstretched hand or from a direct fall on the shoulder. The typed notes locate its weakest part at the junction of its middle and lateral thirds. The slide phrases the same point as most common at the middle third, so be ready for either wording.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The subclavian groove is a surface marking, not the usual fracture site.
B. The sternoclavicular joint is strongly bound and usually survives when the bone fails.
D. The acromial end isn't named as the weakest part of the clavicle.
E. The medial end of the bone is thicker and isn't the described weak point.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2035
A 22-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after falling directly onto his shoulder. A radiograph confirms a fractured clavicle, and on inspection the affected shoulder sits visibly lower than the opposite side. Which of the following best explains the dropped shoulder?
A. Deltoid pulls the medial fragment down toward the thoracic wall
B. Sternocleidomastoid pulls the lateral fragment down toward the thorax
C. Trapezius can't hold the lateral fragment up against the weight of the limb
D. Subclavius depresses both fragments down against the first rib
E. Pectoralis major lifts the medial fragment above the lateral fragment
Answer: C. Trapezius can't hold the lateral fragment up against the weight of the limb
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Clavicle fracture; Clavicle in the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
Once the clavicle is broken, the trapezius is unable to hold up the lateral fragment against the weight of the upper extremity, so the shoulder drops.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Deltoid attaches to the lateral third and the acromion, not the medial fragment.
B. Sternocleidomastoid acts on the medial fragment and elevates it rather than pulling the lateral fragment down.
D. Subclavius is small and isn't the reason the shoulder drops after a fracture.
E. Pectoralis major doesn't lift the medial fragment, and that wouldn't lower the shoulder.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2036
A 30-year-old woman sustains a clavicular fracture in a bicycle crash. On the radiograph the fragment closest to the midline is clearly displaced superiorly relative to the other fragment. Which of the following muscles most directly produces that displacement?
A. Pectoralis major
B. Subclavius
C. Trapezius
D. Deltoid
E. Sternocleidomastoid
Answer: E. Sternocleidomastoid
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Clavicle fracture
Why this is correct:
After a clavicular fracture the sternocleidomastoid elevates the medial fragment of bone, which is why the fragment nearer the midline rides superiorly.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pectoralis major is one of the adductors that may pull the lateral fragment medially.
B. Subclavius depresses the clavicle and doesn't elevate the medial fragment.
C. Trapezius acts on the lateral fragment, and its failure to hold it up is what drops the shoulder.
D. Deltoid attaches laterally and doesn't move the medial fragment upward.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2037
During dissection of the shoulder, a tendon is traced to a small projection on the scapula located just superior to the glenoid cavity. Which of the following muscles most likely gives rise to that tendon?
A. Long head of triceps brachii
B. Long head of biceps brachii
C. Subscapularis
D. Supraspinatus
E. Infraspinatus
Answer: B. Long head of biceps brachii
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The supraglenoid tubercle is the small projection superior to the glenoid cavity, and it's the origin of the long head of biceps brachii.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The long head of triceps brachii arises from the infraglenoid tubercle, below the glenoid cavity.
C. Subscapularis arises from the subscapular fossa on the anterior surface of the scapula.
D. Supraspinatus arises from the supraspinous fossa on the posterior scapula.
E. Infraspinatus arises from the infraspinous fossa on the posterior scapula.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2038
During dissection of the posterior scapula, a muscle is found filling the smaller of the two fossae separated by the scapular spine, lying above that ridge. Which of the following muscles is most likely being examined?
A. Infraspinatus
B. Teres minor
C. Teres major
D. Supraspinatus
E. Subscapularis
Answer: D. Supraspinatus
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The spine divides the posterior scapula into the supraspinous fossa, which is the smaller of the two and lies above the spine, and the larger infraspinous fossa below it. Supraspinatus attaches in the supraspinous fossa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Infraspinatus fills the larger infraspinous fossa below the spine.
B. Teres minor attaches to the middle part of the lateral border of the scapula.
C. Teres major attaches at the inferior angle of the scapula.
E. Subscapularis lies in the subscapular fossa on the anterior surface of the bone.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2039
During dissection of the anterior shoulder, three structures are found converging on a single hook-like process at the superolateral edge of the scapula. Which of the following sets is most likely attached there?
A. Pectoralis major, short head of biceps brachii, and brachialis
B. Pectoralis minor, long head of triceps brachii, and coracobrachialis
C. Pectoralis minor, long head of biceps brachii, and coracobrachialis
D. Supraspinatus, short head of biceps brachii, and coracobrachialis
E. Pectoralis minor, short head of biceps brachii, and coracobrachialis
Answer: E. Pectoralis minor, short head of biceps brachii, and coracobrachialis
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The coracoid process is the hook-like structure on the lateral edge of the superior anterior scapula, and it's the attachment site for pectoralis minor, the short head of biceps brachii, and coracobrachialis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Pectoralis major attaches to the humerus, and brachialis arises from the humeral shaft.
B. The long head of triceps brachii arises from the infraglenoid tubercle.
C. The long head of biceps brachii arises from the supraglenoid tubercle, not the coracoid.
D. Supraspinatus arises from the supraspinous fossa.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2040
During dissection of the shoulder, three tendons are traced to facets on a single prominence on the superior posterior aspect of the humerus. Which of the following sets of muscles most likely inserts there?
A. Supraspinatus, teres minor, and teres major
B. Infraspinatus, teres minor, and teres major
C. Supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and subscapularis
D. Infraspinatus, teres minor, and subscapularis
E. Supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and teres minor
Answer: E. Supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and teres minor
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm; Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The greater tubercle is the prominence on the superior posterior portion of the humerus, and it's the insertion site for supraspinatus on the superior facet, infraspinatus on the middle facet, and teres minor on the inferior facet.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Teres major inserts on the medial lip of the intertubercular groove.
B. Both subscapularis and teres major attach elsewhere, so this set is wrong twice over.
C. Subscapularis inserts on the lesser tubercle, on the anterior side.
D. Subscapularis belongs on the lesser tubercle rather than the greater.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2041
During dissection of the proximal humerus, a tendon is found running within the groove between the two humeral tubercles, and a second muscle is found inserting on the floor of that same groove. Which of the following pairs is most likely?
A. Short head of biceps brachii in the groove and pectoralis major on the floor
B. Long head of biceps brachii in the groove and subscapularis on the floor
C. Long head of biceps brachii in the groove and latissimus dorsi on the floor
D. Long head of triceps brachii in the groove and latissimus dorsi on the floor
E. Long head of biceps brachii in the groove and teres minor on the floor
Answer: C. Long head of biceps brachii in the groove and latissimus dorsi on the floor
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The intertubercular (bicipital) groove lies between the greater and lesser tubercles. The long head of biceps brachii runs through the groove and latissimus dorsi inserts on its floor.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The short head of biceps brachii attaches to the coracoid process and doesn't run in the groove.
B. Subscapularis inserts on the lesser tubercle rather than in the groove.
D. The long head of triceps brachii runs in the posterior arm from the infraglenoid tubercle.
E. Teres minor inserts on the inferior facet of the greater tubercle.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2042
During dissection of the elbow region, a bony process is seen to occupy a depression on the posterior aspect of the distal humerus. Which of the following structures sits in that depression?
A. Olecranon of the ulna
B. Lesser tubercle of the humerus
C. Deltoid tuberosity of the humerus
D. Infraglenoid tubercle of the scapula
E. Greater tubercle of the humerus
Answer: A. Olecranon of the ulna
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The olecranon fossa is the depression on the distal posterior aspect of the humerus, and it receives the olecranon process of the ulna. The olecranon is also the insertion site for triceps brachii.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The lesser tubercle sits at the proximal humerus and takes subscapularis.
C. The deltoid tuberosity is a lateral protuberance on the humeral shaft.
D. The infraglenoid tubercle is on the scapula, below the glenoid cavity.
E. The greater tubercle sits at the proximal humerus and takes supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and teres minor.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2043
An anteroposterior radiograph of the shoulder is reviewed, and a short hook-like process is seen projecting from the superolateral scapula, lying medial to the humeral head. Which of the following structures is most likely being identified?
A. Glenoid fossa
B. Acromion
C. Lesser tubercle
D. Greater tubercle
E. Coracoid process
Answer: E. Coracoid process
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Shoulder radiograph: identifying structures; Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder
Why this is correct:
On the plain film of the shoulder, the coracoid process appears as the hook-like projection from the anterior superolateral scapula, sitting medial to the humeral head.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The glenoid fossa is the articular surface facing the humeral head rather than a projecting process.
B. The acromion is the broad lateral extension of the scapular spine, lying above the humeral head.
C. The lesser tubercle is on the humerus, on its anterior side.
D. The greater tubercle is on the humerus, not the scapula.
Objective: Identify anatomical structures associated with the shoulder and posterior arm on X-Rays, MRI, and CT scans.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2044
A plain film of the shoulder is reviewed before reduction of a dislocation, and the shallow articular surface of the scapula that normally receives the humeral head is identified. Which of the following structures is most likely being identified?
A. Glenoid fossa
B. Supraspinous fossa
C. Infraspinous fossa
D. Subscapular fossa
E. Suprascapular notch
Answer: A. Glenoid fossa
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Shoulder radiograph: identifying structures; Glenohumeral joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The glenoid fossa is the shallow cavity on the lateral scapula that articulates with the head of the humerus to form the glenohumeral joint, and it's one of the structures labeled on the lecture's radiograph.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The supraspinous fossa lies above the scapular spine and holds supraspinatus.
C. The infraspinous fossa lies below the scapular spine and holds infraspinatus.
D. The subscapular fossa is the anterior costal surface of the scapula and holds subscapularis.
E. The suprascapular notch is an indentation on the superior border of the scapula.
Objective: Identify anatomical structures associated with the shoulder and posterior arm on X-Rays, MRI, and CT scans.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2045
On a radiograph of the shoulder, the lateral end of the clavicle is followed laterally until it reaches the bone it articulates with. Which of the following structures is most likely identified at that articulation?
A. Greater tubercle
B. Spine of the scapula
C. Coracoid process
D. Glenoid fossa
E. Acromion
Answer: E. Acromion
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Shoulder radiograph: identifying structures; Acromioclavicular joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The acromial end of the clavicle articulates with the acromion of the scapula at the acromioclavicular joint, and both are labeled on the lecture's radiograph.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The greater tubercle belongs to the humerus and has no clavicular articulation.
B. The spine of the scapula continues into the acromion but isn't the articulating part.
C. The coracoid process is anchored to the clavicle by ligament rather than by a joint.
D. The glenoid fossa articulates with the humeral head, not the clavicle.
Objective: Identify anatomical structures associated with the shoulder and posterior arm on X-Rays, MRI, and CT scans.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2046
During dissection of the upper limb, the prosector points out the single bony articulation that joins the entire upper extremity to the axial skeleton. Which of the following joints is being shown?
A. Sternoclavicular
B. Glenohumeral
C. Acromioclavicular
D. Costoclavicular
E. Scapulothoracic
Answer: A. Sternoclavicular
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint: type and articulation; Clavicle in the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
The sternoclavicular joint is the only articulation conjoining the upper extremity to the axial skeleton, which is why the clavicle serves as the strut that anchors the limb.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The glenohumeral joint is between the humerus and the scapula, both of the limb.
C. The acromioclavicular joint connects two bones that both belong to the upper limb.
D. The costoclavicular connection is a ligament rather than a joint.
E. The scapulothoracic interface isn't a true anatomic joint.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2047
The sternoclavicular joint is classified by the shape of its articular surfaces as a saddle joint, yet in use it permits protraction and retraction, elevation and depression, and circumduction of the clavicle. Which of the following best describes how it functions?
A. As a multiaxial ball and socket joint
B. As a uniaxial pivot joint
C. As a biaxial condyloid joint
D. As a uniaxial hinge joint
E. As a nonaxial plane joint
Answer: A. As a multiaxial ball and socket joint
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The sternoclavicular joint is a saddle synovial joint that functions as a ball and socket and is multiaxial, which is what allows the range of clavicular movement listed.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. A pivot joint permits rotation about a single longitudinal axis.
C. A biaxial condyloid joint wouldn't account for circumduction with rotation.
D. A uniaxial hinge would allow movement in one plane only.
E. A nonaxial plane joint describes the acromioclavicular joint instead.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2049
In a cadaveric study of the sternoclavicular joint, a single ligament is sectioned, after which the medial end of the clavicle can be elevated considerably further than before while the rest of the joint remains intact. Which of the following ligaments was most likely sectioned?
A. Acromioclavicular
B. Interclavicular
C. Costoclavicular
D. Sternoclavicular
E. Coracoclavicular
Answer: C. Costoclavicular
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint: ligaments and articular disc
Why this is correct:
The costoclavicular ligament resists superior translation of the clavicle and limits elevation of the pectoral girdle, so cutting it releases the main check on upward movement.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The acromioclavicular ligament belongs to the other end of the clavicle.
B. The interclavicular ligament resists inferior translation rather than elevation.
D. The sternoclavicular ligament resists anterior and posterior translation.
E. The coracoclavicular ligament stabilizes the acromioclavicular joint.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2050
In a cadaveric sternoclavicular joint, one ligament is cut and the medial end of the clavicle then translates inferiorly much more than it did before, while elevation and anteroposterior movement are unchanged. Which of the following ligaments was most likely cut?
A. Costoclavicular
B. Sternoclavicular
C. Coracoclavicular
D. Acromioclavicular
E. Interclavicular
Answer: E. Interclavicular
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint: ligaments and articular disc
Why this is correct:
The interclavicular ligament resists inferior translation of the clavicle, so losing it allows the medial end to drop while the other directions stay controlled.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The costoclavicular ligament resists superior translation, so elevation would have changed.
B. The sternoclavicular ligament resists anterior and posterior translation.
C. The coracoclavicular ligament acts at the acromioclavicular joint.
D. The acromioclavicular ligament acts at the lateral end of the clavicle.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2051
The sternoclavicular joint space is found to contain a fibrocartilaginous structure that divides the cavity into two separate compartments. Which of the following best describes what this structure contributes to the joint?
A. It increases congruency and absorbs shock
B. It increases joint laxity and absorbs shock
C. It increases congruency and restricts circumduction
D. It increases the range of elevation and absorbs shock
E. It increases congruency and supplies the joint with blood
Answer: A. It increases congruency and absorbs shock
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint: ligaments and articular disc
Why this is correct:
The fibrocartilaginous articular disc separates the sternoclavicular joint into two cavities, increases congruency between the saddle-shaped surfaces, and absorbs shock. The notes add that it also prevents medial dislocation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The disc improves fit rather than adding laxity.
C. The disc doesn't restrict circumduction, which the joint still performs.
D. Elevation is limited by the costoclavicular ligament, not increased by the disc.
E. Blood supply isn't a function attributed to the articular disc.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2052
A 15-year-old boy is brought to the emergency department after falling onto his shoulder. Imaging shows a fractured clavicle, and the joint at the medial end of the bone is intact and well aligned. Which of the following best explains why that joint stayed intact?
A. Its capsule is reinforced by the deltoid and trapezius attachments
B. Its articular surfaces are congruent enough that displacement can't occur
C. Its capsule is absent, so force passes straight through to the bone
D. Its articular disc transmits the whole force directly into the first rib
E. Its surrounding ligaments are strong enough that the clavicle usually fails first
Answer: E. Its surrounding ligaments are strong enough that the clavicle usually fails first
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint dislocation; Clavicle fracture
Why this is correct:
Dislocations of the sternoclavicular joint are rare because the surrounding ligaments are so strong, and the clavicle is likely to fracture before the joint dislocates.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Deltoid and trapezius reinforce the acromioclavicular ligament at the other end of the bone.
B. The saddle surfaces are improved by the disc but congruency isn't what prevents dislocation here.
C. The joint has a capsule, and its anterior and posterior ligaments are thickenings of it.
D. The disc absorbs shock and prevents medial dislocation rather than shunting all force to the rib.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2053
A 28-year-old man has an injury at the medial end of the clavicle. Imaging and examination show that the sternoclavicular ligament has torn while the costoclavicular ligament remains intact. Which of the following best describes the resulting injury?
A. An avulsion fracture of the proximal clavicle
B. A separation of the acromioclavicular joint
C. A subluxation of the sternoclavicular joint
D. A rupture of the coracoclavicular ligament
E. A complete dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint
Answer: C. A subluxation of the sternoclavicular joint
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Sternoclavicular joint dislocation; Sternoclavicular joint: ligaments and articular disc
Why this is correct:
A subluxation of the sternoclavicular joint occurs when one of the two ligaments, the sternoclavicular or the costoclavicular, is torn. A dislocation requires both to tear.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. An avulsion fracture pulls bone away from the proximal clavicle rather than tearing one ligament.
B. A separation involves the acromioclavicular joint at the opposite end of the clavicle.
D. The coracoclavicular ligament belongs to the acromioclavicular joint.
E. A complete dislocation would require both ligaments to be torn.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

Question 2054

The lecture illustrates sternoclavicular dislocations as either anterior or posterior displacements of the medial clavicle.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2055
The joint formed between the acromial end of the clavicle and the acromion of the scapula is being classified in a review of the shoulder complex. Which of the following best describes it?
A. A uniaxial hinge synovial joint
B. A multiaxial ball and socket synovial joint
C. A biaxial condyloid synovial joint
D. A multiaxial saddle synovial joint
E. A uniaxial plane synovial joint
Answer: E. A uniaxial plane synovial joint
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Acromioclavicular joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The acromioclavicular joint is a plane type of synovial joint and is uniaxial, with flat articular surfaces where the acromion rotates on the acromial end of the clavicle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. A hinge joint allows flexion and extension in one plane, which isn't this joint's motion.
B. The ball and socket classification belongs to the glenohumeral joint.
C. A condyloid joint has an oval convex surface in a matching concavity.
D. The saddle classification belongs to the sternoclavicular joint.

Question 2056
During dissection of a 62-year-old body donor, a complete articular disc is found within the acromioclavicular joint. Which of the following best explains this finding?
A. It indicates degeneration and is present in about 10 percent of adults
B. It indicates prior dislocation and is present in about 10 percent of adults
C. It's a normal variant present in about 50 percent of adults

Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Acromioclavicular joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
A complete articular disc is present in about 10 percent of adults at the acromioclavicular joint, and the lecture notes that it isn't an anomaly but an indication of degeneration.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Prior dislocation isn't the explanation given for a complete disc.
C. The stated frequency is about 10 percent, not half.
D. The disc is a sign of degeneration rather than a protection against it.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2058
During dissection of the shoulder, the prosector identifies the structure described as the primary stabilizer of the acromioclavicular joint, which allows the scapula and free limb to hang passively from the strut-like clavicle. Which of the following is it?
A. Coracoclavicular ligament
B. Acromioclavicular ligament
C. Costoclavicular ligament
D. Coracoacromial ligament
E. Interclavicular ligament
Answer: A. Coracoclavicular ligament
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments
Why this is correct:
The coracoclavicular ligament is a pair of strong bands anchoring the clavicle to the coracoid process. It's the primary stabilizer of the acromioclavicular joint and lets the scapula and limb be suspended from the clavicle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The acromioclavicular ligament strengthens the joint superiorly but isn't the primary stabilizer.
C. The costoclavicular ligament belongs to the sternoclavicular joint.
D. The coracoacromial ligament forms part of the arch over the humeral head.
E. The interclavicular ligament spans between the clavicles at the sternal ends.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2059
Two bands anchor the clavicle to the coracoid process. On dissection, the more medial of the two is vertically oriented and shaped like an inverted triangle. Which of the following names that band?
A. Coracoacromial ligament
B. Acromioclavicular ligament
C. Transverse humeral ligament
D. Conoid ligament
E. Trapezoid ligament
Answer: D. Conoid ligament
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments
Why this is correct:
The coracoclavicular ligament has two components. The conoid ligament is the medial, vertically oriented band shaped like an inverted triangle, and the trapezoid is the lateral, horizontally oriented band.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The coracoacromial ligament runs from the coracoid to the acromion, not to the clavicle.
B. The acromioclavicular ligament runs between the clavicle and the acromion.
C. The transverse humeral ligament bridges the humeral tubercles.
E. The trapezoid ligament is the lateral and horizontally oriented component.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

Question 2060
During dissection the acromioclavicular ligament is found to be reinforced on its superior surface by the attachments of two overlying muscles. Which of the following pairs of muscles reinforces it?
A. Deltoid and pectoralis major
B. Trapezius and rhomboid major

Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2061
A 20-year-old rugby player lands on an outstretched arm, driving an upward force along the humerus into the shoulder. Imaging shows widening of the acromioclavicular joint, but the clavicle isn't displaced superiorly and the acromion isn't unusually prominent. Which of the following ligaments is most likely ruptured?
A. Interclavicular
B. Costoclavicular
C. Acromioclavicular
D. Coracoacromial
E. Coracoclavicular
Answer: C. Acromioclavicular
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Acromioclavicular sprain and shoulder separation; Acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments
Why this is correct:
Acromioclavicular subluxations are usually associated with upward forces along the humerus and result in rupture of the acromioclavicular ligament. Because the coracoclavicular ligament is intact, the clavicle isn't displaced superiorly.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The interclavicular ligament acts at the sternoclavicular joint.
B. The costoclavicular ligament acts at the sternoclavicular joint.
D. The coracoacromial ligament forms the arch above the humeral head and isn't torn in this pattern.
E. Coracoclavicular rupture produces the more severe injury with superior clavicular displacement.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2062
A 25-year-old cyclist is brought to the emergency department after landing directly on the tip of his shoulder. On examination the clavicle sits visibly higher than normal and the acromion is unusually prominent on palpation. Which of the following best describes the ligament damage?
A. The coracoclavicular ligament in addition to the acromioclavicular
B. The coracoacromial ligament in addition to the acromioclavicular
C. The transverse humeral ligament in addition to the acromioclavicular
D. The costoclavicular ligament in addition to the acromioclavicular
E. The interclavicular ligament in addition to the acromioclavicular
Answer: A. The coracoclavicular ligament in addition to the acromioclavicular
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Acromioclavicular sprain and shoulder separation; Acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments
Why this is correct:
Acromioclavicular dislocation is the more severe injury and is associated with blunt force trauma to the tip of the shoulder. It ruptures the strong coracoclavicular ligament along with the acromioclavicular ligament, so the clavicle is displaced superiorly and the acromion becomes more prominent.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The coracoacromial ligament isn't part of the acromioclavicular joint's stabilizing pair.
C. The transverse humeral ligament holds the biceps tendon in the intertubercular groove.
D. The costoclavicular ligament stabilizes the sternoclavicular joint.
E. The interclavicular ligament stabilizes the sternoclavicular joint.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2063
A 31-year-old man sustains a shoulder separation in which the scapula and free limb lose their passive suspension from the clavicle. Which of the following best explains the resulting deformity?
A. The limb falls away under gravity while the clavicle remains in place
B. The scapula is pulled medially by the rhomboids while the clavicle descends
C. The clavicle is pulled inferiorly by subclavius while the limb remains in place
D. The scapula is pulled laterally by serratus anterior while the clavicle rises
E. The limb is pulled superiorly by trapezius while the clavicle descends
Answer: A. The limb falls away under gravity while the clavicle remains in place
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Acromioclavicular sprain and shoulder separation; Acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments
Why this is correct:
The coracoclavicular ligament normally lets the scapula and free limb be passively suspended from the strut-like clavicle. Once it ruptures, gravity carries the limb downward while the clavicle stays up, which is why the clavicle looks displaced superiorly.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The rhomboids retract the scapula and aren't the cause of this appearance.
C. Subclavius is small and isn't what produces the deformity.
D. Serratus anterior protracts the scapula and isn't the cause of this appearance.
E. Trapezius doesn't lift the limb in this injury, and the clavicle doesn't descend.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2064
The articulation between the humeral head and the glenoid fossa of the scapula is being classified during a review of the shoulder complex. Which of the following best describes it?
A. A multiaxial ball and socket synovial joint
B. A uniaxial plane synovial joint
C. A biaxial condyloid synovial joint
D. A multiaxial saddle synovial joint
E. A uniaxial hinge synovial joint
Answer: A. A multiaxial ball and socket synovial joint
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Glenohumeral joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The glenohumeral joint is a ball and socket type of synovial joint and is multiaxial, formed by the large humeral head and the shallow glenoid fossa of the scapula.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The plane classification belongs to the acromioclavicular joint.
C. A condyloid joint is biaxial and doesn't describe this articulation.
D. The saddle classification belongs to the sternoclavicular joint.
E. A hinge joint permits motion in a single plane.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2065
In a cadaveric study of the glenohumeral joint, the humeral head is found to be in contact with only a small fraction of the glenoid surface in any given position of the limb. Which of the following values best matches the figure given in lecture?
A. About 70 percent
B. About 50 percent
C. About 90 percent
D. About 30 percent
E. About 10 percent
Answer: D. About 30 percent
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Glenohumeral joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
Only about 30 percent of the humeral head is in contact with the glenoid at any given position, which is part of why the joint has great mobility and poor stability.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Seventy percent would describe a far more congruent joint than this one.
B. About 50 percent is the figure for how much the labrum deepens the socket.
C. Ninety percent is the figure given for anterior glenohumeral dislocations.
E. Ten percent is the figure given for a complete articular disc in the acromioclavicular joint.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2066
A 19-year-old competitive swimmer has recurrent episodes in which her shoulder feels as though it slips out of place, and examination confirms glenohumeral instability. Which of the following combinations of features best accounts for the joint's great mobility and poor stability?
A. A shallow ovoid fossa, poor articular congruency, and a lax joint capsule
B. A deep fossa, poor articular congruency, and a lax joint capsule
C. A deep fossa, high articular congruency, and a tight joint capsule
D. A shallow ovoid fossa, high articular congruency, and a tight joint capsule
E. A shallow ovoid fossa, high articular congruency, and a lax joint capsule
Answer: A. A shallow ovoid fossa, poor articular congruency, and a lax joint capsule
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Glenohumeral joint: type and articulation; Fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint
Why this is correct:
The glenoid fossa is a shallow ovoid, the articular congruency is poor, and the capsule is lax. Those features together are what the lecture gives as the source of the joint's mobility and its weak static stability.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The glenoid fossa is shallow, not deep.
C. A deep, congruent, tightly bound joint would be stable rather than mobile.
D. High congruency and a tight capsule contradict the described joint.
E. High congruency contradicts the described joint.

Question 2067

Question 2069
During inspection of a dissected glenohumeral joint, the labrum is found to differ in character from its upper margin to its lower margin. Which of the following best contrasts the two?
A. Superiorly and inferiorly it's mobile and meniscoid, but it's thicker along the lower margin
B. Superiorly it's mobile and meniscoid, inferiorly it's thicker and equally mobile
C. Superiorly and inferiorly it's firmly attached, but it's thicker along the upper margin
D. Superiorly it's rounder, smaller, and more firmly attached, inferiorly it's mobile and meniscoid

B. The inferior labrum is described as more firmly attached rather than equally mobile.

Question 2070

Question 2071
A 68-year-old man sustains a proximal humeral fracture, and at operation the glenohumeral joint capsule is found to be torn where it attaches to bone well below the tubercles. Which of the following best explains why the capsule reaches that far distally?
A. The capsule attaches to the deltoid tuberosity along its inferior margin
B. The capsule attaches only to the greater and lesser tubercles of the humerus
C. The capsule has no bony humeral attachment and blends only with periosteum
D. Inferiorly the humeral attachment stops short of the anatomical neck
E. Inferiorly the humeral attachment extends more distally toward the surgical neck
Answer: E. Inferiorly the humeral attachment extends more distally toward the surgical neck
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint; Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The capsule attaches to the anatomical neck of the humerus, but inferiorly the humeral attachment is more distal, toward the surgical neck. That's why a fracture at that level can involve the capsule.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The deltoid tuberosity lies on the shaft, well distal to any capsular attachment.
B. The tubercles take tendon insertions rather than defining the capsular attachment.
C. The capsule has a defined bony attachment at the anatomical neck.
D. The attachment extends past the anatomical neck inferiorly rather than stopping short of it.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2072
In a cadaveric glenohumeral joint, traction on the humerus separates the head from the glenoid fossa by 2 to 2.5 cm before the soft tissues become taut. Which of the following best explains the amount of translation permitted?
A. The glenohumeral ligaments attach only to the glenoid labrum
B. The coracohumeral ligament is lax in the resting position
C. The glenoid labrum is absent along the inferior rim of the fossa
D. The fibrous capsule is roughly twice the size of the humeral head
E. The fibrous capsule is roughly the same size as the humeral head
Answer: D. The fibrous capsule is roughly twice the size of the humeral head
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint
Why this is correct:
The capsule of the glenohumeral joint is twice the size of the humeral head, which allows translational movements and about 2 to 2.5 cm of distraction.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The glenohumeral ligaments are thickenings of the capsule itself.
B. The coracohumeral ligament strengthens the capsule superiorly and isn't the reason for distraction.
C. The labrum is present inferiorly, where it's rounder, smaller, and more firmly attached.
E. A capsule matched to the head would restrict rather than permit that translation.

Question 2073

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2074
The glenohumeral ligaments are thickenings of the fibrous capsule, and in lecture they're described as superior, middle, and inferior, with the inferior one having an anterior band, an axillary pouch, and a posterior band. Which part of the capsule do they reinforce?
A. The superior capsule alone
B. The posterior capsule alone
C. The posterior and superior capsule
D. The inferior capsule alone
E. The anterior and inferior capsule
Answer: E. The anterior and inferior capsule
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Glenohumeral and coracohumeral ligaments; Fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint
Why this is correct:
The glenohumeral ligaments reinforce the anterior and inferior parts of the joint capsule, which matters because the capsule is weakest anteroinferiorly.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The superior capsule alone leaves out the anterior and inferior reinforcement.
B. The posterior capsule isn't the region these ligaments reinforce.
C. The superior capsule is strengthened by the coracohumeral ligament instead.
D. The inferior capsule alone leaves out the anterior reinforcement.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2077
In a cadaveric study the coracoacromial ligament is divided, and the humeral head can then be pushed considerably further in one direction than it could before. Which of the following displacements is most likely increased?
A. Inferior displacement of the humeral head
B. Medial displacement of the humeral head
C. Superior displacement of the humeral head
D. Posterior displacement of the humeral head
E. Anterior displacement of the humeral head
Answer: C. Superior displacement of the humeral head
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Coracoacromial arch
Why this is correct:
The coracoacromial arch sits above the humeral head and prevents its superior displacement, so dividing the ligament that completes the arch removes that restraint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Inferior movement is limited by the capsule and the rotator cuff rather than the arch.
B. Medial displacement is blocked by the glenoid fossa itself.
D. Posterior displacement isn't the direction the arch blocks.
E. Anterior displacement is restrained by the anterior capsule and glenohumeral ligaments.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2078
A 28-year-old man sustains a glenohumeral dislocation during a wrestling match, and the humeral head comes to lie anteroinferior to the glenoid fossa rather than above it. Which of the following best explains why the head didn't displace superiorly?
A. The joint capsule is tightest at its superior attachment
B. The glenohumeral ligaments are strongest superiorly
C. The supraspinatus tendon alone blocks superior movement
D. The glenoid labrum is thickest along its superior rim
E. The coracoacromial arch is strong superiorly
Answer: E. The coracoacromial arch is strong superiorly
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Coracoacromial arch; Glenohumeral dislocation
Why this is correct:
Glenohumeral dislocation happens primarily anteriorly because the coracoacromial arch is strong superiorly and the joint capsule is weak anteroinferiorly, so the head takes the path of least resistance.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The capsule is lax rather than tight, and its weakness is anteroinferior.
B. The glenohumeral ligaments reinforce the anterior and inferior capsule.
C. The supraspinatus tendon runs in the subacromial space but isn't described as the superior block.
D. The labrum is mobile and meniscoid superiorly rather than thickest there.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2081
A 48-year-old competitive swimmer has shoulder pain when raising the arm overhead. Imaging shows narrowing of the roughly 1 cm interval that normally lies between the coracoacromial arch and the humeral head. Which of the following structures is most likely being compressed?
A. The infraspinatus tendon
B. The subscapularis tendon
C. The teres minor tendon

Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Subacromial space and bursa; Coracoacromial arch
Why this is correct:
The subacromial space is about 1 cm between the arch and the humeral head, and the supraspinatus tendon runs through it. Narrowing that interval compresses the tendon, which fits the pain on overhead activity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Infraspinatus passes posteriorly and not under the arch.
B. Subscapularis passes anteriorly to the lesser tubercle.
C. Teres minor passes posteriorly to the greater tubercle.
D. The long head of triceps brachii doesn't enter the subacromial space.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2082
A 21-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a tackle, and radiographs confirm that the humeral head has left the glenoid fossa. Which of the following directions of displacement is the most likely?
A. Medial
B. Superior
C. Inferior
D. Posterior
E. Anterior
Answer: E. Anterior
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Glenohumeral dislocation
Why this is correct:
The glenohumeral joint is the most frequently dislocated joint in the body, and about 90 percent of those dislocations are anterior.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Medial displacement is blocked by the glenoid fossa and the scapula.
B. Superior displacement is prevented by the coracoacromial arch.
C. Inferior displacement isn't the predominant pattern described.
D. Posterior dislocations occur but are far less common than anterior ones.
