MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2083
A 33-year-old woman sustains an anterior glenohumeral dislocation. Before and after reduction, the clinician tests sensation over the skin of the lateral shoulder and the strength of abduction of the arm. Which of the following structures is being assessed?
A. Radial nerve
B. Musculocutaneous nerve
C. Axillary nerve
D. Lower subscapular nerve
E. Suprascapular nerve
Answer: C. Axillary nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Glenohumeral dislocation; Deltoid
Why this is correct:
The axillary nerve is at risk in glenohumeral dislocation. It supplies the deltoid, which abducts the arm, and the skin over the deltoid, so testing abduction and lateral shoulder sensation checks that nerve.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve supplies the posterior compartment and the skin of the posterior arm and forearm.
B. The musculocutaneous nerve supplies the anterior arm muscles and the lateral forearm skin.
D. The lower subscapular nerve supplies teres major and subscapularis with no cutaneous branch.
E. The suprascapular nerve supplies supraspinatus and infraspinatus and carries no cutaneous territory over the deltoid.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2084
A 26-year-old man has an anterior glenohumeral dislocation that's about to be reduced in the emergency department. Which of the following is most critical to perform both before and after the reduction?

B. Documenting the range of passive abduction of the arm

Question 2085
In a review of the shoulder complex, the scapulothoracic interface is singled out as not being a true anatomic joint. Which of the following best explains why?

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2086
A 54-year-old woman has a sternoclavicular joint that fused after an old injury, and the clavicle on that side can no longer move. Which of the following is most likely reduced as a result?
A. Rotation of the humeral head within the glenoid fossa
B. Rotation of the radius on the ulna in the forearm
C. Inferior translation of the humeral head in the fossa
D. Flexion and extension at the elbow joint
E. Movement of the scapula on the thoracic wall
Answer: E. Movement of the scapula on the thoracic wall
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Scapulothoracic interface; Sternoclavicular joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
Movement of the scapula at the scapulothoracic interface depends on movement at the sternoclavicular and acromioclavicular joints, so losing motion at the sternoclavicular joint limits how the scapula can move on the thorax.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Glenohumeral rotation depends on that joint's own capsule and muscles.
B. Forearm rotation occurs at the radioulnar joints.
C. Inferior translation at the glenohumeral joint depends on its capsule and cuff.
D. Elbow motion is unrelated to the sternoclavicular joint.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2087
A review of the shoulder complex lists its articulations together with the one interface that isn't a true joint. Which of the following sets is correct?
A. Sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular, scapulothoracic, and coracoacromial
B. Sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular, glenohumeral, and scapulothoracic
C. Sternoclavicular, glenohumeral, scapulothoracic, and costoclavicular
D. Acromioclavicular, glenohumeral, scapulothoracic, and coracoclavicular
E. Sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular, glenohumeral, and costoclavicular
Answer: B. Sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular, glenohumeral, and scapulothoracic
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Scapulothoracic interface; Purpose of the shoulder complex
Why this is correct:
The joints of the shoulder complex are the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular, and glenohumeral joints, together with the scapulothoracic interface.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The glenohumeral joint is missing and the coracoacromial arch is a ligamentous structure.
C. The acromioclavicular joint is missing and a ligament has been included.
D. The coracoclavicular connection is a ligament and the sternoclavicular joint is missing.
E. The costoclavicular connection is a ligament rather than a joint of the complex.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

Difficulty: Easy

The shoulder girdle muscles are subclavius, pectoralis minor, serratus anterior, trapezius, levator scapulae, rhomboid major, and rhomboid minor.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Coracobrachialis is grouped with the scapulohumeral movers of the humerus.
B. Deltoid is grouped with the scapulohumeral movers of the humerus.
C. Latissimus dorsi is grouped with the thoracohumeral movers of the humerus.
D. Teres major is grouped with the scapulohumeral movers of the humerus.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2089

B. Biceps brachii and triceps brachii are listed as other extrinsic muscles.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2091
A 30-year-old man is tested with the arm held out to the side at 90 degrees against resistance, and that movement is weak while flexion and extension of the shoulder are preserved. Which of the following is most likely responsible for the tested movement?
A. The posterior part of the deltoid, arising from the spine of the scapula
B. The anterior part of the deltoid, arising from the acromion process
C. The middle part of the deltoid, arising from the acromion
D. The posterior part of the deltoid, arising from the lateral third of the clavicle
E. The anterior part of the deltoid, arising from the lateral third of the clavicle
Answer: C. The middle part of the deltoid, arising from the acromion
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Deltoid
Why this is correct:
The middle part of the deltoid arises from the acromion process and abducts the humerus, while the anterior and posterior parts assist with flexion and medial rotation and with extension and lateral rotation respectively.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The posterior part assists in extension and lateral rotation of the arm.
B. The anterior part arises from the lateral third of the clavicle, not the acromion.
D. The posterior part arises from the spine of the scapula, not the clavicle.
E. The anterior part assists in flexion and medial rotation of the arm.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2092
A 72-year-old woman sustained a fracture of the proximal humerus. Six weeks later the rounded contour of that shoulder has disappeared, there's a slight hollow just below the acromion, and sensation over the point of the shoulder is reduced. Which of the following nerves is most likely injured?
A. Radial
B. Musculocutaneous
C. Suprascapular
D. Axillary
E. Lower subscapular
Answer: D. Axillary
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Deltoid; Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm
Why this is correct:
When the axillary nerve is severely damaged the deltoid atrophies, so the rounded contour of the shoulder disappears and a hollow appears inferior to the acromion. The nerve also supplies the skin over the deltoid, which explains the sensory loss.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Radial injury affects the posterior compartment of the arm and forearm.
B. Musculocutaneous injury affects the anterior arm muscles and the lateral forearm skin.
C. Suprascapular injury affects supraspinatus and infraspinatus and causes no sensory loss over the shoulder.
E. Lower subscapular injury affects teres major and subscapularis without cutaneous loss.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2093
During dissection of the shoulder, all three parts of the deltoid are traced distally and converge on a single attachment on the humerus. Which of the following is that attachment?
A. Surgical neck
B. Lesser tubercle
C. Deltoid tuberosity
D. Greater tubercle
E. Intertubercular groove
Answer: C. Deltoid tuberosity
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Deltoid; Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The anterior, middle, and posterior parts of the deltoid all insert on the deltoid tuberosity, the lateral protuberance on the humeral shaft.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The surgical neck is a narrow region distal to the tubercles and a common fracture site.
B. The lesser tubercle takes subscapularis.
D. The greater tubercle takes supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and teres minor.
E. The intertubercular groove carries the long head of biceps and takes latissimus dorsi on its floor.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2095
During dissection a muscle running from the inferior angle of the scapula to the medial lip of the intertubercular groove is found to have lost its nerve supply. Which of the following nerves was most likely injured?
A. Upper subscapular
B. Lower subscapular
C. Axillary
D. Thoracodorsal
E. Suprascapular
Answer: B. Lower subscapular
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Teres major
Why this is correct:
Teres major is innervated by the lower subscapular nerve, which carries fibers from C5 and C6 and also supplies subscapularis.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The upper subscapular nerve supplies subscapularis.
C. The axillary nerve supplies deltoid and teres minor.
D. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies latissimus dorsi.
E. The suprascapular nerve supplies supraspinatus and infraspinatus.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2096
A 44-year-old man has an injury to the lower subscapular nerve following penetrating trauma to the posterior axilla. Which of the following movements of the arm is most likely weakened?
A. Adduction and medial rotation
B. Extension and lateral rotation
C. Abduction and lateral rotation
D. Abduction and medial rotation
E. Flexion and medial rotation
Answer: A. Adduction and medial rotation
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Teres major; Subscapularis as a rotator cuff muscle
Why this is correct:
The lower subscapular nerve supplies teres major and subscapularis. Teres major adducts and medially rotates the humerus, and subscapularis medially rotates and adducts it, so both of those actions weaken.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Extension and lateral rotation come from the posterior deltoid and the lateral rotators.
C. Abduction comes from deltoid and supraspinatus, and lateral rotation from infraspinatus and teres minor.
D. Abduction isn't supplied by this nerve.
E. Flexion of the arm comes from the anterior deltoid and the clavicular head of pectoralis major.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2097
During a shoulder dissection, a student is asked to identify the four short muscles that run from the scapula to the tubercles of the humerus and whose tendons blend with the fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint. Which of the following muscles belongs in that group?
A. Coracobrachialis
B. Latissimus dorsi
C. Teres minor
D. Pectoralis minor
E. Teres major
Answer: C. Teres minor
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Rotator cuff: the SITS muscles; Teres minor
Why this is correct:
The rotator cuff is made of supraspinatus, infraspinatus, teres minor, and subscapularis (SITS). Teres minor runs from the lateral border of the scapula to the greater tubercle, and its tendon blends with the joint capsule.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Coracobrachialis runs from the coracoid process to the humeral shaft and does not reach the tubercles.
B. Latissimus dorsi is a thoracohumeral muscle rather than a cuff muscle.
D. Pectoralis minor attaches to the coracoid process and the ribs, not to the humerus.
E. Teres major runs from the inferior angle of the scapula to the intertubercular groove and is not part of the cuff.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2098
A 38-year-old man is evaluated for shoulder instability. Imaging shows that the tendons of four scapulohumeral muscles have become thinned where they blend with the fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint. Which of the following best explains the consequence of losing that tendinous sleeve?
A. The humeral head is no longer held in the glenoid cavity
B. The acromion is no longer held against the clavicle
C. The scapula is no longer held against the thoracic wall
D. The glenoid labrum is no longer held against the glenoid rim
E. The clavicle is no longer held against the manubrium
Answer: A. The humeral head is no longer held in the glenoid cavity
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Rotator cuff: the SITS muscles; Glenohumeral joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The cuff tendons blend with the capsule to form a musculotendinous sleeve that protects and strengthens the joint and helps hold the head of the humerus in the glenoid cavity. Losing it produces instability of the glenohumeral joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The acromion is held to the clavicle by the acromioclavicular and coracoclavicular ligaments.
C. The scapula is held against the thoracic wall by serratus anterior.
D. The labrum is attached to the glenoid rim itself rather than suspended by the cuff tendons.
E. The clavicle is held to the manubrium by the sternoclavicular ligaments, not by the cuff.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2099
During a cadaver lab, three of the four rotator cuff tendons are traced to a single bony prominence on the proximal humerus, where they attach at three separate facets. Which of the following structures receives those three tendons?
A. Lesser tubercle
B. Greater tubercle
C. Intertubercular groove
D. Infraglenoid tubercle
E. Deltoid tuberosity
Answer: B. Greater tubercle
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Rotator cuff: the SITS muscles; Supraspinatus; Teres minor
Why this is correct:
Supraspinatus attaches to the superior facet, infraspinatus to the middle facet, and teres minor to the inferior facet of the greater tubercle. Subscapularis is the one cuff muscle that goes to the lesser tubercle instead.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The lesser tubercle receives only subscapularis.
C. The intertubercular groove holds the long head of biceps tendon and receives latissimus dorsi, teres major, and pectoralis major.
D. The infraglenoid tubercle is on the scapula and gives origin to the long head of triceps.
E. The deltoid tuberosity is the attachment for deltoid, which is not a cuff muscle.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2100
During dissection of the posterior scapula, a muscle is traced from a fossa superior to the spine of the scapula to the superior facet of the greater tubercle of the humerus. Which of the following muscles is most likely being traced?
A. Subscapularis
B. Supraspinatus
C. Infraspinatus
D. Teres major
E. Teres minor
Answer: B. Supraspinatus

Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Supraspinatus; Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder
Why this is correct:
Supraspinatus arises from the supraspinous fossa, which lies superior to the spine of the scapula, and inserts on the superior facet of the greater tubercle.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Subscapularis arises from the subscapular fossa on the anterior surface and reaches the lesser tubercle.
C. Infraspinatus arises from the infraspinous fossa below the spine and reaches the middle facet.
D. Teres major arises from the inferior angle and reaches the medial lip of the intertubercular groove.
E. Teres minor arises from the middle part of the lateral border of the scapula and reaches the inferior facet.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2101
A 45-year-old woman is examined after a shoulder injury. She can hold the arm abducted once it is passively lifted away from her side, but she cannot begin the movement herself. Which of the following muscles is most likely to be the one affected?
A. Teres minor
B. Teres major
C. Subscapularis
D. Supraspinatus
E. Infraspinatus
Answer: D. Supraspinatus
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Supraspinatus; Rotator cuff injury and the deltoid force couple
Why this is correct:
Supraspinatus is the cuff muscle that abducts the arm, and loss of it leaves a patient with difficulty initiating abduction of the limb at the glenohumeral joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Teres minor laterally rotates the arm alongside infraspinatus.
B. Teres major adducts and medially rotates the arm and is not a cuff muscle.
C. Subscapularis medially rotates and adducts the humerus.
E. Infraspinatus laterally rotates the arm rather than abducting it.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2102
During a shoulder dissection, a nerve is traced through the suprascapular notch and followed to a muscle lying in the fossa above the scapular spine. Which of the following nerves is most likely being traced?
A. Upper subscapular nerve
B. Dorsal scapular nerve

E. Lower subscapular nerve

The suprascapular nerve passes through the suprascapular notch deep to the superior transverse scapular ligament and supplies supraspinatus, which occupies the supraspinous fossa, along with infraspinatus.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2104
A 29-year-old pitcher is evaluated for weakness of the throwing shoulder. MRI shows a full thickness tear of the two tendons that attach to the middle and inferior facets of the greater tubercle. Which of the following actions is most directly affected?
A. Extension of the humerus
B. Medial rotation of the humerus
C. Adduction of the humerus
D. Lateral rotation of the humerus
E. Abduction of the humerus
Answer: D. Lateral rotation of the humerus

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Infraspinatus; Teres minor; Humerus and ulna landmarks for the shoulder and posterior arm

Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Extension of the humerus is listed for the posterior part of deltoid and for the long head of triceps, neither of which attaches at these facets.

C. Adduction is listed for subscapularis and teres major, and teres major attaches to the medial lip of the intertubercular groove.
E. Abduction is the action of supraspinatus, which attaches to the superior facet of the greater tubercle.

Question 2107
Two rotator cuff muscles share the action of lateral rotation of the arm, but they are supplied by different nerves. Which of the following nerves supplies the one of that pair whose tendon reaches the inferior facet of the greater tubercle?
A. Upper subscapular nerve
B. Thoracodorsal nerve

D. Suprascapular nerve

Reference: Teres minor; Infraspinatus; Axillary and subscapular nerves at the shoulder

Why the other answers are wrong:

B. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies latissimus dorsi, which is not a cuff muscle.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2108
A 52-year-old man has an injury that spares the suprascapular nerve but damages the nerve supplying the deltoid. Which of the following rotator cuff muscles is most likely to be affected as well?
A. Teres minor
B. Teres major
C. Subscapularis
D. Infraspinatus
E. Supraspinatus
Answer: A. Teres minor
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Teres minor; Axillary and subscapular nerves at the shoulder; Deltoid
Why this is correct:
The axillary nerve supplies deltoid and teres minor, so an injury to the nerve of the deltoid takes teres minor with it while sparing the suprascapular nerve muscles.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Teres major is supplied by the lower subscapular nerve and is not a cuff muscle.
C. Subscapularis is supplied by the upper and lower subscapular nerves.
D. Infraspinatus is supplied by the suprascapular nerve, which was spared.
E. Supraspinatus is supplied by the suprascapular nerve, which was spared.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2109
During dissection of the anterior surface of the scapula, a muscle is traced from the fossa covering that surface to a prominence on the anterior aspect of the proximal humerus. Which of the following is the distal attachment of that muscle?
A. Surgical neck of the humerus
B. Lesser tubercle
C. Greater tubercle
D. Medial lip of the intertubercular groove
E. Deltoid tuberosity
Answer: B. Lesser tubercle
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Subscapularis as a rotator cuff muscle; Rotator cuff: the SITS muscles
Why this is correct:
Subscapularis arises from the subscapular fossa on the anterior surface of the scapula and attaches to the lesser tubercle, which is the prominence on the superior anterior portion of the humerus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The surgical neck is a fracture site rather than a muscle attachment for the cuff.
C. The greater tubercle receives supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and teres minor.
D. The medial lip of the intertubercular groove receives teres major.
E. The deltoid tuberosity is the attachment for deltoid.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2110
Among the four rotator cuff muscles, one is a medial rotator while the others abduct or laterally rotate the arm. Which of the following best describes the actions of that muscle?
A. Laterally rotates and abducts the humerus
B. Abducts and medially rotates the humerus
C. Medially rotates and adducts the humerus
D. Laterally rotates and adducts the humerus
E. Extends and medially rotates the humerus
Answer: C. Medially rotates and adducts the humerus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Subscapularis as a rotator cuff muscle; Functions of the rotator cuff
Why this is correct:
Subscapularis medially rotates and adducts the humerus, which sets it apart from supraspinatus (abduction) and from infraspinatus and teres minor (lateral rotation).
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Lateral rotation with abduction does not match any single cuff muscle in the lecture.
B. Abduction belongs to supraspinatus, which does not medially rotate the humerus.
D. Lateral rotation with adduction does not match the actions given for infraspinatus or teres minor.
E. Extension of the humerus belongs to the posterior deltoid and the long head of triceps.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2111
One rotator cuff muscle receives two named nerves rather than one. Which of the following pairs supplies that muscle?
A. Upper and lower subscapular nerves
B. Dorsal scapular and suprascapular nerves
C. Thoracodorsal and lower subscapular nerves

Subscapularis is supplied by both the upper and the lower subscapular nerves, which arise from the posterior cord of the brachial plexus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The dorsal scapular nerve supplies the rhomboids and levator scapulae.
C. The thoracodorsal nerve supplies latissimus dorsi, which is not a cuff muscle.
D. The suprascapular nerve supplies supraspinatus and infraspinatus, and the axillary nerve supplies teres minor, but they do not share a muscle.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2112
The glenohumeral joint has a shallow socket, a lax capsule, and only limited contact between the humeral head and the glenoid at any position. Which of the following best explains how the rotator cuff compensates for that arrangement?
A. It acts as a fibrocartilaginous rim that deepens the socket
B. It acts as a set of dynamic stabilizers that make up for the lack of static stabilizers
C. It acts as a bony arch that blocks superior displacement of the head
D. It acts as a set of static stabilizers that make up for the lack of dynamic stabilizers
E. It acts as a capsular thickening that resists anterior translation
Answer: B. It acts as a set of dynamic stabilizers that make up for the lack of static stabilizers
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Functions of the rotator cuff; Glenohumeral joint: type and articulation
Why this is correct:
The cuff muscles are regulators of dynamic stability that compensate for the lack of static stabilizers, working through isometric and isotonic muscle activity.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Deepening the socket by about 50 percent is the role of the glenoid labrum.
C. Blocking superior displacement is the role of the coracoacromial arch.
D. The relationship is the reverse, since the cuff is the dynamic element in a joint short on static support.
E. Resisting anterior translation through capsular thickening describes the glenohumeral ligaments.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2113
As the arm is raised, the rotator cuff muscles contract together and act on the position of the humeral head within the socket. Which of the following best describes that effect?
A. They distract the head from the glenoid fossa and lift it up
B. They translate the head anteriorly against the capsule
C. They compress the head into the glenoid fossa and keep it down
D. They rotate the glenoid fossa upward against the thorax
E. They tension the coracoclavicular ligament against the coracoid
Answer: C. They compress the head into the glenoid fossa and keep it down
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Functions of the rotator cuff; Rotator cuff: the SITS muscles
Why this is correct:
As controllers of arthrokinematics, the cuff muscles compress the humeral head into the glenoid fossa and keep the head down so that the joint stays centered during movement.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Distraction of the head away from the glenoid is the opposite of the cuff's stabilizing role.
B. Anterior translation of the head against the capsule is what the glenohumeral ligaments resist.
D. Upward rotation of the glenoid against the thorax is produced by trapezius and serratus anterior.
E. The coracoclavicular ligament is a stabilizer of the acromioclavicular joint and is not tensioned by the cuff.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2114
A 40-year-old swimmer has pain at the top of the shoulder when the arm is lifted overhead. Which of the following rotator cuff functions is most critical for preventing contact between the greater tubercle and the overlying bone during that movement?
A. Isometric holding of the scapula against the posterior thoracic wall
B. Elevation of the humeral head toward the coracoacromial ligament
C. Rotation of the humeral head to clear the greater tubercle from the acromion
D. Tensioning of the superior fibrous capsule against the glenoid rim
E. Compression of the humeral head into the glenoid fossa during loading
Answer: C. Rotation of the humeral head to clear the greater tubercle from the acromion
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Functions of the rotator cuff; Subacromial space and bursa
Why this is correct:
Among its arthrokinematic functions, the cuff rotates the humeral head so that the greater tubercle clears the acromion, which matters because the subacromial space is only about 1 cm and holds the supraspinatus tendon and the bursa.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Holding the scapula against the thoracic wall is the job of serratus anterior.
B. Elevation of the head toward the arch is what causes contact rather than preventing it.
D. Tensioning the superior capsule is a passive restraint rather than the clearing mechanism.
E. Compression into the glenoid centers the head but does not by itself clear the tubercle from the acromion.
Objective: Compare and contrast the rotator cuff muscles; explain their significance in stabilization of the glenohumeral joint.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2115
During a motion analysis session, a subject abducts the upper limb through its full range. Which of the following best describes the division of that motion between the glenohumeral joint and the scapulothoracic interface?
A. 150 degrees at the glenohumeral joint and 30 degrees at the scapulothoracic interface
B. 60 degrees at the glenohumeral joint and 120 degrees at the scapulothoracic interface
C. 180 degrees at the glenohumeral joint and no motion at the scapulothoracic interface
D. 90 degrees at the glenohumeral joint and 90 degrees at the scapulothoracic interface
E. 120 degrees at the glenohumeral joint and 60 degrees at the scapulothoracic interface
Answer: E. 120 degrees at the glenohumeral joint and 60 degrees at the scapulothoracic interface
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Scapulohumeral rhythm; Scapulothoracic interface
Why this is correct:
Scapulohumeral rhythm runs at a 2:1 ratio, so 120 degrees of glenohumeral motion plus 60 degrees of scapular rotation produces 180 degrees of total abduction.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. This would be a 5:1 ratio, which is not the rhythm given in the lecture.
B. This reverses the ratio and gives the scapula twice the motion of the glenohumeral joint.
C. The scapulothoracic interface contributes motion, so abduction is not purely glenohumeral.
D. An equal split would be a 1:1 ratio rather than the 2:1 rhythm described.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2116
A student examining scapulohumeral rhythm notes that the scapula does not begin to contribute at the very start of abduction. Which of the following best describes that early portion of the movement?
A. The first 30 degrees is independent of scapulohumeral rhythm
B. The first 120 degrees is independent of scapulohumeral rhythm
C. The first 60 degrees is independent of scapulohumeral rhythm
D. The first 90 degrees is independent of scapulohumeral rhythm
E. The first 150 degrees is independent of scapulohumeral rhythm
Answer: A. The first 30 degrees is independent of scapulohumeral rhythm
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Scapulohumeral rhythm
Why this is correct:
The lecture states that the first 30 degrees of flexion or abduction is independent of the scapulohumeral rhythm, after which every 2 degrees at the glenohumeral joint is matched by 1 degree of superior scapular rotation.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. One hundred twenty degrees is the glenohumeral share of full abduction.
C. Sixty degrees is the amount of scapular rotation across full abduction, not the independent portion.
D. Ninety degrees is the point past which serratus anterior is needed to raise the arm, not the independent portion.
E. One hundred fifty degrees exceeds the glenohumeral contribution entirely.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2117
A 34-year-old woman cannot raise her arm above the horizontal, and the medial border of her scapula stands away from the thoracic wall when she pushes against a wall. Which of the following muscles is most likely to be the one affected?
A. Serratus anterior
B. Levator scapulae
C. Subclavius
D. Trapezius
E. Rhomboid major
Answer: A. Serratus anterior
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Scapulohumeral rhythm; Shoulder girdle muscles and movers of the humerus
Why this is correct:
Scapulohumeral rhythm requires trapezius and serratus anterior to rotate the scapula upward, and serratus anterior is also the muscle that holds the scapula against the thoracic wall, so its loss produces both the failure to raise the arm and the protruding medial border.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Levator scapulae elevates the scapula rather than holding it against the thorax.
C. Subclavius anchors and depresses the clavicle and does not act on the scapula.
D. Trapezius contributes to upward rotation but does not hold the medial border against the thoracic wall.
E. Rhomboid major retracts the scapula rather than rotating it upward.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2118
A 47-year-old house painter comes to the clinic because of several months of shoulder pain that is worst when he works with his arms above his head. Coronal MRI shows a tear of a rotator cuff tendon. Which of the following tendons is most likely torn?
A. Subscapularis
B. Supraspinatus
C. Teres minor
D. Long head of biceps brachii
E. Infraspinatus
Answer: B. Supraspinatus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Rotator cuff injury and the deltoid force couple; Supraspinatus
Why this is correct:
Rupture or tear of the supraspinatus tendon is the most common rotator cuff injury, and the cuff is typically injured during repetitive use of the upper limb above the horizontal plane.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Subscapularis sits on the anterior scapula and is not the tendon most often torn.
C. Teres minor is the smallest cuff muscle and is not the usual site of rupture.
D. The long head of biceps runs in the subacromial space but is not a rotator cuff tendon.
E. Infraspinatus tears occur but are not the most common cuff injury described.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2119
A 61-year-old man has a complete tear of the supraspinatus tendon. On examination he cannot abduct the limb at the glenohumeral joint even though the deltoid contracts strongly. Which of the following best explains the loss of abduction?
A. The force couple between the trapezius and serratus anterior has been compromised
B. The force couple between the deltoid and the rotator cuff has been compromised
C. The capsule no longer permits distraction of the humeral head
D. The coracoacromial arch no longer blocks superior displacement of the head
E. The glenoid labrum no longer deepens the socket for the humeral head
Answer: B. The force couple between the deltoid and the rotator cuff has been compromised
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Rotator cuff injury and the deltoid force couple; Functions of the rotator cuff
Why this is correct:
With a complete tear of supraspinatus the deltoid and cuff can no longer work as a force couple, so the deltoid alone cannot abduct the limb at the glenohumeral joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The trapezius and serratus anterior couple rotates the scapula and is not what a supraspinatus tear disrupts.
C. The capsule normally permits several centimeters of distraction, so this is not a new deficit.
D. The coracoacromial arch is a bony and ligamentous structure that a tendon tear does not remove.
E. The labrum is unaffected by a cuff tendon tear.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2120
A 55-year-old warehouse worker has chronic cuff disease, and imaging shows the humeral head riding high in the socket with the joint no longer tracking normally. Which of the following best describes the state of the glenohumeral joint in cuff injury?
A. It becomes unstable because the cuff normally provides dynamic stability
B. It becomes subluxed because the coracoclavicular ligament normally suspends it
C. It becomes ankylosed because the cuff normally provides passive tension
D. It becomes stiff because the capsule normally permits distraction
E. It becomes hypermobile because the labrum normally limits translation
Answer: A. It becomes unstable because the cuff normally provides dynamic stability
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Rotator cuff injury and the deltoid force couple; Functions of the rotator cuff
Why this is correct:
Injury or disease that damages the rotator cuff produces instability of the glenohumeral joint, because the cuff is the dynamic stabilizer that keeps the head seated and down in the shallow glenoid.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The coracoclavicular ligament suspends the scapula from the clavicle at the acromioclavicular joint.
C. Cuff damage does not fuse the joint, and the cuff is a dynamic rather than a purely passive restraint.
D. Stiffness from a thickened, tightened capsule describes adhesive capsulitis rather than cuff injury.
E. The labrum deepens the socket but is not what fails in cuff injury.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2121
During dissection of the posterior arm, the three heads of a large extensor muscle are separated. One head is traced proximally past the glenohumeral joint to a small projection on the scapula. Which of the following is the proximal attachment of that head?
A. Inferior angle of the scapula

Reference: Triceps brachii and anconeus; Scapula: landmarks of the shoulder

The long head of triceps brachii arises from the infraglenoid tubercle, the small projection inferior to the glenoid cavity, which is why it is the only head that crosses the shoulder joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:

Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2122
In the posterior arm, two heads of triceps brachii arise from the posterior surface of the humerus and are separated from one another by an oblique bony channel. Which of the following structures lies in that channel?
A. The median nerve and the brachial artery
B. The ulnar nerve and the superior ulnar collateral artery
C. The suprascapular nerve and the suprascapular artery
D. The radial nerve and the deep brachial artery
E. The axillary nerve and the posterior circumflex humeral artery

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Triceps brachii and anconeus; Radial nerve in the radial groove; Deep brachial artery in the posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The lateral head of triceps arises superior to the radial groove and the medial head inferior to it, and the radial nerve runs with the deep brachial artery in that groove between the two heads.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The median nerve and brachial artery stay in the anterior compartment.
B. The ulnar nerve and superior ulnar collateral artery pass posterior to the medial epicondyle.
C. The suprascapular nerve and artery are at the suprascapular notch on the scapula.
E. The axillary nerve and posterior circumflex humeral artery pass through the quadrangular space, not the radial groove.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2123
During dissection of the posterior elbow, a small muscle is traced from the lateral epicondyle of the humerus to the lateral side of the olecranon. Which of the following best describes the function and innervation of that muscle?
A. It extends the shoulder joint and is supplied by the axillary nerve
B. It pronates the forearm and is supplied by the median nerve
C. It stabilizes the elbow during flexion and is supplied by the ulnar nerve

B. Pronation is a forearm action and is not attributed to anconeus in this lecture.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2124
At the superior border of the scapula, a ligament bridges a notch, and a nerve and an artery cross that region in different planes. Which of the following best describes their relationship to the ligament?
A. The artery passes deep to the ligament and the nerve pierces it
B. The nerve passes superior to the ligament and the artery passes deep to it
C. Both the artery and the nerve pass superior to the ligament
D. Both the artery and the nerve pass deep to the ligament

Why the other answers are wrong:

B. This reverses the relationship given by the mnemonic.
C. The nerve passes beneath the ligament rather than over it.
D. The artery passes above the ligament rather than beneath it.

Question 2128
A 26-year-old man is examined after a shoulder injury. He has weakness of abduction and lateral rotation together with an area of numbness over the lateral aspect of the shoulder. Which of the following nerves is most likely injured?
A. Axillary nerve
B. Upper subscapular nerve

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2129
During a dissection, a cord of the brachial plexus is traced in the axilla and found to give off the upper subscapular, thoracodorsal, lower subscapular, axillary, and radial nerves. Which of the following cords is most likely being traced?
A. Inferior trunk
B. Superior trunk
C. Posterior cord
D. Medial cord
E. Lateral cord
Answer: C. Posterior cord
Order: First
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Axillary and subscapular nerves at the shoulder; Radial nerve in the radial groove
Why this is correct:
The posterior cord gives the upper subscapular, thoracodorsal, and lower subscapular nerves and then continues as the axillary and radial nerves.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The inferior trunk continues as the anterior division that becomes the medial cord.
B. The superior trunk gives the suprascapular nerve and the nerve to subclavius.
D. The medial cord gives the medial pectoral nerve, the two medial cutaneous nerves, the medial root of the median nerve, and the ulnar nerve.
E. The lateral cord gives the lateral pectoral nerve, the lateral root of the median nerve, and the musculocutaneous nerve.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2136
During dissection of the root of the neck, an artery is traced from the subclavian artery and followed posteriorly toward the scapula alongside a nerve, passing above a ligament at the superior border of the scapula. Which of the following arteries is most likely being traced?
A. Circumflex scapular artery
B. Posterior circumflex humeral artery
C. Thoracoacromial artery
D. Suprascapular artery
E. Subscapular artery
Answer: D. Suprascapular artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Suprascapular, dorsal scapular, and thoracoacromial arteries; Suprascapular nerve and the superior transverse scapular ligament
Why this is correct:
The suprascapular artery branches from the subclavian artery or its thyrocervical trunk, passes posteriorly with the suprascapular nerve, and runs superior to the superior transverse scapular ligament to supply the posterior shoulder.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The circumflex scapular artery arises from the subscapular artery and curves around the lateral border of the scapula.
B. The posterior circumflex humeral artery branches from the third part of the axillary artery and runs in the quadrangular space.
C. The thoracoacromial artery branches from the second part of the axillary artery.
E. The subscapular artery arises from the third part of the axillary artery in the axilla.
Objective: Describe the blood supply to the shoulder region and posterior arm, and discuss examples of collateral blood supply and arterial anastomoses around the shoulder joint and scapular region.

Question 2143
A 48-year-old man undergoes ligation of the axillary artery between the first rib and the lower border of pectoralis minor after a penetrating injury. Blood flow to the arm is preserved. Which of the following pairs of vessels most likely carried that flow?
A. Superior and inferior ulnar collateral arteries

Why the other answers are wrong:

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2144
Two patients have occlusion of the same artery supplying the upper limb. One developed the occlusion gradually over years and has no symptoms, while the other developed it suddenly and has an ischemic limb. Which of the following best explains the difference?
A. Slow occlusion shifts flow into the superficial veins of the limb
B. Slow occlusion allows time for collateral circulation to develop
C. Slow occlusion prevents anastomotic channels from forming
D. Sudden occlusion widens the existing anastomotic channels
E. Sudden occlusion redirects flow through the deep brachial artery
Answer: B. Slow occlusion allows time for collateral circulation to develop
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Scapular arterial anastomoses and collateral circulation
Why this is correct:
Slow occlusion of an artery often allows sufficient collateral circulation to develop and prevents ischemia, while sudden occlusion does not allow enough time, so ischemia of the upper limb results.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Veins drain the limb and cannot carry arterial supply to it.
C. Slow occlusion encourages rather than prevents collateral development.
D. Sudden occlusion gives no time for channels to enlarge, which is why ischemia occurs.
E. The deep brachial artery supplies the posterior arm and does not substitute for a proximal occlusion in this way.
Objective: Describe the blood supply to the shoulder region and posterior arm, and discuss examples of collateral blood supply and arterial anastomoses around the shoulder joint and scapular region.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2150
A 24-year-old volleyball player has weakness of shoulder abduction with numbness over the lateral shoulder after a blow to the posterior shoulder. Imaging suggests compression of neurovascular structures at a gap in the posterior scapular region. Which of the following spaces is most likely involved?
A. Triangular interval
B. Triangular space
C. Subacromial space
D. Quadrangular space
E. Suprascapular notch
Answer: D. Quadrangular space
Order: Third
Difficulty: Hard
Reference: Quadrangular space; Axillary and subscapular nerves at the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The quadrangular space transmits the axillary nerve and the posterior circumflex humeral artery, and the axillary nerve supplies deltoid, teres minor, and the skin over the deltoid, which matches weak abduction with lateral shoulder numbness.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The triangular interval transmits the radial nerve and deep brachial artery, so a lesion there would weaken elbow extension.
B. The triangular space transmits the circumflex scapular artery, which carries no motor or sensory fibers.
C. The subacromial space holds the supraspinatus tendon and bursa, and compression there causes pain rather than numbness.
E. Compression at the suprascapular notch would weaken abduction and lateral rotation without any sensory loss.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

Question 2151
During dissection of the posterior shoulder, a three sided gap is opened between the long head of triceps brachii, teres major, and teres minor. Which of the following structures is found passing through it?

B. Suprascapular artery
C. Posterior circumflex humeral artery
D. Circumflex scapular artery
E. Deep brachial artery
Answer: D. Circumflex scapular artery

Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Triangular space and triangular interval; Subscapular and circumflex humeral arteries at the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The triangular space is bordered by the long head of triceps brachii, teres major, and teres minor, and the circumflex scapular artery passes through it on its way around the lateral border of the scapula.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The thoracodorsal artery descends to latissimus dorsi without passing through this gap.
B. The suprascapular artery crosses above the superior transverse scapular ligament.
C. The posterior circumflex humeral artery passes through the quadrangular space with the axillary nerve.
E. The deep brachial artery passes through the triangular interval with the radial nerve.
Objective: Describe the blood supply to the shoulder region and posterior arm, and discuss examples of collateral blood supply and arterial anastomoses around the shoulder joint and scapular region.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2152
A gap in the posterior shoulder region is bounded by the long head of triceps brachii, teres major, and the shaft of the humerus. Which of the following structures passes through it?
A. Circumflex scapular artery alone
B. Suprascapular nerve and suprascapular artery
C. Axillary nerve and posterior circumflex humeral artery
D. Radial nerve and deep brachial artery
E. Ulnar nerve and superior ulnar collateral artery
Answer: D. Radial nerve and deep brachial artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Triangular space and triangular interval; Deep brachial artery in the posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The triangular interval is bordered by the long head of triceps brachii, teres major, and the humerus, and it transmits the deep brachial artery and the radial nerve into the posterior compartment of the arm.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The circumflex scapular artery passes through the triangular space.
B. The suprascapular nerve and artery cross at the suprascapular notch on the scapula.
C. The axillary nerve and posterior circumflex humeral artery pass through the quadrangular space.
E. The ulnar nerve and superior ulnar collateral artery run toward the medial epicondyle.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2153
Two three sided gaps of the posterior shoulder share the long head of triceps brachii and teres major as borders, and they are told apart by their third border. Which of the following correctly identifies that third border for the triangular interval?
A. The acromion
B. Teres minor
C. The lateral head of triceps brachii
D. The lateral border of the scapula
E. The humerus
Answer: E. The humerus
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Triangular space and triangular interval; Quadrangular space
Why this is correct:
The triangular space is completed by teres minor, while the triangular interval is completed by the humerus, and that difference is what separates the two gaps and their contents.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The acromion is part of the coracoacromial arch above the joint.
B. Teres minor completes the triangular space rather than the triangular interval.
C. The lateral head of triceps is not listed as a border of these gaps.
D. The lateral border of the scapula is where the circumflex scapular artery runs, not a listed border of either gap.
Objective: Describe the attachments, innervation, blood supply, primary functions of the muscles of the shoulder and posterior arm. Predict the clinical consequences of injury to these muscles or their innervation.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2154
A 29-year-old man is brought to the emergency department after a metal bar struck the middle of his upper arm. Radiographs show a transverse fracture through the humeral shaft. Which of the following structures is most likely injured?
A. Musculocutaneous nerve
B. Axillary nerve
C. Radial nerve
D. Ulnar nerve
E. Suprascapular nerve
Answer: C. Radial nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Humeral shaft fracture: structures at risk; Radial nerve in the radial groove
Why this is correct:
The radial nerve runs in the radial groove on the posterior surface of the humeral shaft, so a transverse shaft fracture, which is usually caused by a direct blow to the arm, puts it at risk.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The musculocutaneous nerve runs in the anterior compartment between biceps and brachialis.
B. The axillary nerve is at risk at the surgical neck rather than the midshaft.
D. The ulnar nerve is at risk behind the medial epicondyle, distal to the shaft.
E. The suprascapular nerve is confined to the scapular region.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2155
A transverse fracture of the humeral shaft is seen on radiograph. Along with the nerve in the radial groove, one vessel is at risk at the same site. Which of the following vessels is most likely injured?
A. Thoracodorsal artery
B. Posterior circumflex humeral artery
C. Deep brachial artery
D. Suprascapular artery
E. Circumflex scapular artery
Answer: C. Deep brachial artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Humeral shaft fracture: structures at risk; Deep brachial artery in the posterior arm
Why this is correct:
The deep brachial artery runs with the radial nerve in the radial groove along the posterior humeral shaft, so both are at risk in a transverse shaft fracture.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The thoracodorsal artery descends in the axilla to latissimus dorsi.
B. The posterior circumflex humeral artery lies at the surgical neck, which is proximal to the shaft.
D. The suprascapular artery supplies the posterior shoulder at the scapular notch.
E. The circumflex scapular artery runs around the lateral border of the scapula.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2156
A 36-year-old man has a midshaft humeral fracture after a direct blow to the arm. Which of the following deficits is most likely to be found on examination?
A. Weakness of medial rotation of the arm at the shoulder
B. Weakness of abduction of the arm at the shoulder
C. Weakness of flexion of the forearm at the elbow
D. Weakness of extension of the forearm at the elbow
E. Weakness of protraction of the scapula against the thoracic wall
Answer: D. Weakness of extension of the forearm at the elbow
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Humeral shaft fracture: structures at risk; Triceps brachii and anconeus
Why this is correct:
A midshaft fracture endangers the radial nerve in the radial groove, and the radial nerve supplies triceps brachii, which extends the forearm at the elbow.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Medial rotation is produced by subscapularis and teres major, supplied by the subscapular nerves.
B. Abduction weakness points to supraspinatus or deltoid, supplied by the suprascapular and axillary nerves.
C. Flexion of the forearm is produced by the anterior arm muscles supplied by the musculocutaneous nerve.
E. Protraction of the scapula is produced by serratus anterior, supplied by the long thoracic nerve.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2157
An 81-year-old woman with a long history of osteoporosis falls at home and is brought to the emergency department. Radiographs show a fracture through the narrow portion of the humerus just distal to the tubercles. Which of the following nerves is most likely injured?
A. Radial nerve
B. Musculocutaneous nerve
C. Median nerve
D. Axillary nerve
E. Suprascapular nerve
Answer: D. Axillary nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Easy
Reference: Surgical neck fracture: structures at risk; Quadrangular space
Why this is correct:
The surgical neck is the narrow portion just distal to the tubercles and is a common fracture site in elderly patients with osteoporosis. The axillary nerve passes through the quadrangular space against the surgical neck and is at risk there.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The radial nerve is at risk in the radial groove along the shaft, further distal.
B. The musculocutaneous nerve runs in the anterior compartment of the arm.
C. The median nerve travels with the brachial artery in the anterior compartment.
E. The suprascapular nerve stays within the scapular region.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2158
A fracture of the surgical neck of the humerus is confirmed on radiograph. In addition to a nerve, one artery lies directly against the bone at that level. Which of the following vessels is most likely injured?
A. Circumflex scapular artery
B. Posterior circumflex humeral artery
C. Deep brachial artery
D. Dorsal scapular artery
E. Suprascapular artery
Answer: B. Posterior circumflex humeral artery
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Surgical neck fracture: structures at risk; Subscapular and circumflex humeral arteries at the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The posterior circumflex humeral artery runs with the axillary nerve through the quadrangular space, whose lateral border is the surgical neck, so both are at risk in a surgical neck fracture.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The circumflex scapular artery curves around the lateral border of the scapula.
C. The deep brachial artery accompanies the radial nerve along the shaft in the radial groove.
D. The dorsal scapular artery runs along the medial scapular region.
E. The suprascapular artery runs above the superior transverse scapular ligament at the scapula.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2159
Six months after a proximal humeral fracture, a 76-year-old woman has a flattened shoulder contour with a slight hollow below the acromion. Which of the following best explains that appearance?
A. Atrophy of the deltoid following damage to its nerve
B. Atrophy of triceps brachii following damage to its nerve
C. Atrophy of teres major following damage to its nerve
D. Atrophy of subscapularis following damage to its nerve
E. Atrophy of supraspinatus following damage to its nerve
Answer: A. Atrophy of the deltoid following damage to its nerve
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Surgical neck fracture: structures at risk; Deltoid
Why this is correct:
When the axillary nerve is severely damaged, the deltoid atrophies, the rounded contour of the shoulder disappears, and a slight hollow appears inferior to the acromion, often with sensory loss over the deltoid.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. Triceps brachii atrophy would affect the posterior arm rather than the shoulder contour.
C. Teres major atrophy would not change the rounded contour of the shoulder.
D. Subscapularis lies on the anterior surface of the scapula and its wasting is not visible as a shoulder contour change.
E. Supraspinatus atrophy would hollow the supraspinous fossa rather than flatten the lateral shoulder.
Objective: Identify the bony features of the clavicle, scapula, and humerus. Discuss common sites of fractures, predict what structures may be at risk, and discuss what sensory &/or motor deficits may occur.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2160
During a shoulder arthroscopy, the surgeon notes that sensation from inside the glenohumeral joint capsule is not carried by a single nerve. The lecture lists the nerves that convey pain and proprioception from this joint, following Hilton's Law. Which of the following nerves appears on that list?
A. Dorsal scapular nerve
B. Nerve to subclavius
C. Suprascapular nerve
D. Long thoracic nerve
E. Medial cutaneous nerve of the arm
Answer: C. Suprascapular nerve
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Hilton's Law and innervation of the glenohumeral joint; Suprascapular nerve and the superior transverse scapular ligament
Why this is correct:
The lecture lists the innervation of the glenohumeral joint as the suprascapular, axillary, lateral and medial pectoral, musculocutaneous, upper and lower subscapular, thoracodorsal, and radial nerves. The suprascapular nerve is named separately as supplying supraspinatus, infraspinatus, and the glenohumeral joint.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The dorsal scapular nerve supplies the rhomboids and levator scapulae and isn't on the lecture's list for this joint.
B. The nerve to subclavius supplies subclavius and isn't on the lecture's list for this joint.
D. The long thoracic nerve supplies serratus anterior and isn't on the lecture's list for this joint.
E. The medial cutaneous nerve of the arm is sensory to the skin of the medial arm and isn't on the lecture's list for this joint.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2161
A surgeon planning a glenohumeral joint procedure reviews the nerves that convey pain and proprioception from the capsule. Which of the following nerves is among those listed for this joint?
A. Suprascapular nerve
B. Long thoracic nerve
C. Dorsal scapular nerve
D. Nerve to subclavius
E. Medial cutaneous nerve of the forearm
Answer: A. Suprascapular nerve
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Hilton's Law and innervation of the glenohumeral joint; Suprascapular nerve and the superior transverse scapular ligament
Why this is correct:
The glenohumeral joint is supplied by the suprascapular, axillary, lateral and medial pectoral, musculocutaneous, upper and lower subscapular, thoracodorsal, and radial nerves, and the suprascapular nerve also supplies supraspinatus and infraspinatus.
Why the other answers are wrong:
B. The long thoracic nerve supplies serratus anterior and is not listed for this joint.
C. The dorsal scapular nerve supplies the rhomboids and levator scapulae.
D. The nerve to subclavius supplies the subclavius muscle at the clavicle.
E. The medial cutaneous nerve of the forearm is sensory to the medial forearm.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2162
During a review of the glenohumeral joint, the vessels supplying the joint itself are listed. Which of the following sets of arteries supplies this joint?
A. Superior thoracic, lateral thoracic, and internal thoracic arteries
B. Superior ulnar collateral, inferior ulnar collateral, and radial recurrent arteries
C. Deep brachial, radial collateral, and middle collateral arteries
D. Thoracodorsal, dorsal scapular, and lateral thoracic arteries
E. Anterior circumflex humeral, posterior circumflex humeral, and suprascapular arteries
Answer: E. Anterior circumflex humeral, posterior circumflex humeral, and suprascapular arteries
Order: First
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Hilton's Law and innervation of the glenohumeral joint; Subscapular and circumflex humeral arteries at the shoulder
Why this is correct:
The blood supply of the glenohumeral joint is given as the anterior and posterior circumflex humeral arteries together with the suprascapular artery.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. These arteries supply the thoracic wall and the breast.
B. These arteries belong to the anastomosis around the elbow.
C. These arteries supply the posterior compartment of the arm.
D. These arteries supply latissimus dorsi, the medial scapular region, and the lateral thoracic wall.
Objective: Describe the anatomical characteristics (osteology, ligaments, tendons, and capsule) of the sternoclavicular, acromioclavicular and glenohumeral joints. Discuss the functions of the joints, and distinguish the nerves that convey joint pain and proprioception following Hilton's Law.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2163
A 57-year-old woman with diabetes comes to the clinic because of several months of shoulder pain and progressive stiffness, and she now has difficulty abducting the arm. Passive and active range of motion are both reduced. Which of the following best explains her findings?
A. Fibrocartilaginous detachment of the glenoid labrum from the glenoid rim
B. Anterior displacement of the humeral head through a weak anteroinferior capsule
C. A tear of the supraspinatus tendon that disrupts the deltoid force couple
D. Adhesive fibrosis and scarring that thicken and tighten the joint capsule
E. Rupture of the coracoclavicular ligament with superior displacement of the clavicle
Answer: D. Adhesive fibrosis and scarring that thicken and tighten the joint capsule
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Adhesive capsulitis; Fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint
Why this is correct:
Adhesive capsulitis, or frozen shoulder, occurs when adhesive fibrosis or scarring thickens and tightens the glenohumeral joint capsule, producing pain, stiffness, and difficulty abducting the arm. Diabetes is one of its risk factors.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Labral detachment is not the mechanism described for frozen shoulder in this lecture.
B. Anterior displacement of the head describes glenohumeral dislocation, which is an acute event.
C. A cuff tear limits active abduction but does not stiffen the joint in both active and passive motion.
E. Coracoclavicular rupture is an acromioclavicular dislocation presenting after trauma to the tip of the shoulder.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2164
A 63-year-old man develops a painful, stiff shoulder after spending several weeks in a sling following an unrelated injury. Which of the following is most likely to have contributed to his condition?
A. Sudden traction pulling the upper limb superiorly
B. A fall directly onto the tip of the shoulder
C. A medical condition that prevented him from moving the arm
D. A fall onto an outstretched hand transmitting force to the clavicle
E. Repetitive use of the limb above the horizontal plane
Answer: C. A medical condition that prevented him from moving the arm
Order: Second
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Adhesive capsulitis
Why this is correct:
Risk factors for adhesive capsulitis include medical conditions that prevent moving the arm, injuries to the glenohumeral joint region, and diabetes, and weeks of immobilization in a sling fits the first of those.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. Sudden superior traction on the limb is a mechanism for lower brachial plexus injury.
B. A fall onto the tip of the shoulder produces acromioclavicular dislocation.
D. A fall on an outstretched hand transmitting force to the clavicle produces a clavicular fracture.
E. Repetitive overhead use is the mechanism given for rotator cuff injury.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.

MSK / Week 14 / Shoulder and Posterior Arm
Question 2165
A 58-year-old woman with diabetes comes to the clinic because of several months of shoulder pain, stiffness, and difficulty abducting the arm. She reports no injury and no work or sport that takes her arm above shoulder height. Which of the following structures is most likely at fault?
A. The subacromial bursa
B. The glenohumeral joint capsule
C. The glenoid labrum
D. The supraspinatus tendon
E. The coracoacromial ligament
Answer: B. The glenohumeral joint capsule
Order: Third
Difficulty: Medium
Reference: Adhesive capsulitis; Fibrous capsule of the glenohumeral joint
Why this is correct:
Adhesive capsulitis is caused when adhesive fibrosis and scarring thicken and tighten the glenohumeral joint capsule, and the lecture gives its presentation as pain, stiffness, and difficulty abducting the arm, with diabetes among the risk factors.
Why the other answers are wrong:
A. The subacromial bursa reduces friction in the subacromial space, and the lecture doesn't name it as the cause of this presentation.
C. The glenoid labrum deepens the socket by about 50 percent, and the lecture doesn't list stiffness as a consequence of labral injury.
D. A supraspinatus tear follows repetitive use of the upper limb above the horizontal plane, which this history rules out.
E. The coracoacromial ligament is part of the arch that prevents superior displacement of the humeral head, not a structure the lecture ties to this presentation.
Objective: Discuss the clinical presentation and anatomical basis for the following: rotator cuff injuries, dislocation of the sternoclavicular joint, dislocation of the acromioclavicular joint (shoulder separation), dislocation of the glenohumeral joint (shoulder dislocation), and shoulder impingement/instability.
